=claude-org-setup --glue= renders =claude-code-ide-org-glue.el= into =$DOOMDIR= (=7c86ab4c=). It was generated because it first had to carry an explicit project list, which varies by machine. The list became derived at wire time the same day, and =render_glue= now writes fixed text, verified 2026-09-23. Still, a changed glue reaches a user only when they re-run =--glue=, and until then =--check= reports drift. That drift is what made =claude-org-setup-test= depend on the real =~/.config/doom= (=94e6b615=). The proposal is to ship the glue as a real file in the repo and have =--glue= link it into =$DOOMDIR=, which makes drift impossible. =--check= would then ask only whether the link resolves to this checkout. It changes =1caed585='s install contract ("generated, marker-headed, drift-checked files"), so it needs the user's decision. The Doom module is already loaded from the checkout, so tying Doom to the checkout's path adds no new dependency.

- [2026-09-24 Thu 07:35]--[2026-09-24 Thu 07:39] continue the live-follow brainstorm

:PLAN:
*The design, approved section by section 2026-09-24 (the user), brainstormed on the architectural path.*

*Scope decisions* (the user, 2026-09-23 and 2026-09-24):
- A render follows its transcript only while its buffer is open, not in the background for every live session.
- It updates a whole turn at a time, not per paragraph or on a poll.
- The render file is extended by appending, not re-rendered and reverted, and not written into the buffer directly. A full re-render would reset folds on every turn and costs more as the session grows. A buffer written directly would become a second source of truth beside the file.

*Turn boundaries, measured 2026-09-23 on one transcript.* A finished turn ends at its =system= / =turn_duration= line (41 of them, one per completed turn, written after the Stop hooks settle). =stop_reason: end_turn= is not a boundary: it appeared 79 times, because a blocking Stop hook makes a turn continue and end again. A turn cut short by an interrupt may never get a =turn_duration=, so the next prompt closes it.

**1. Opening, state and trigger**

Opening a render interactively, or with =T= from the review buffer, always writes a full render, forced, and that render *stops at the last finished turn*. An unfinished turn is left out, because otherwise it would be rendered twice once it finishes. The render also yields the transcript byte offset just past that turn's boundary. That offset and the transcript's path are buffer-local, since following exists only while the buffer does. Reopening starts from a fresh full render.

The trigger is =file-notify-add-watch= on the transcript, added when following starts and removed when it stops. A change event, debounced by half a second, reads the transcript from the saved offset. It acts only when the new bytes hold a =turn_duration= line, or a new prompt that closes an interrupted turn, and it ignores a partial last line until its newline arrives. If the file cannot be watched, the buffer polls every 5 seconds instead and says so once in the echo area.

**2. The renderer**

=claude-code-ide-org--render-transcript= splits into a header function (the title, =#+STARTUP=, and the re-key note of =b57c7515=) and a function that renders one turn. A full render is the header plus every finished turn; an append is only the new finished turns. So both paths share one turn renderer and cannot disagree about what a turn looks like.

A new reader parses the transcript from a byte offset and returns the turns finished since then, plus the new offset.

The re-key note stays at the top, written only by the full render. While following, re-keying is not detected: a re-keyed transcript simply stops growing, and the note appears when the render is next opened. That is simpler than moving the note to the end, and the cost is a render that goes quiet until reopened.

There is one writer while a render is live. Calling =claude-code-ide-org-render-session= for such a session switches to its buffer and writes nothing, because a rewrite under =auto-revert-tail-mode= would confuse its idea of where the file ends.

If the transcript shrinks below the saved offset, or its first line changes, the next update does a full render and reverts the buffer. The reader's place is lost in that rare case, and the echo area says why.

**3. The buffer**

A new minor mode, =claude-code-ide-org-render-live-mode=, with the lighter =Live=, owns the watch. It is the module's first minor mode, and it is deliberately not called =Follow=, which would read as Emacs's built-in =follow-mode=. It turns on when a render is opened interactively, and =M-x= toggles it, for instance to freeze a render while reading. When following stops on its own (the transcript is gone, the watch fails, or a rebuild happens), the mode turns itself off and the echo area says why, so the mode line is never wrong. Killing the buffer removes the watch.

The render buffer uses =auto-revert-tail-mode= and is read-only: edits to a render are lost anyway, and tail mode will not append to a modified buffer. Tail mode inserts only at the end and leaves point alone. At the very end of the buffer, point is expected to follow new text, but that is to be confirmed in testing, not assumed.

A buffer-local change hook folds any =:TOOLS:= drawer inside newly appended text. Drawers the reader opened further up stay open, because only the appended region is touched; a whole-buffer fold would close them.

**4. Testing**

ERT, against scratch transcripts in a temporary =projects/= tree:
- The offset reader returns only finished turns: =turn_duration= finishes a turn, a new prompt finishes an interrupted one, and a trailing partial line is left alone.
- The initial render stops at the last finished turn, and appending the rest later yields a file byte-identical to one full render of the finished transcript. This is the central property: append-only must never differ from a full render.
- A shrunk transcript, or a changed first line, leads to a full render.
- Calling =render-session= on a live session writes nothing.
- An appended =:TOOLS:= drawer arrives folded, and a drawer opened earlier stays open.
- The watch is added and removed with the mode, and the mode turns itself off when following stops.

Each test is shown to fail against HEAD's version. Then a live check after a reload: open this session's render, leave point mid-buffer inside an opened drawer, finish a turn, and confirm the new turn appears with point and the drawer undisturbed, and that point at the end follows. Time one append against a full render of the same transcript to confirm the cost argument.

Files: =config.el= (the renderer split, the offset reader, the minor mode, the command's interactive path) and =config-test.el=.
:END:

=claude-code-ide-org-render-session= renders only when it is called, so a render of a running session goes stale from its next turn onward. Designed 2026-09-24 on the architectural path: while a render is open, a new minor mode (=Live=) watches the transcript and appends each finished turn to the render file, and =auto-revert-tail-mode= brings it into a read-only buffer without disturbing the reader's place or folds.

*Established:*
- The renderer is nearly append-shaped: a header once, then turns in order (read 2026-09-23). The two exceptions that remain, the re-key note at the top and the lack of saved state, are handled in the plan.
- A finished turn ends at its =turn_duration= line. =end_turn= is not a boundary, because a blocking Stop hook makes a turn end twice (measured 2026-09-23).
- Renders live in =~/.claude/org-renders/= (2026-09-24).

*Not established:* whether Claude Code ever rewrites a transcript's earlier lines. The plan's rebuild rule guards that assumption.

:PLAN:
*The design, approved 2026-09-23 (the user), brainstormed on the bounded path.*

*Candidates: every project, newest first, each line labelled* (the user, 2026-09-23). The list is every =~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl= by modified time, not just the current project's. Deriving the current project's slug is what =claude-code-ide-org--transcript-file='s docstring warns goes wrong in worktrees and subdirectories; this way a session started in a worktree (=…--claude-worktrees-pr8-review=) or in another repo stays reachable. There were 96 transcripts across 5 project directories and 185 MB on 2026-09-23. Transcripts are pruned at about 30 days (=cleanupPeriodDays=, unset here).

*Renders whose transcript is gone.* The list also offers any render in =claude-code-ide-org-transcript-render-directory= whose transcript has been pruned, marked =(render only)= and opened as it is. That directory defaults to =~/.claude/org-renders/=, *inside* =~/.claude=, and whether Claude Code's cleanup prunes a directory it did not create has not been established; the oldest render was 13 days old on 2026-09-24. Settle this before building, by checking Claude Code's cleanup behaviour or by watching a render survive past 30 days. If renders are pruned, either move the default out of =~/.claude= or drop the render-only entries. They cost nothing if renders survive, so they stay in the design until then.

*Each line* shows the modified time, the project (the directory slug, shortened), the session's name and its 8-character id. The name is the *last* =custom-title= in the file, since =/rename= appends a new one each time (56 in one transcript, the first of them stale), else =ai-title=, else the first prompt's synopsis via =--prompt-synopsis=.

*Cost.* Titles are read from a window at the end of each file, which grows when the window holds none, as =--transcript-first-stamp= already grows from the start. Whole files are never read.

*Completion* keeps the list in this order (a completion table with =display-sort-function= set to =identity=) and matches on any part of the line: project, name or id. A typed full id or 8-character prefix is also accepted, resolved the way every other tool resolves ids. Only the interactive form changes: callers passing an id, including the review buffer's =T=, are unaffected.

*Testing*, in =config-test.el= against a scratch =projects/= tree: newest-first order; the last =custom-title= winning over earlier ones and over =ai-title=; the prompt-synopsis fallback; a render-only entry; a prefix resolving; the title read staying within its window. Each is shown to fail against HEAD's version. Then one live run of the picker.

Files: =config.el= (=claude-code-ide-org-render-session='s interactive form and a new candidates function) and =config-test.el=.
:END:

=M-x claude-code-ide-org-render-session= prompts with a bare =read-string "Session id: "=, so a reader needs a UUID before they can read anything. The user hit this on their first try of =96ddf1ef='s renderer (2026-09-23). Designed that day on the bounded path: a picker over every project's transcripts, newest first, each line labelled with its project and session name, plus renders whose transcript has been pruned.

*Established (2026-09-23):* =/rename= appends a =custom-title= line each time, so the last one is the current name. A session never renamed carries an =ai-title=. Transcripts are pruned at about 30 days, and the oldest on disk was 29 days old.

That heading fixes one docstring. This one is about the whole class:
=bin/check-conventions= checks documents against the code, but nothing
checks a docstring against the function it documents. A docstring is
loaded into a reader's attention the way CLAUDE.md is, so a stale one
outranks the code until someone checks. =60ed5b96='s docstring misled
two readings in one day, in opposite directions. Whether any mechanical
check can catch this, or only review can, is open.

Claude Code 2.1.280 lists 33 hook events in its binary's event enum; this repo wires 7 (settings.json and hooks/hooks.json together), and PostToolUseFailure and SessionEnd were found only because a spike went looking (2026-09-23). Proposal: a SessionStart check compares the running version (the path in CLAUDE_CODE_EXECPATH, or claude --version) against a stamp; on a change it diffs the enum against a committed snapshot and reports added and removed events, asking whether to open a spike. The extraction reads a minified binary, so it must fail loudly: an unreadable list is reported as such, never as "no change". Unclassified: the brainstorming skill decides spike or bounded.

*Candidates among the 26 unwired events* (2.1.280, 2026-09-23). All are
judged from the name alone except the two already measured.
- =PostToolUseFailure=: measured; now 3ad389be's failure hook.
- =SessionEnd=: measured; now 3485b0d0's clock close.
- =PreCompact= / =PostCompact=: =conventions-inject= fires once per
  session, so after a compaction the pointer may be gone while its sentinel
  holds.
- =InstructionsLoaded=: could show whether the path-scoped rules actually
  loaded, the blind spot 436e0991 works around.
- =StopFailure=: if a turn ending in an API error fires no =Stop=, it
  leaves no =pause= guidepost. Time tracking only.
- =SubagentStart= / =SubagentStop=: subagent lanes in the queue. Time
  tracking only.
- =ConfigChange=: might see the =time_tracking= option flip, which
  =claude-org-setup= cannot.
- =Setup=: might run =claude-org-setup= at install.
- =FileChanged=: might give =apply-detect= a direct signal in place of
  comparing mtimes.
Judged unrelated: =Notification=, =MessageDisplay=, =Elicitation=,
=ElicitationResult=, =TaskCreated=, =TaskCompleted=, =TeammateIdle=,
=PreModelSwitch=, =PostModelSwitch=, =PostToolBatch=,
=UserPromptExpansion=, =CwdChanged=, =DirectoryAdded=, =WorktreeCreate=,
=WorktreeRemove=.


* TODO A refused Emacs write falls through to an edit behind Emacs's back, where it should back off, retry, then stop and ask :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ec1e6839-bc47-4d5b-9d1e-b35b4b4e4070
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 20:20]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:BLOCKER:  c35087d8-19fe-4179-a61c-cb615b2e7679
:END:
:PLAN:
*A transient refusal prompts the human to save, and the agent waits on
the save* (the user, 2026-09-23). The prompt turns the back-off from a
guess about when the buffer will clear into a wait for something the
human was asked to do.

*The prompt appears in Emacs, raised by the tool that refused.* The
refusal runs inside Emacs, and that is where the human and the unsaved
buffer are, so the tool raises it there, naming the file and the waiting
session. A line in the agent's own reply is not enough, because the human
may not be watching the terminal while they edit, and the edit is the
reason for the refusal.

*The agent then waits, and never falls through.* It polls
`buffer-modified-p` on the Fibonacci schedule (1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8 s,
capped) and retries the refused call the moment the buffer is saved. If
the cap passes with the buffer still modified, it ends its turn saying
what it is waiting for. A save the human declines is theirs to make.

*Only the transient case gets a prompt.* A save clears unsaved changes,
so asking for one is useful. It clears none of the other three
refusals: uncommitted changes under `replace=true`, a read-only buffer,
and an edit no tool can make. For those the agent stops and asks at
once, and the ask says which of the three it hit.

*The mechanism is built under =dd4b9a9f=, and this heading is what is
left* (the user, 2026-09-23). Its child =c35087d8= builds the prompt and
the wait with =org_edit= as the first user: a header line on the buffer
asking for the save, cleared by an after-save hook, and a wait script the
agent runs in the background on the Fibonacci schedule. It also records
why the waiting stays out of the tool: a tool that polled would freeze
the single-threaded Emacs the human needs to save in. So this heading,
blocked on =c35087d8=, covers the rest. First, extending the same prompt
and wait to the other tools that refuse an unsaved buffer
(=org_slice_add_member=, and =org_wrap_plan= once =60d6ab6e= makes it
refuse instead of saving). Second, making the three permanent refusals
stop and ask at once. Third, the =PreToolUse= guard that refuses a
direct write to a tracked org file while Emacs is visiting it.

*=create-lockfiles= as the guard's signal* (moved from =b5f94b88= at its
close, 2026-09-23). Emacs writes a lockfile beside a file exactly while
its buffer holds unsaved changes, and Doom leaves the setting off. Turned
on, it lets the =PreToolUse= guard tell whether the human has unsaved
changes with a file test instead of an =emacsclient= call. The guard is a
hook, and hooks here must survive an Emacs outage. Two things to measure
before relying on it: that the lockfile really disappears on save and on
revert, and that a stale one left by a crashed Emacs is recognisable,
since it names a PID that no longer runs.
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-22 Tue 20:20]--[2026-09-22 Tue 20:20] =>  0:00
- [2026-09-22 Tue 20:20]--[2026-09-22 Tue 20:20] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 08:42]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:44] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-23 Wed 08:42]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:44] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 08:44]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:45] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 08:44]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:45] record the save-prompt decision
:END:

When an org tool refuses a write, sessions fall through to `Edit`, `sed -i` or `awk … && mv` on `TODO.org`, which writes behind the live buffer. The user wants them to stop and wait instead, perhaps with a Fibonacci back-off and retry (2026-09-22). Retrying only helps when the refusal is transient. The human's unsaved changes clear when they save, so back off on those (1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8 s, capped), then ask. Three refusals never clear by waiting: `org_amend replace=true` on a file with uncommitted changes, a read-only buffer, and an edit no tool can make (`dd4b9a9f`, `24c7b537`, `da6a2fba`). For those the session should stop and ask at once, never fall through. The enforcement is a `PreToolUse` guard that refuses any direct write to the tracked org files while Emacs is visiting them. That is a sibling of `b69ecfe4`, and it avoids that heading's false-positive problem because it refuses the write itself without parsing the hunk.

Established (2026-09-22): three sessions bypassed Emacs that day. One edited a line-number citation into place with `Edit` because the buffer was read-only. One rewrote a drawer and body with `awk` and `mv`, and the `#+TAGS` line with `sed -i`, because `replace=true` refused. One hand-edited a headline tag and repaired a torn drawer. Each checked `buffer-modified-p` first, so nothing was lost, and the risk is the one time the check is skipped. The concrete harm on record is `53b0047d`, a clock marker desynced by a direct write. `Edit` and `Write` are easy to guard. Writes through `Bash` are not, and deserve a measurement of how often they occur before any design. `b5f94b88` points the other way, letting a session interject without waiting, and the queue already covers capture and amend. So this covers only the writes the queue does not.

*3ad389be's failure hook covers the outage half* (2026-09-23). On a
refused or timed-out call it tells the session not to hand-edit the tracked
org files and to tell the user. Refusals from a healthy Emacs never reach
=PostToolUseFailure=, so the =PreToolUse= guard proposed here is still
needed for them.


* TODO Answer a bad tool call with its error rather than a hang, until upstream defines json-rpc-error :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ecbbec2e-50d9-4581-9ded-15716f16c5f3
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 20:19]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

A tool call with a missing required argument or an unknown tool name gets no reply and reaches the model as "The operation timed out" (`ce012ad4`, confirmed 2026-09-22), and every such timeout in this project's transcripts was a misnamed argument that the error text would have corrected at once. The upstream fix may be slow to land, so this module could catch `json-rpc-error` itself: an advice around `claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--handle-tools-call` or `--validate-args`, converting the signal into a tool result that carries the message. A bare `define-error` is *not* enough, since upstream's outer handler replies with `id` nil and the client cannot match that reply. Unclassified: the brainstorming skill decides spike or bounded when it is picked up.

*A stopgap now ships with 3ad389be's failure hook* (the user, 2026-09-23).
When a call times out and Emacs answers the hook's probe idle, the hook
tells the session to check its argument names. It does not replace this
fix, which turns the timeout into the real error.


* TODO org_amend accepts a headline-shaped line in drawer text, which tears the drawer open :code:observed:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c0c5e015-c33e-4e1a-ad6e-907f9fa56c07
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 16:46]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:BLOCKER:  b2d4012c-6c61-47df-8086-d1cd205c73fc
:END:

Observed 2026-09-22 writing a :PLAN: drawer through org_amend drawer=PLAN: six section headers written as "** Title" at column zero were parsed as real headings, which terminated the drawer at the first one, manufactured six children under the heading, displaced the :LOGBOOK: below the orphaned :END:, and left the statistics cookie counting the wrong thing. Nothing refused it and the reply said "created :PLAN:". This is adjacent to 8a23d6ec but not covered by it: that rule and the escaping 3cd7b7d3 shipped both apply to a ^\*+ line inside a #+BEGIN_ block, where the damage is a fake heading; here there is no block, the text is drawer prose, and the damage is structural. bin/lint-org caught it only as six "heading has no :ID:" errors, which name the symptom rather than the cause. Proposed: extend the write-path escape to any ^\*+ line in drawer text, or refuse it there, since a drawer cannot contain a headline at all and escaping silently rewrites what the caller wrote.


* TODO org_amend replace=true eats the blank lines before the next heading, so org stops seeing it :code:observed:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5b46fbfd-6d0e-4eea-bcf5-baa1e39e1997
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 16:14]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:BLOCKER:  b2d4012c-6c61-47df-8086-d1cd205c73fc
:END:

claude-code-ide-org--replace-body deletes the body region and inserts (string-trim text), so replacing the last body before a heading takes the blank lines separating them with the trim and glues the heading onto the final line. Observed 2026-09-22 on 59861867: TODO.org:1008 became "written.** TODO Miss: ..." and org stopped parsing a heading there at all — org_outline reported the new id as resolving to no heading while org_amend, which locates by property, still wrote to it happily, and bin/lint-org reported 0 errors throughout. The damage is silent in both directions and the repair was a hand edit, which is itself against the rules. Proposed: --replace-body re-establishes the separation it found, or defers to claude-code-ide-org-heading-separator-lines; and bin/lint-org gains a check for a heading not at the start of its own line, which is the class org-lint calls "possibly misplaced heading" and only warns about.

*What this slice is, and what it is not.* Two closed slices already
worked this subject and neither is being reopened. `749301a0` made a
span's owner, edges and payload well defined; `c44c2119` hardened the
pass's own mechanics — its cost, its error reporting, its undo. What
neither touched is the *judgement* the pass asks for: a human is shown a
span, told nothing about why it exists, and asked to accept it, with
keystrokes that claim more than they mean, over clock machinery that is
still demonstrably wrong. That is the whole of this slice. Membership is
every live `:CATEGORY: Apply` and `Clock` heading that no slice already
holds, less the four `MAYBE`s, which stay in the backlog because a slice
member with no cookie reads as one this slice considered and dropped.

*Deliberately not `NEXT`, and not a decision to start.* Time tracking
stopped being a co-equal goal on 2026-09-18 (`8bbae3aa`); this declares
the body of work and its sequence so that picking it up later is a
choice rather than an archaeology. Thirty-odd un-grouped headings is how
a next action gets chosen by recency.

*The order, and why it is this order.*

**First the two decisions, because they reshape what follows.** Whether
`queue-append` should derive clock events from `DOING` transitions
(`55125136`, `WAITING` on the user) reverses a separation `4f6a6bb1`
chose deliberately, and half the members below are written against the
current split. How far the apply cycle can be automated (`361e2708`) is
bounded by the interactive constraint the queue exists for, and it
decides whether later ergonomics work is worth doing at all. Building
before either is answered is building twice.

**Then the record's active errors, worst first.** A turn that ends
holding an open clock (`3485b0d0`) mis-attributes everything after it;
`c` claiming its whole envelope (`aee8487e`) has recorded 3h11m over
sixteen minutes; overlapping intervals on two headings (`2d100d0d`)
cannot both be true and subtraction reaches only one. Then the pass's
own failures to complete: a conjunctive mark guard that marks and then
fails at apply (`653fea66`), gap spans that carry no events so half the
buffer cannot be dismissed (`445fe577`), the "aged out" mislabel
(`2c733857`), applied changes a `git checkout` can destroy (`79c224ab`),
a stale clock history that hangs the first clock-in after a restart
(`001fb310`), and the fatal signal at buffer quit (`489d6f61`). These
come before anything cosmetic because every one of them writes something
false, or loses something true, while the pass is otherwise working.

**Then the evidence, because the judgement is the product.** An
unassigned span's readout omitting what would identify its heading
(`5a6d2c75`) and a no-evidence span printing nothing at all
(`cf483f5d`) are the two halves of the same hole: the human is asked to
decide and shown nothing to decide on. Annotating at review time
(`8028e875`) and a key for flipping timestamps active (`01849bef`)
follow, because both are currently hand edits to text the pass just
wrote.

**Then the keystrokes, which are only worth shaping once the content is
right.** Marking for dismissal so one reason serves several
(`ee3c22c9`), `d` landing on the replacement line (`e95cc5d1`),
recentring after `m` (`396fd1c3`), and returning point and windows on
exit (`6c64cc9c`).

**Then measurement, which is the point of the whole feature and is
meaningless before the above.** Whether a short zero-run span should
default to claimed (`a865f83c`) is research that sets a default the rest
inherit; the review-attention clock stopping at the first apply
(`e3d1531d`) invalidates 101 recorded intervals, which is why rebuilding
them (`b5448133`) is sequenced immediately after rather than before. The
human half of the ratio already recorded and thrown away (`20cecd53`),
the daily report of worked headings carrying no interval (`37147e9e`),
the A/B across the phase-3 fixes (`445ec1f1`) and a clocktable that can
show a parent's own time (`64d34a64`) are the reporting surface that
makes any of it checkable.

**Last, the consumer-facing toggle** (`2cd74268`): auto-applying state
transitions in a task-only install is the only member that ships
behaviour to someone else's repo, and it should not go out over
machinery this slice has not finished correcting.

*Ruled out.* Widening to `:CATEGORY: Queue` — spans and attribution live
there too, and the union is the second goal entire rather than a slice.
Folding any of this into `8a2eb687`, whose subject is silent wrongness
in the tracker's own machinery, not the pass over it. And nominating a
`NEXT` member: a parked slice with a nominated action invites exactly
the drift the parking is for.

*Correction to "Ruled out", made on reading the composed checklist.*
`64d34a64` carries `NEXT` and the paragraph above reads as though
nothing here does. It is not this slice's nomination: it is the `Clock`
category's, made before this slice existed, and a member line copies its
referent's keyword by construction. What the ruled-out sentence means
stands — this slice nominates nothing of its own, and the slice heading
itself is `TODO`, not `NEXT`.
:END:

Thirty-four live headings carry `:CATEGORY: Apply` or `Clock`, and three
of them belong to a slice; the rest have sat un-grouped since the pass
was built. They are one subject — a human is asked, item by item, to
confirm a span they are shown no evidence for, with keystrokes that
claim more than they mean, over a clock that is still demonstrably
wrong. This slice declares that work and its order. It is deliberately
not `NEXT`: time tracking stopped being a co-equal goal on 2026-09-18
(`:ID:` 8bbae3aa), and declaring the body of work is not a decision to
start it.

* TODO A session that ends holding an open clock never closes it: 8 of 42 clocking sessions end on a clock_in :code:bounded:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3485b0d0-3b47-4a28-ae6f-e2061fa8f226
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 15:29]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 16:05]--[2026-09-23 Wed 16:06] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 16:05]--[2026-09-23 Wed 16:06] brainstorm the open-clock-at-stop backstop
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 16:09]--[2026-09-23 Wed 16:11] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-23 Wed 16:09]--[2026-09-23 Wed 16:11] design option 2: close abandoned sessions' clocks
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 17:06]--[2026-09-23 Wed 17:08] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-23 Wed 17:06]--[2026-09-23 Wed 17:08] record the approved design in :PLAN:
:END:
:PLAN:
Close the clock a session leaves open when the session itself ends; leave
the per-turn rule (=org_clock_out= as the turn's last call) as prose. Most
turn ends that hold a clock are harmless: the next turn carries on the same
heading. What is lost is the session that never returns, whose =clock_in=
stays the lane's anchor for ever. (The user, 2026-09-23, chose this over
enforcing the per-turn rule at =Stop= and over relaxing it.)

*The hook.* =bin/hooks/session-end-clock-close=, on =SessionEnd=. When the
session's queue file shows the main lane's (=agent_id= null) last clock
event is a =clock_in=, it pipes its payload to =queue-append clock_out=;
otherwise it does nothing. It reads the queue with the tolerant
=jq -n -R … fromjson?= stream =clock-target-check= uses, so one malformed
line cannot flip the answer. It self-gates on
=CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_TIME_TRACKING= like the other time hooks, and always
exits 0: nobody is left to answer a block. The event carries a null id,
like every queued =clock_out=; pairing is per lane, so it needs none.

*The timestamp is the session's end, not the last =pause=.* =queue-append=
stamps now and takes no override, and a backdated event is the
synthetic-evidence pattern 3ad389be declined. The record is written from
the turn guideposts inside the pair, so an idle tail widens the item's span
without claiming the idle as work. =source= reads =SessionEnd=, which marks
the pair as closed by the hook at review. This is not the guess 7771fc63
retired: the session has ended, which is evidence. A =/resume= reusing the
id owes a fresh =clock_in= before its first write anyway.

*Scope.* Main lane only: a subagent holding its own clock has no case to
design for. Out: SIGKILL and crashes, which fire no =SessionEnd=, and the
sessions already stranded, which are dismissed at review. A dead-session
detector is justified only if orphans keep appearing after this ships.

*Wiring and prose.* A =SessionEnd= row in =.claude/settings.json= (setting
the time-tracking variable in the command string, as that file's time rows
do) and in =hooks/hooks.json=; a row in the table in
=skills/org/references/org-time-tracking.md=, the reference, not the
generated rules copy.

*Testing.* =bin/session-end-clock-close-test=, on the pattern of
=bin/clock-target-check-test=, over fixture queues: open clock (appends one
=clock_out=), already closed, never clocked, only a subagent lane open (all
three append nothing), a malformed line beside an open clock (still
appends), time tracking off (nothing). Prove the suite discriminates by
running it against a no-op hook. Then one live headless session that clocks
in and is sent SIGTERM, to see the =clock_out= land.
:END:

A session that ends holding an open clock never closes it: the review pass
keeps an unmatched =clock_in= as the anchor for work still running, so the
orphan never resolves. Measured 2026-09-23 over the live queues: 8 of 42
clocking sessions end on a =clock_in=. The per-turn breach is wider (433 of
1,843 turn ends after a first =clock_in= held a clock) but mostly harmless,
so it stays a prose rule. =SessionEnd= fires on exit, SIGHUP and SIGTERM,
even mid-tool, but not on SIGKILL. The fix closes the clock there; the
design is in =:PLAN:=.

* TODO No org tool changes an existing heading's tags, so every tag change is a hand edit behind Emacs's back :code:bounded:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       da6a2fba-1ab6-4e6f-94f1-3a945d1fdd84
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 11:03]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:
Designed 2026-09-22 in session ba40781f through the brainstorming skill,
which kept the path tag the heading already carried: bounded, with
=org_set_property= as the pattern to copy. It was written into a Plan Mode
file, and the user asked for it here.

A new immediate tool, *=org_set_tags=*, taking =id=, =add= and =remove=,
each a space- or comma-separated tag list with colons stripped. The elisp
is =claude-code-ide-org-set-tags= in =config.el=, beside
=claude-code-ide-org-set-property=, and follows that function's order of
steps:

1. Resolve the heading with =claude-code-ide-org--id-find=. Refuse while
   =claude-code-ide-org--file-busy-p= holds, with the same unsaved-buffer
   message =org_set_property= gives, and when both lists are empty.
2. *Path-tag aware* (the user's choice, 2026-09-22): if =add= names a path
   tag, any other path tag already on the heading is removed, so one call
   does the replacement. If =add= names two path tags, refuse. A move down
   (=arch= to =spike=) is not refused, since the one-way rule is the
   skill's judgement, but the report names the tag it replaced so the move
   is visible. The three names come from one =defconst=, which the lint's
   one-path-tag rule switches to as well.
3. Compute the result from =org-get-tags nil t=, local tags only: current,
   minus =remove= and any displaced path tag, plus =add=, deduplicated, in
   order with new tags appended. Write it with =org-set-tags= and save,
   inside =claude-code-ide-org--at-id-writable=.
4. Report =Tags on "<title>": :a:b: (was :a:c:)=, plus =(replaced path tag
   X)= when that happened. When nothing changed, say so and save nothing.
   Removing a tag the heading lacks is a note, not an error.

Register it with =claude-code-ide-make-tool= beside =org_set_property='s
registration, and add ="org_set_tags"= to
=claude-code-ide-org--worked-tool-names=, since changing tags works the
heading. Add a row to the Immediate table in =org-mcp-tools.md= and
replace its sentence sending tag changes to text editing; replace the
brainstorming skill's "Reading and setting the tag" text edit with one
=org_set_tags add=<path>= call; re-run =bin/claude-org-setup=.

Verification: ERT against scratch files like the =set-property= tests
(add, remove, dedupe with no =:code:code:=, =bounded= to =arch= leaving one
path tag and =:code:= untouched, refusal of two path tags in one =add=,
refusal on a busy buffer, a no-op with no save, inherited tags not copied,
an unknown id). Prove the replacement test discriminates by disabling the
displacement and watching it fail. Live: reload the module, call the tool
on a scratch heading, and check the headline, the lint and the tool list.

*The tool also logs the change* (the user, 2026-09-22). Every change it
makes appends a line to the heading's =:LOGBOOK:=, distinct from a
keyword transition:
=- Tags ":after:" from ":before:" [timestamp] \\= with the caller's note
indented beneath, so =org_set_tags= takes an optional =note=. Both values
are the whole local tag sets. A no-op writes no line. Consolidation
already sorts such a line into the drawer's timeline and keeps its note
attached (tested 2026-09-22). Until this tool exists, the brainstorming
skill writes the line by hand with the headline edit; once it does, that
paragraph of the skill becomes one call.

A companion that logs tag changes made by hand in Emacs (the user, 2026-09-23). The tool logs its own changes, but a human's =C-c C-q= logs nothing. Org itself supplies every piece this needs. =org-set-tags= runs =org-after-tags-change-hook= when the tags really change. =org-add-log-setup= takes a purpose, and =org-store-log-note= looks up that purpose's template in =org-log-note-headings=. So adding =(tags . "Tags %-12s from %-12S %t")= to that alist and calling =(org-add-log-setup 'tags NEW OLD 'note)= writes =- Tags ":after:" from ":before:" [ts]=. org wraps =%s= and =%S= in double quotes, and =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= puts the line in =:LOGBOOK:=. With ='note=, org prompts for the indented reason; with ='time=, it writes the bare line.

Three constraints:
- The hook receives no arguments, and the old tags are local to =org-set-tags=. So the mechanism is =:around= advice on =org-set-tags=: read =(org-get-tags nil t)= first, call the original, and log when the result differs.
- =org-add-log-setup= only queues the note, and =post-command-hook= writes it later, provided =this-command= still matches. That is reliable for an interactive command, but it is the deferred-note path that breaks non-interactively (the state-transition rules, citing 3d576d29). So =org_set_tags= writes its own line directly, and binds a suppression variable so the advice does not log the same change a second time. Apply's suppression of the auto-clock trigger is the pattern to copy.
- It is scoped to the tracked org files. The advice does nothing in the user's other org files, for the same reason =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= was chosen over a global =setq=.

Out of reach: a session editing headline text calls no org function, so neither path sees the change. That is why the tool is still needed, and why the skill's hand edit keeps its hand-written line until the tool ships.

Verification: an ERT test calls =org-set-tags= under a simulated command loop (=this-command= bound, =post-command-hook= run) and asserts the line and its placement in =:LOGBOOK:=. A second asserts that =org_set_tags= writes exactly one line with the advice active. A third asserts that no line is written in an untracked file. The live check is a =C-c C-q= by hand on a scratch heading.
:END:
:LOGBOOK:

A heading's tags can be set only when it is created, through
=org_capture='s =tags=. After that nothing in the tool surface changes
them: =org_set_property= goes through =org-entry-put=, which refuses
=TAGS= outright ("The TAGS property cannot be set with org-entry-put",
tested 2026-09-22), and the MCP tools rules send tag changes to text
editing. So every added, removed or replaced tag is an edit written
behind Emacs's back, which is the unsaved-buffer divergence 53b0047d and
60d6ab6e describe.

*Established:*
- The need is now routine, not occasional: the brainstorming skill adds
  or replaces a path tag (=:spike:=, =:bounded:=, =:arch:=) on any
  already-filed heading it classifies (4ae7a04b), and =bin/lint-org=
  errors when a replacement leaves two.
- Org does the work natively (=org-set-tags=, =org-toggle-tag=), and
  =org_set_property= is the existing pattern for a small write tool
  addressed by =:ID:=.
- A tag edit by hand also risks the repeated-tag error the lint already
  catches, the case that produced =:code:code:=.


* TODO A span whose only resume is a synthetic bracket edge reports "no transcript for session nil (aged out)"
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2c733857-a280-42cc-bd7c-e182a4fb6ea3
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 10:45]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:PLAN:
=claude-code-ide-org--span-prompt-lines= finds the span's session from its
events' =:session-id=. The synthetic =resume= and =pause= edges that
b09aca60 adds (=--with-bracket-edges=, flagged =:synthetic t=) carry no
session id by design. So when a span's only resume is synthetic, the
session list is empty, =have-transcript= is nil, and the line reads "no
transcript for session nil (aged out)".

Fix it in that function. Skip synthetic events when collecting resumes
and sessions. If no real resume remains, treat the span like one with no
guideposts inside, which answers with the last prompt before the span,
from the session that owns it. When even that session is unknown, say
the stretch followed a mid-turn clock-out, not that a transcript aged
out. Test: a span whose only resume is synthetic renders no "aged out"
line, and a genuinely aged-out session still does.
:END:

The review buffer's prompt line reads "no transcript for session nil
(aged out)" for a span whose only =resume= is a synthetic bracket edge,
which carries no session id. Nothing aged out; the line is wrong. Seen
2026-09-22 on a 10:35–10:37 span from session 9e00a7a2. The span itself is
correct.

:PROPERTIES:

:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 16:11]
:CATEGORY: Queue

CLOCK: [2026-09-21 Mon 16:52]--[2026-09-21 Mon 16:53] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-21 Mon 16:52]--[2026-09-21 Mon 16:53] rewrite the body as a record

:PLAN:
Reproduce before fixing. Take session 9e00a7a2's queue file and the
applied watermarks from 2026-09-21, and replay the review's span
derivation across successive applies. Find which =clock_out= each phantom
span is built from, and why the one =clock_in= at 10:06:31 is still
available to pair with it after the span it opened was applied.
Hypothesis, unverified: pairing is positional, and applying a span
consumes the =clock_out= but not the =clock_in=, so every later orphan
=clock_out= re-pairs with it.

The fix follows from the reproduction. Once a =clock_in= has opened an
applied span it cannot open another, and a =clock_out= with no unconsumed
=clock_in= becomes an unassigned span, not a suggestion for the last
heading clocked. Add a regression test: one =clock_in=, an apply, then two
further =clock_out=s produce no further suggestion for that heading.

Time-tracking only. It never arises with the feature off.

A single =clock_in= goes on producing suggested spans for its heading at
review after review, labelled with its note, and each one applied writes
another CLOCK line into that heading's LOGBOOK, even after the heading
is DONE.

*Established (2026-09-21):* session 9e00a7a2 queued one =clock_in= on
c8773ec2, at 10:06:31. By the evening apply, c8773ec2's LOGBOOK held
eight phantom annotations with that note (10:52, 11:38, 12:34, 13:26,
13:56, 14:34, 14:44 and 15:26) against its one real span, 10:06 to

* TODO org_wrap_plan saves a buffer holding the human's unsaved edits, where org_amend would queue and org_slice_add_member refuses
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       60d6ab6e-a898-4894-82be-9f6086daf62d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 15:59]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:
=claude-code-ide-org-wrap-plan= runs through =--at-id-writable= and
calls =save-buffer=, and it never asks whether the buffer holds unsaved
human edits. The other writers do ask. =org_amend= defers to the queue
behind the write-through gate (b5f94b88), and =org_slice_add_member= and
=org_set_property= refuse and ask for a retry once saved. So a wrap
invoked while the human is mid-edit saves their half-done text along
with the wrap, and whatever commits next carries it under a message
that does not describe it.

Put =org_wrap_plan= behind the same refusal the other structural writers
use. It is a structural write, not prose, so it should refuse rather
than queue. Then sweep every caller of =--at-id-writable= for the same
gap: the refusal belongs in one shared place, not per tool. The
regression test modifies a buffer, calls the wrap, and asserts both a
refusal and that the file on disk is unchanged.
:END:

Observed 2026-09-21. The user reflowed 94e6b615's =:PLAN:= drawer and
did not save. A session's =org_wrap_plan= on b52df20b saved the buffer,
and the reflow landed in that session's commit ffc67a7 under a message
about b52df20b. Nothing was lost, but only by luck. Refuse on unsaved
edits, as the other structural writers do.


* TODO The member-drawer backfill clause is scaffolding: once every open heading has its canonical shape, the lint should require it and the clause retire
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       fce6bd35-27ad-4724-8dbb-c9df4b68fb10
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 12:50]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:PLAN:
The SOP adopted 2026-09-21 (org conventions, "A slice's plan, and its members'") says a member with no =:PLAN:= drawer gets one before its step is worked. That clause exists only because most open headings predate the convention. Once =6521dd56='s corpus pass has given every open heading its canonical shape, the clause describes a state that cannot occur. Prose describing an impossible state is exactly what =bf7e0b8e='s deletion pass removed.

So, after the corpus pass lands:
1. =bin/lint-org= errors on a member of an open slice that has no =:PLAN:= drawer. That keeps the shape enforced and stops it depending on anyone remembering.
2. Delete the backfill clause from the conventions, keeping the rule that a decision lands in the member's drawer.
3. Re-run =bin/claude-org-setup= so the promoted copy follows.

Order within the slice: after =e128e4fa=, since that pass is what produces the shape.
:END:

The SOP's instruction to give a drawerless member a =:PLAN:= is scaffolding for a pre-convention corpus. When =6521dd56='s pass makes the shape universal, the lint should enforce it and the clause should be deleted.

* WAITING Decide whether queue-append should derive clock events from DOING transitions, reversing the separation 4f6a6bb1 chose :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       55125136-5939-434e-9676-96ea66a75770
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 11:05]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:

Row 5 of the enforcement audit's convert list (3cd7b7d3), set aside there as the user's decision. "Call org_clock_in when entering DOING and org_clock_out when leaving" is an eleven-row table in org-time-tracking.md and the most mechanical clause left in the always-loaded rules: queue-append sees every todo event and could append the clock event itself, unless the call is marked retroactive. Against it: org_set_todo and org_clock_in are separate calls on purpose, so that state and clock are decided separately (4f6a6bb1), and a retroactive DOING or a grouping's DOING must open nothing. And clock timing has its own open defect: work after a mid-turn clock_out reaches review as nothing (b09aca60), which derived events would make more common, not less, since every transition out of DOING would close the run. Decide after b09aca60 is fixed. WAITING on the user.

*The user, 2026-09-21: leave it as it is* — state and clock stay separate calls — and fix b09aca60 first. Still parked; revisit only if, with clock-out timing made safe, the second call is still being missed often enough to show in the miss count.


* TODO org_set_todo's no-change check reads the file and ignores the queue, so a state set and reverted between applies cannot be queued back :code:bug:bounded:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       57f37f0e-4461-42f4-8b33-cde59318b93a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 10:42]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:
The no-change refusal in =claude-code-ide-org-set-todo= compares the requested
state with the heading's *effective* state instead of the keyword on disk.
Effective state is the keyword on disk, carried forward through the heading's
pending =todo= events.

A return trip is queued, not cancelled (the user, 2026-09-23). =REVIEW -> DOING
-> REVIEW= goes into the queue as two ordinary items. It is the true history,
since =REVIEW -> DOING= is rework by the keyword semantics. Apply already
replays each event and never collapses them (see
=claude-code-ide-org--review-items-from-queue=). =--review-projected-staleness=
accepts the return, because its =from= matches the pre-batch state. Cancelling
the pair was declined for two reasons. It would need a new cancel event kind, or
a writer that reads the queue, which breaks the blind =O_APPEND= property
=6b1e73c4= kept. And it would erase a rework round trip that really happened.

Effective state. A new helper, =claude-code-ide-org--effective-todo-state (id
disk-state)=, reads =claude-code-ide-org--queue-events=: every session,
unapplied events only. It keeps the =todo= events for =id=, orders them by
=:ts=, and returns the last one's =:state=, or =disk-state= when there are none.
It reads the queue and never writes it, like =--pending-capture= already does.

The check.
- Requested equals effective: refused as now. That also catches a duplicate
  =DOING= queued over a disk =REVIEW=. When effective differs from disk, the
  refusal names it starred: =no change -- "H" already holds DOING* (queued, not
  yet applied)=.
- Otherwise: queued. When effective differs from disk, the reply adds =, after
  DOING* in the queue= after the closing parenthesis.

=(was X)= is a contract, not prose, and is unchanged. =bin/hooks/queue-append=
recovers =from= with a greedy =sed 's/.*(was \([^)]*\)).*/\1/p'=. So the reply
carries exactly one =(was ...)=, holding the disk state and nothing else, and no
nudge text may contain "(was". =from= keeps meaning "on disk at queue time",
which the chain logic of =6b1e73c4= relies on. The starred effective state sits
outside the parentheses, for the reader. The =*= notation replaced "(was X)" in
footnotes; this reply is where it does not (the user, 2026-09-23).

Out of scope: the pending-capture branch
(=claude-code-ide-org--tolerating-pending-capture='s pending path). A heading
that has not been written has no disk state to disagree with.

Docstring: the no-change paragraph and the =from= paragraph each gain a line
saying the refusal reads effective state while =from= still reads disk.

Tests. Each is proved to discriminate by copying the fix aside, restoring HEAD's
version and re-running.
- =config-test.el=: disk =REVIEW= with =-> DOING= queued; asking for =REVIEW=
  queues, and the reply's clause still parses to =REVIEW=.
- =config-test.el=: the same setup; asking for =DOING= is refused, and the reply
  shows =DOING*=.
- =config-test.el=: the round trip =REVIEW -> DOING -> REVIEW= applies in one
  review pass, nothing flagged stale, and =:LOGBOOK:= gets both State lines.
- =bin/queue-append-test=: a reply carrying the "after X* in the queue" suffix
  yields =from: REVIEW=.
- The existing no-pending-event tests stand unchanged.

Files: =modules/tools/claude-code-ide-org/config.el=, =config-test.el=,
=bin/queue-append-test=.
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 08:51]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:52] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 08:51]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:52] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 08:55]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:56] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 08:55]--[2026-09-23 Wed 08:56] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 09:27]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:29] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-23 Wed 09:27]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:29] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 09:29]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:30] =>  0:01
- Tags ":code:bug:bounded:" from ":code:bug:" [2026-09-23 Wed 09:29] \\
  classified by brainstorming: one function's existing check changes
- [2026-09-23 Wed 09:29]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:30] record the effective-state design in PLAN
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 09:31]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:32] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 09:31]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:32] condense body to the convention
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 09:36]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:37] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 09:36]--[2026-09-23 Wed 09:37] commit the user's refill
:END:

=org_set_todo='s no-change refusal compares the requested state with the keyword
on disk and ignores the queue. So after a queued =REVIEW -> DOING=, the return
to =REVIEW= is refused, and the next apply lands =DOING= on a heading that is
back in =REVIEW=. The reverse also slips through: a second =DOING= is queued as
a duplicate. Observed 2026-09-21 on 63713df3. The fix tests the heading's
effective state, meaning the disk keyword carried through its pending =todo=
events, and queues the return trip rather than cancelling the pair. The design
is in =:PLAN:=.

* DOING [0/12] The misses a human catches reach no record: one heading per kind of miss, amended at each catch, so the catch is counted :code:
DEADLINE: <2026-10-12 Mon>
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       158d7e80-6d72-4518-a804-d8abf2e7e693
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 08:44]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-09-21 Mon 08:44] \\
  composing at the user's direction

- [2026-09-21 Mon 09:19]--[2026-09-21 Mon 09:20] make the denominator work with time tracking off

- [2026-09-21 Mon 10:29]--[2026-09-21 Mon 10:30] record a self-caught unverified claim

- [2026-09-23 Wed 15:20]--[2026-09-23 Wed 15:21] correct a miss line, record the catch
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 18:50]--[2026-09-23 Wed 18:51] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 18:50]--[2026-09-23 Wed 18:51] record four self-caught misses
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 20:05]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:06] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 20:05]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:06] record a user-caught and two self-caught misses
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 20:27]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:28] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 20:27]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:28] record the user's org-ql catch
:END:
:PLAN:
*What this is for, and why it is not a member of the drift slice (decided with the user 2026-09-21).* It is an instrument, not a deliverable: it is read to decide which rule to move out of recall next, and to tell whether a conversion worked. A slice closes on its pull request; this stays open while it is useful, so membership would hold the slice open for a reason unrelated to its code.

*No clean "before" exists, and none is needed.* The slice's conversions land before any baseline could accumulate, and delaying them to collect one was declined. The comparison is therefore *by kind, not by date*: a kind whose rule was mechanised should fall to zero counted misses, while the kinds left as judgement are the control. If an unconverted kind falls just as far, the conversions explain nothing.

*How long, stated as volume rather than calendar.* Two events on the first day. Ranking eleven kinds needs on the order of thirty counted events; at that rate, two to three working weeks. Read it at the daily ceremony (the report already prints the count by rule), and review it properly at thirty events or on 2026-10-12, whichever comes first — then close the children whose kinds are both mechanised and silent, and give any kind that reached the catch-all twice its own sibling.

*Conditions that make the number mean something.* A catch counts only when the session records it, so an unrecorded catch is itself a miss of this convention and goes on the catch-all when noticed. The denominator is sessions that wrote at least one queue line, which =bin/miss-rate= reports per day and per project.

*Consumer projects.* The kinds live here because the rules missed are the plugin's; a catch made in another project amends these headings and its queue line carries that project's =cwd=, so =bin/miss-rate --project= separates them. Requires the consumer to run a plugin checkout that has the miss code and to have re-run =claude-org-setup=. For agentic-euchre on 2026-09-21 neither was true: its skill link resolves to the worktree =claude-code-ide-org-swc=, detached at 656fc16, twenty-two commits before the counter existed. Unverified: whether that link wires the plugin's hooks at all — euchre's last queue lines are from 2026-09-15.

*Corrected the same day, on the user's prompt: euchre runs with time tracking off, and its silence since 2026-09-15 is suspended work, not evidence about the wiring.* What time tracking being off does change is the denominator. Without guideposts, a session that misses nothing and queues no state change writes no line at all, so "sessions that wrote at least one queue line" would have counted only the sessions with something to report — a rate biased upward in every default consumer. The first =org_*= call of a session now appends an ungated =session= line (dropped by the reader, like =miss=), so the denominator is "sessions that used an org tool" in either configuration. Two kinds cannot fire there at all and should be read as absent, not as zero: =clock-target= is gated with the feature, and the clock child has nothing to observe.

*Who does the observing (settled with the user 2026-09-21): both, and the user never touches the tracker.* A hook or tool catch counts itself. A catch by the user needs only that they say so in conversation, in any words; the session amends the line. A catch by the session of its own slip is recorded the same way. The amend's =note= begins =user:= or =self:= and the counter puts that in the rule — =caught:<slug>/user=, =/self=, or =/unmarked= — because the two are different measurements: what gets past the session, and what it repairs on its own. The user's series is the more trustworthy one, since they can see whether the dated line appeared.
:END:

The miss rate (63713df3) is a floor: it counts what a hook or a tool caught, and the misses the user catches in conversation — the ones the drift analysis is about — reach no file. Decided by the user 2026-09-21: each kind of miss gets a child heading here, and a catch is answered by one dated body line amended onto the matching child, naming what happened. "Capture or amend" therefore dissolves to amend; a kind nothing covers goes on the catch-all child until it earns its own. A child closes when its rule has been moved out of recall.

** TODO Miss: an org question answered by scraping text instead of asking org
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       67861ff3-3a72-4c53-8fd8-1cae9a31bc77
:CREATED:  [2026-09-22 Tue 16:10]
:MISS_RULE: text-scrape
:END:

Earned its own sibling on 2026-09-22, the day the rule was caught twice, per
the catch-all's note that a rule appearing a second time stops being
uncategorised. The two lines below moved here from
[[id:59861867-e807-43a8-a0fa-5ca227167022][59861867]] and are unchanged but
for the cross-reference in the second, which pointed at the first while both
lived there.

The rule is the org-dev skill's §0b: before answering a question, ask whether
org's own semantics decide it — entry boundaries, membership, property
inheritance, keyword sets, clock arithmetic — and if so ask Emacs, which is
the parser that writes these files. A regex over org text is a second
implementation of those semantics, and it disagrees *silently*, in the
direction of looking right. §0b's second half was added 2026-08-28 for
exactly this case: the original rule triggered on *building* a helper, and
what kept happening was reading one to answer a question.

Both =PreToolUse= guards this repo has (=emacsclient-guard=,
=generated-guard=) guard *writes*. Nothing guards a read, so this rule is
still enforced by recitation alone — which is the condition the guard family
exists to remove, and the likely shape of this heading's close. One dated
line per catch.

- [2026-09-22 Tue] Caught by the user: "why are you grepping? =org_outline=
  should tell you that, no?" Asked for 8a2eb687 grouped by =:CATEGORY:=, the
  session scraped member ids with =awk= and queried Emacs through
  =emacsclient=, and the output came back buried in text properties.
  =org_outline= with the member ids as scope returns each one's
  =:CATEGORY:=. That was the always-loaded "start with org_outline" rule,
  recited and missed.
- [2026-09-22 Tue] Caught by the user: "you reached for =awk= instead of
  using the =emacs-tools=, didn't you!" Asked for open tasks not in any open
  slice, the session scraped slice members out of =TODO.org= with =awk=
  instead of calling =org_outline= with the three slice ids as scope, which
  returns each slice's members as =->= references. Second catch of this rule
  today — the entry above — which is what earned this heading. The scrape
  then failed *silently*: the member file came out empty, and a 140-line
  candidate list was printed whose "not in a slice" filter had done nothing,
  with slice members sitting in it unremoved. So the second face of the catch
  is the always-loaded rule that silence is not a passing check — an
  intermediate with zero rows was used as a filter without ever being looked
  at.

- [2026-09-23 Wed] Self-caught: surveying slice 8a2eb687's members for =:PLAN:= drawers, the session counted drawer lines with an =awk= scan of =TODO.org= that finds heading boundaries by regex, when =org_body drawer=PLAN= answers the same question per heading.
- [2026-09-23 Wed] Caught by the user: "I think you give up on =org-ql= too quickly. I saw you try one query and fall back to grep." Looking for the transcripts-to-org proposal, the session tried =heading:"transcript"= once and then grepped =TODO.org= and =DONE.org= three times. A bare-word query (=transcript readable=) is org-ql's full-text =rifle= predicate, and it finds =96ddf1ef= in DONE.org in one call. The session had treated the five predicates =org_query='s description names as the whole language.


* TODO Nothing runs the shell hook suites, so three sat red for a day and a merge without anyone seeing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       94e6b615-3778-426b-b40a-306b517a837f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-19 Sat 16:34]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 20:52]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:53] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 20:52]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:52] assigned at review
- [2026-09-23 Wed 20:52]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:53] brainstorm the all-suites runner
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 20:55]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:56] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 20:55]--[2026-09-23 Wed 20:56] brainstorm the all-suites runner
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 21:04]--[2026-09-23 Wed 21:07] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-23 Wed 21:04]--[2026-09-23 Wed 21:07] brainstorm the all-suites runner
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 21:07]--[2026-09-23 Wed 21:11] =>  0:04
- [2026-09-23 Wed 21:07]--[2026-09-23 Wed 21:11] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 21:16]--[2026-09-23 Wed 21:17] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-23 Wed 21:16]--[2026-09-23 Wed 21:17] assigned at review
:END:
:PLAN:
*The design, approved 2026-09-23 (the user), brainstormed on the bounded path.* One runner, =bin/test-all=, runs every suite and demands positive evidence from each, and pre-push calls it. Pre-push rather than pre-commit (the user, 2026-09-21): a full run measured 41 s that day (24 s shell, 17 s ERT), which is tolerable per push and too heavy per commit, since most commits here are bookkeeping that touches only =TODO.org=. It is a separate script rather than a =--all= flag on =bin/test=, and =next-session.md= does not name it: pre-push already runs it on every push that matters, and a second copy there would drift.

*The runner.* =bin/test-all= runs =bin/test= (ERT), then every =bin/*-test= found by glob, not from a list. The original failure was suites nobody remembered, and a list would reintroduce that for every new suite. The suites run one at a time. Each runs with =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= unset and =CLAUDE_ORG_QUEUE_DIR= pointed at a temporary directory, on top of each suite's own isolation, so a suite's provoked refusals cannot reach the real queue (the 2026-09-21 incident). It prints one line per suite (name, counts, seconds) and a total, and a failing suite's full output.

*Positive evidence, never silence* (the user, 2026-09-23, choosing it over trusting exit codes). Every shell suite ends with the line =<suite>: N passed, M failed= and exits =$((M > 0))=. A suite passes only with that line present, N > 0, M = 0 and exit 0; a missing line fails. For ERT the runner parses =Ran N tests, … 0 unexpected=. About 18 suites change to the standard line. That also removes a latent silent pass: six suites (=apply-detect=, =check-conventions=, =doc-sync-check=, =prepare-commit-msg=, =queue-append=, =sync-plans=) end with =exit "$count"=, which wraps to 0 at 256 failures.

*Hermetic suites* (the user, 2026-09-23).
- =queue-append-test= reads the real =TODO.org= to find the =:MISS_RULE: uncategorised= heading. It gets its own fixture heading instead.
- =claude-org-setup-test='s =--check= cases leave =DOOMDIR= unset, so =--check= also judges the user's real =~/.config/doom=: the glue's content, the =load!= stub in =config.el=, and the absence of the old 202 override. Any change to what =--glue= generates would then turn the suite red on the user's machine until =--glue= is re-run, and pre-push would block the very push carrying the change. A machine with no Doom config skips those checks and passes without testing anything. So =DOOMDIR= points at a scratch directory holding a freshly rendered glue and the stub, and one case is added for each of the three drift conditions. (=c562b69a= would remove the glue's drift at its source.)
- The runner fails statically on any suite that refers to =$repo_root/TODO.org=, =$repo_root/DONE.org=, =~/.config/doom= or =$HOME/.config=, so this cannot come back unnoticed.

*Pre-push skips org-only pushes* (the user, 2026-09-23). If every path in the pushed range is =TODO.org=, =DONE.org= or under =plans/=, the hook prints that the suites were skipped, and why, and runs none. That is safe only because the suites are hermetic, which is why hermeticity is part of this heading. Any other push runs =bin/test-all= and is refused on failure. =--no-verify= remains the escape.

*Testing.* =bin/test-all-test=, with fake suites in a scratch =bin/=: pass; fail; exit 0 with no final line; =0 passed=; 256 failures; a suite reading the real =TODO.org=. =bin/pre-push-test= gains an org-only range, a mixed range, and a failing suite that blocks the push. =claude-org-setup-test= gains the three drift cases. Each fix is shown to fail against HEAD's version. Then one live =bin/test-all= on the real tree, reporting its totals and time.

Files: =bin/test-all= and =bin/test-all-test= (new), =.githooks/pre-push=, about 18 =bin/*-test= files including =queue-append-test= and =claude-org-setup-test=.
:END:

=bin/test= runs only the ERT suite. Nothing runs the 21 shell =bin/*-test= suites, so one can stay red through a merge with nobody seeing. Designed 2026-09-23 on the bounded path: =bin/test-all= requires a standard final line from every suite, and pre-push runs it on any push that is not org-only.

*Established:*
- PR #28 turned three suites red: 40, 9 and 6 failures, 64 across four suites with setup's 9. Nobody saw them until 2026-09-19. All were fixed that day ([[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::1b4b3be][1b4b3be]], [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::f733179][f733179]], [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::7f54503][7f54503]]), and one was a real bug. DONE.org records an earlier instance in =bin/pre-commit-test=.
- The suites were found writing provoked refusals into the calling session's queue on 2026-09-21, fixed in [[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::bc23c76][bc23c76]].
- A full run took 41 s on 2026-09-21: 24 s shell, 17 s ERT.
- Only three suites print a count line. Six end with =exit "$count"=, which wraps to 0 at 256 failures. =queue-append-test= reads the real =TODO.org= (2026-09-23).

Noticed, not in scope: =claude-org-setup --check= reads the real =~/.config/doom=, so that suite is not hermetic either.

* NEXT [0/2] config.el is the last place time tracking and task tracking are still entangled, 30% of 18k lines :code:arch:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a7f844ba-7b70-48c6-833c-cd7e4e38cf14
:CREATED:  [2026-09-19 Sat 08:08]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:LOGBOOK:

- [2026-09-22 Tue 16:25]--[2026-09-22 Tue 16:26] brainstorm splitting config.el at the time seam
CLOCK: [2026-09-22 Tue 16:32]--[2026-09-22 Tue 16:33] =>  0:01

CLOCK: [2026-09-22 Tue 16:36]--[2026-09-22 Tue 16:37] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-22 Tue 16:36]--[2026-09-22 Tue 16:37] brainstorm splitting config.el at the time seam
CLOCK: [2026-09-22 Tue 16:39]--[2026-09-22 Tue 16:46] =>  0:07

- Tags ":code:arch:" from ":code:" [2026-09-22 Tue 16:42] \\
  classified at its first brainstorm: it restructures how the module's
  components fit together and changes load order in a module with a
  recorded load-order trap, which is the architectural definition rather
  than a bounded change to an existing flow
:END:
:PLAN:
Designed 2026-09-22 through the brainstorming skill, which classified the
heading =arch= — its first classification.

*The mechanism is Doom's own, and finding it reversed an earlier decision in
this same dialogue.* The first decision taken was "separate files now,
conditional load later" (the user, 2026-09-22), made while it appeared that
Emacs could not know whether time tracking is on: =config.el= never reads
=CLAUDE_PLUGIN_OPTION_TIME_TRACKING=, which reaches *hooks* as an
environment variable, and the option would have needed new plumbing. That
constraint is false at the Doom level. =modulep!= tests a module flag and
=load!= loads a =+name.el= file conditionally — the pattern Doom's own
=doom/compat= module uses for four of its files, and fifteen =+flag.el=
files exist in this install. So the deferral is unnecessary and the design
delivers the consumer benefit directly (the user, 2026-09-22, choosing the
flag over autoloads and over both).

*Measured 2026-09-22, against a file that has grown to 18,394 lines since
the body above was written.* The 30% claim holds: eight time-shaped sections
total 5,358 lines, 29%. Two of the body's other facts do not. The
tool-registration assertion lives in =bin/lib/check-org-dev-skill.el=, not
=bin/check-conventions= — a different checker, run by pre-commit only when
the org-dev skill is staged. And the =;;; MCP tool registration= header is
*orphaned*: eleven unrelated sections sit between it and the block it names.

*Which retires the body's second motivation as stated.* "Splitting would
materialise a decomposition already documented in comments — that is what
makes this a move rather than a redesign" is not true of the file as it
stands: task-side code makes **130 references to 47 symbols defined inside
the time-named sections**, and time-side code makes 148 references to 78
symbols outside them. The comments do not delimit what they name.

*The invariant, in its positive form.*

"Task never calls time" is the checkable half, and on its own it would
strand the places where task-side code legitimately wants time behaviour.
The rule's constructive form is that *=config.el= defines extension points
and =+time.el= registers into them*. The daily ceremony is the model, since
it already documents one hook and one payload with either half possibly
absent: =config.el= runs a report-contribution hook and =+time.el= adds the
stale-interval half, rather than =config.el= naming
=--find-stale-open-intervals=. The lint then checks something literal — no
symbol defined in =+time.el= appears in =config.el=.

*Two children, because the two halves are unlike work.*

*Kept as a parent with two children rather than one heading* (the user,
2026-09-22): all the judgement and all the risk are in the re-homing, and
the cut is mechanical once the direction holds. The parent is not divided
with =org_divide=, because its subject — the entanglement itself — is the
parent concept rather than either step.

*=fb849c29=, re-home, no behaviour change.* The 130 references sort into
three unlike groups. Five generic helpers misfiled into "Stale interval
recovery" — =--tracked-files=, =--parse-org-timestamp=, =--report-scope=,
=--today-p=, =--tracked-buffer-p= — account for 61 of the 130 and belong in
the =Helper= section. Core task entry points sitting in time-named sections,
=claude-code-ide-org-review= and =--review-items-from-queue= among them, go
to the sections their names imply. The review buffer's thirteen interactive
commands are the sibling section's problem. What remains in the seven span
and attention sections is the real time half. The orphaned
=;;; MCP tool registration= header goes in the same pass, since the plan
depends on the comments becoming a trustworthy map. Proved by =bin/test=
staying green and the direction lint going from 130 violations to zero.

*=055886c1=, the cut.* The seven sections move to =+time.el=, and
=config.el= ends its own code with =(if (modulep! +time) (load! "+time"))=.
The gated load goes *immediately before the tool-registration block*, not
at the end of the file: after every task definition, before the
registrations, and leaving the registration block still the last top-level
form — so =bin/lib/check-org-dev-skill.el='s anchor keeps holding with no
documentation change. "Clock report" *stays* in =config.el=:
=org_clock_report= is one of the eighteen registered tools, the clock tools
stay registered with time tracking off, and it wraps =org-clock-report= over
CLOCK lines that exist regardless of the feature. A consumer enables the
half with =:tools (claude-code-ide-org +time)=.

*The review buffer is shared, and stays.*

It applies task events and span events in one place, so it remains in
=config.el= whether or not time tracking exists. =config.el= defines the
keymap with the task keys; =+time.el= adds its thirteen with =define-key= at
load. The renderer gains a fallback branch: an item whose kind it does not
recognise renders as unrecognised rather than erroring — which is also what
a queue file written while the feature was on and read after it was turned
off will produce.

*The two switches.*

Doom's =+time= flag and the plugin's =time_tracking= option are
independent, and the dangerous disagreement is *option on, flag off*: hooks
keep appending clock and span events, nothing loads to consume them, and the
queue accumulates silently. The Doom-native place to catch that is
=doctor.el=, which 64 modules in this install carry and this one does not.
Adding one belongs to the cut. *Flagged at design time as the section least
likely to survive review*, since it adds a file the module has never had.

*Verification.*

Re-home: =bin/test= green before and after, the direction lint at zero, and
a symbol-level diff showing every move is a move — same definition text,
different position. Cut: load with the flag off and assert every registered
tool still resolves; with the flag on, assert the review buffer's time keys
are bound and the ceremony's second half appears. Prove the lint
discriminates by re-introducing one task-to-time reference and watching it
fail.

*Out of scope.*

Autoload cookies and lazy loading inside either half — Doom's other
mechanism, used by 64 modules here, and a separate question. The per-domain
split into eight or ten files, which motivation 3 would favour and this
design does not attempt: two files leave the largest at roughly 13,000
lines. And whether the plugin option and the Doom flag should become one
thing rather than two that agree.

*Corrections from the plan self-review (2026-09-22).* Two numbers above
need fixing, one of them wrong rather than merely loose.

*=doctor.el= is carried by 82 modules in this install, not 64.* The 64 was
the =autoload.el= count reused from a different measurement in the same
session — the figure quoted under "Out of scope" for autoloads is the one
that is correct, and the switches section borrowed it by mistake. The
argument is unaffected and slightly stronger: =doctor.el= is the most
widely adopted of the optional module files here.

*And "the seven span and attention sections" is the eight measured ones
minus "Clock report".* So =+time.el= receives roughly 5,268 lines rather
than the 5,358 the measurement reports, since the 90 lines behind
=org_clock_report= stay with the registered tool.
:END:

=8bbae3aa= separated time tracking from task tracking everywhere except the module. The hooks self-gate, the rules forked into =org-time-tracking.md=, the manifest declares the option, the prose demoted the goal — and =modules/tools/claude-code-ide-org/config.el= still holds both in one file of **17,954 lines**, every line of which a consumer loads whether or not the feature is on.

*Measured 2026-09-19.* The file declares **45 =;;;= sections**, and eight of them are time-shaped — =Span evidence= (1859), =One stretch of work, one lane= (1767), and the clock, guidepost, stale-interval and attention machinery — totalling **5405 lines, 30% of the file**. The remaining 37 sections are 12,504 lines. No section dominates: the largest is 1991 lines and a dozen sit between 400 and 2000, so the decomposition is broad rather than lopsided.

*Four motivations, ranked, because only some are independent:*

1. *The time/task seam.* The strongest, the only one that benefits a **consumer**, and a direct continuation of =8bbae3aa= rather than a new idea. A default install loads 5405 lines that can never run for it.
2. *The structure already exists.* Splitting would **materialise** a decomposition already documented in comments — =Capture=, =Refile=, =Write-through gate=, =Native transition enforcement= — not invent one. That is what makes this a move rather than a redesign.
3. *Size alone.* Real, and the weakest: a large file is not wrong.
4. *A digest boundary.* What prompted the idea, and now the least of it. =2cd74268= wants a checksum over the apply path; a digest over eleven *named* functions is fragile to additions, where a file boundary includes new ones automatically. Filed as a sibling of that heading rather than a child precisely because motivation 1 outlives the ratchet being cancelled.

*Two costs to name before committing.* =claude-code-ide-org--append-to-drawer= has four call sites across four sections, only two of them on the apply path, so shared helpers need a home and the split is not mechanical — and drawer writing is where **both** of this project's data-loss incidents lived (=ba8249c1=, =b74e0f19=, per =c084553c='s survey). And =bin/check-conventions= asserts "config.el tool-registration block is the last top-level form", which exists for a reason and needs rethinking rather than deleting.

*Also to establish first, cheaply:* whether the module is loaded by Doom as one file or by path. It is symlinked into =~/.config/doom/modules/tools/= and enabled via =:tools claude-code-ide-org=, so splitting turns implicit single-file loading into explicit ordering — and the =org-clock-persist-load= trap (org-dev skill §2) is the standing evidence that load *order* in this module is not a free variable.

*Not a member of any slice.* Its strongest motivation continues a closed one; it earns a slice of its own or joins whichever is nominated next.

*Re-measured 2026-09-22, and the design is in =:PLAN:=.* The file is now
18,394 lines and the eight time-shaped sections total 5,358 of them, so the
30% claim above holds. Two other facts above do not: the tool-registration
assertion lives in =bin/lib/check-org-dev-skill.el=, not
=bin/check-conventions=, and the =;;; MCP tool registration= header is
orphaned eleven sections away from the block it names. The second motivation
as written — that splitting would materialise a decomposition the comments
already document — is retired by measurement: task-side code makes 130
references to 47 symbols defined inside the time-named sections, so the
comments do not delimit what they name. The heading now has two children,
the re-homing that carries all the judgement and the cut that is mechanical
once it lands.

Measured 2026-09-22: task-side code makes 130 references to 47 symbols defined inside the eight time-named sections, so those sections are not a seam and cutting on them would move the wrong code.
