* TODO Promotion never removes, and --check never looks, so a dropped rule file would linger in every consumer :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       96ad6e95-452d-4be2-9bc7-b2c2efa44988
:CREATED:  [2026-09-19 Sat 05:44]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Asked by the user 2026-09-19 while the promoted set was being changed: does the setup check look for files previously deployed that have since disappeared from the source? **It does not.**

*Verified.* =--check= iterates =\"${promote[@]}\"= and nothing else — it asks "is each file I *would* write correct?" and never reads the target directory's contents. Promotion writes; nothing removes. So a file dropped from the promote list stays in every consumer's =.claude/rules/= indefinitely, **still loaded every session**, describing a mechanism that may no longer exist — while =--check= reports =exit 0= throughout.

*Same class as the finding just closed* (=827bb778=): the output reads as "your rules are correct" and means "the files I know about are correct". The difference is that this one has never fired, which is why nobody noticed.

*Why it has never fired.* The promote list has only ever **grown**: =42a3221= created it, =org-footnote-citations.md= joined 2026-09-16, =org-time-tracking.md= today. Nothing has been removed. The first removal would be silent, in every consumer at once, and the symptom would be a session following a rule the plugin no longer ships — which is the hardest kind of wrongness to trace, because the file looks generated and authoritative.

*Cheap to detect, because the marker already exists.* Every promoted copy opens with =<!-- Generated by claude-org-setup from the claude-code-ide-org plugin.=, which the clobber guard already greps for. An orphan is therefore exactly: a file in =.claude/rules/= carrying that marker whose basename is not in =promote=. Two lines in the =--check= block, and it can only ever flag files this script wrote — a consumer's own hand-written rules have no marker and are never touched.

*What to decide rather than assume:* whether =--check= merely **reports** an orphan or whether =claude-org-setup= **deletes** it. Reporting is the conservative default and matches every other row's behaviour. Deleting is what actually fixes a consumer, but the script's standing contract is that it refuses to touch anything it did not generate, and an unattended delete of a file a human may have since edited is the one way that contract breaks. A =--prune= flag is the likely shape.

*Related, and worth checking in the same pass:* the same question applies to =templates/= and to the skill symlink, neither of which is reconciled either. And =bin/sync-plans --check= solved the mirror-image problem for =plans/= — drift between a directory and a source of truth — so its shape is the precedent to read first.


* TODO check-org-dev-skill verifies org's symbols but not the module's, so a phantom function passed for weeks :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9ad0e497-cc43-4d7c-87a9-3b6262cb2d8a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-18 Fri 18:33]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Found 2026-09-18 while excising the =warp= alias. =.claude/skills/org-dev/SKILL.md= told readers that =claude-code-ide-org--wire-warp-tools= "re-runs automatically at startup — don't call it by hand". **No such function has ever existed.** =git log -S= across all history finds the string in exactly two commits: =a2a8c7c=, which introduced it, and =d5c9398=, which removed it. It is absent from the live =~/.config/doom/config.el= too.

*It arrived as prose, not as a rename.* =a2a8c7c= is "Migrate org/org-dev skills from zip archives to native SKILL.md format" — the name came out of the binary =org-dev.skill= archive that preceded plain text. The likeliest history is that it described a block in the maintainer's personal Doom config, which packaging later moved into the module as =claude-code-ide-org-standalone-wire= (=:ID:= e396f94a, =1caed585=), and the skill's prose was never updated. So it was outlived rather than replaced.

*The gap is specific and the fix is cheap.* =bin/check-org-dev-skill= already asserts that org's own facilities still exist — =org-add-archive-files=, =org-id-locations=, =org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= and five more — by name. It does **not** assert the same for the =claude-code-ide-org-*= symbols the skill names, which is the one category the skill is most likely to get wrong, because those are this project's own and churn fastest. An =fboundp= sweep over every =claude-code-ide-org-…= symbol appearing in backticks in =SKILL.md= is about as cheap a check as exists, and it would have caught this the day it landed.

*Worth extending to the org skill too*, and to the promoted references: they name module functions in prose constantly (=--grouping-heading-p=, =--container-heading-p=, =--trigger-auto-clock-in=, =--queue-file-drained-p=). One of those was already found wrong on 2026-09-18 — =config.el='s own comment said =--queue-drained-p=, which does not exist either (corrected in =ea6e603=). Two phantoms in one day, from two different files, is the argument for mechanising rather than re-reading.

*One caveat on scope, so the check does not become a nuisance:* a skill legitimately names *retired* functions when explaining history — =--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo= is gone on purpose and the state-transition rules say so. The check therefore wants the same treatment =check-conventions= gives cited =:ID:=s: assert the symbol resolves, unless the prose marks it as retired. Deciding that marker is the only design question here.


* MAYBE Deploy and test the plugin under Warp's agentic framework :research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       27e16e9a-5f71-402e-8db0-259bb8a0ad4c
:CREATED:  [2026-09-18 Fri 18:02]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Deferred 2026-09-18 by the maintainer: =.warp/.mcp.json= "currently has no consumers; it is a vestige of an early experiment", and there is no adequate Warp setup to take it further at this time. Filed =MAYBE= rather than dropped because the file and its Emacs-side machinery are still in the tree.

*What the deferral just changed.* The maintainer repointed =.warp/.mcp.json= from =/mcp/warp= to =/mcp/claude-code-ide-org=, so it now matches the root =.mcp.json= byte for byte. That resolves a review finding on PR #28 — the twin had been left naming the order-dependent alias — but by a different route than the reviewer assumed: not "Warp needs the alias", but "nothing needs it".

*Which leaves the alias with no consumer at all.* =config.el= line 17311 still runs =(claude-code-ide-mcp-server-register-session "warp" (car projects) nil)=, and the =defcustom= docstring justifies it as "the id the shipped =.mcp.json= URL (=/mcp/warp=) names, kept because that seam is the one verified against Warp's own agent". No shipped config names that URL any more. So the registration is now dead, and the question this heading really gates is whether it comes back or comes out.

*Five places still describe it as live*, found 2026-09-18 and left standing deliberately until this is decided — removing the alias and correcting its prose are one act, not two:

- =config.el= 17110 — a comment asserting the shipped =.mcp.json= names =/mcp/warp=
- =skills/org/references/org-emacs-setup.md= 77 — "the session id the shipped =/mcp/warp= URL names"
- =.claude/skills/org-dev/SKILL.md= 296 — "matching =.mcp.json='s =http://localhost:45571/mcp/warp="
- =config-test.el= 15895 — the same claim in a docstring; its fixture at 15861 legitimately uses the URL and needs no change
- =DOOM.md= 48 — an illustrative alternate port, stale rather than wrong

*If this is ever picked up*, the thing to establish first is whether Warp's project-scoped MCP support still works the way =6a6d5b4e= found it: approval-gated, session-scoped, re-toggled after a restart, and reading only =~/.warp/.mcp.json= and the project's =.warp/.mcp.json=. That investigation is the reason the file exists, and its real yield was not the proxy it chased but the =200=-versus-=202= defect in this project's own HTTP server.

*If it is not picked up*, the cheap close is to delete the alias registration, correct the four false claims, and decide whether =.warp/.mcp.json= stays as a zero-cost convention or goes with it. That is a bounded pass and does not need Warp to exist.


* TODO The plugin has never been tagged or version-bumped, and nothing would notice :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       827e1c42-708e-4f1d-bf25-d8b8b980b7ef
:CREATED:  [2026-09-18 Fri 17:18]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Asked for 2026-09-18: create a version tag, and find a way to keep the tag and the manifest version in step automatically.

*Where things stand, measured.* =.claude-plugin/plugin.json= says =0.1.0= and has said so since the manifest was written — four commits have touched that file and **none** bumped the version, across the packaging slice, =749301a0=, and =8bbae3aa='s thirteen members. The repo carries two tags, =archive/review-ui-fixes-presplit= and =archive/span-pipeline-planning=, neither version-shaped. So there is no release history at all, and =claude plugin list= cheerfully reports "Version: 0.1.0" to anyone who asks.

*Half the ask already exists, and it is better than expected.* =claude plugin tag= creates a ={name}--v{version}= tag **from the manifest**, validating that =plugin.json= and any enclosing marketplace entry agree, with =--dry-run=, =-m= (=%s= interpolates the version), =--push= and =--remote=. It refuses on a dirty tree or an existing tag unless forced. So tag *creation* needs no invention — the open question is narrower than it looked.

*What is actually missing is the discipline, which is exactly what this project's evidence says not to leave to memory.* Three failures, in order of likelihood:

- *The version is never bumped.* It has not been, once, through everything above.
- *A bump happens and no tag follows*, so the manifest claims a release that has no commit.
- *A tag exists and the manifest has moved on*, the same drift one direction over.

*Why it has not bitten yet, and why that is the trap rather than a reprieve.* Every consumer today loads the plugin **in place** — euchre by a relative symlink into a clone — and the docs are explicit that a plugin loaded in place, or from a =command= source, updates regardless of the version field. So the version is currently **inert**: wrong, visible, and consequence-free. It starts mattering the moment anything is installed from a marketplace, at which point "users only receive updates when you bump this field" makes a stale =0.1.0= a silent refusal to ship updates.

*Candidate mechanisms, weighted.* The shape that fits this repo's precedent is a **check that refuses**, not a reminder:

- =.githooks/pre-push= already refuses a push while the =plans/= archive is stale, so the pattern and the file both exist. A version-versus-tag assertion there would fire at the one moment that matters — sharing.
- =bin/check-conventions= is the other candidate and is weaker for this: it runs pre-commit on a narrow staged set, and a version bump is not a =.org= or rules edit.
- =claude plugin tag --dry-run= is the validator to call rather than reimplement; it already knows how to compare the manifest against a marketplace entry, which a hand-rolled =git tag -l= grep would not.

*What has to be decided rather than assumed.* **When a bump is due** — every merged slice, every PR, or only at a deliberate release — because a check can only enforce a rule that exists. The slice/PR cadence is the one this project actually has, and =8bbae3aa= merging would be the natural first bump. And **what the first tag should be**: =0.1.0= against today's HEAD records a release that is not what =0.1.0= ever described, while bumping first and tagging that is honest but discards the history the tag was supposed to give.

*One thing to verify rather than assume when this is worked:* whether =claude plugin tag= objects to this repo having no marketplace entry at all. Its validation is described as covering "any enclosing marketplace entry", which reads as tolerant of none — but that is a reading, not a test, and =--dry-run= settles it in one command.


* TODO The conventions never say how stories and slices compose, and "not declared" reads as a ban on composing one :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0b5cacf5-9299-499d-9036-2da2625c1233
:CREATED:  [2026-09-18 Fri 11:39]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-09-18, after an exposition on slices and stories that they suspected ran afoul of the governing documents. Checked: two parts were already documented, one sentence genuinely misleads, and three ideas are absent entirely.

*The misleading sentence.* =org-conventions.md= says a story "is not declared, it is emergent... **Don't classify a heading as one when writing it**", and =CLAUDE.md= compresses that to "detected and never declared". Strictly this is about *labelling* — there is no =:KIND: story=, and a story is detected from its keyworded children by =--container-heading-p=. Naturally read, it forbids *composing* a task together with its subtasks, which is ordinary and fine. It misled a session on 2026-09-18 into arguing that a deliberate parent-with-subtasks shape was disallowed and that a slice was therefore the only option. The fix is to separate *detection* from *composition* in the wording, not to change the practice.

*Three ideas absent from every governing document*, each stated by the user and none written down:

- *A well-composed story brings all of its subtasks into a slice*, and a slice that closes several whole stories is the highest expression of the two concepts working together. The conventions define both groupings on an emergent-vs-declared axis and never say how they compose.
- *Partial stories inside slices are historical*, not a pattern to copy: the story work predates the slice mechanism. Without this a reader infers that partial adoption is the intended shape.
- *The lifecycle, which is the load-bearing omission.* A task — story or not — first exists **outside** a slice, and a slice **promotes** it from the backlog when completing it has been prioritized. So slice membership *means* "this is prioritized for near-term completion". Grepped 2026-09-18: nothing in =org-conventions.md=, =CLAUDE.md= or the org skill describes promotion from a backlog. The only documented way into a slice is adoption-on-discovery, which is the mid-flight exception rather than the ordinary path — so the conventions currently describe the exception and omit the rule.

*Two things checked and found already correct*, recorded so they are not re-opened: deferral by deleting a member's checkbox cookie while keeping its reference line is documented (the dropping section, and "A cancelled or deferred member has its checkbox cookie deleted"), and the dual-membership guard is documented ("Check no other slice already counts it... a discovery already claimed elsewhere is not adopted, it is cross-referenced"). The user's deferral exception is *consistent* with that guard rather than in tension with it: dropping the checkbox is exactly what stops two cookies counting the same work.

*Why this is ordinary rather than a defect in the framework, in the user's own framing:* "this framework is a means, not an end in and of itself. As such it has also served as a means of improving itself." The vocabulary was arrived at by working the system, and it sharpens by being wrestled with — this heading is an instance of that, not an exception to it.

Proposed: revise the story section of =org-conventions.md= to split detection from composition, add the story/slice composition rule and the backlog-promotion lifecycle, and note the historical reason partial stories exist. =CLAUDE.md='s one-screen map repeats "detected and never declared" and needs the same correction. Re-run =bin/claude-org-setup= and =bin/check-conventions=, since the promoted copies are generated.

Not a member of =8bbae3aa=: that slice's proximate goal is making the plugin usable in another project, and this is convention refinement that can wait for a slice of its own.

*Measured 2026-09-18, and it inverts the premise the conventions rest on.* Of the six live groupings in =TODO.org= — level-1 headings with keyworded children — **five carry no CLOCK line of their own**, meaning they never worked as leaves and so never accidentally acquired children. They were *composed*. Only one (=a1d63f52=, 9 own CLOCK lines) is the accidental shape the conventions describe.

=CLAUDE.md='s account — "each was clocked honestly while it was still a leaf, and became a grouping later by acquiring children or by a refile" — is true of the *historical* corpus it was written against, and is now the minority case. The prose describes a world the practice has left. (Caveat: this counted level-1 headings in =TODO.org= only, by a crude scan; =DONE.org= was not included, and the nine-groupings figure in =CLAUDE.md= presumably spans both.)

*So deliberate composition is the norm, which is the evidence behind every gap above* — the conventions read as though a story happens to you.

*And it raises a property the user offered: =:KIND: story=.* Better founded than it first looks, but conditional, and the condition is a consumer rather than a preference. The project has retired declared state that nothing read — =PLANNING= went for exactly that, 7 uses in 432 transitions.

The asymmetry to preserve: a **slice** must be declared because its members are *links*, so there is nothing to detect and declaration is the only option. A **story's** children are structurally present and already detected by =--container-heading-p=, so a label is a *second* source of truth that can disagree with the tree — marked with no children, or children with no mark. That asymmetry is why the two differ today and it is a real reason, not an oversight.

Which makes the decision clean: **=:KIND: story= earns its place if and only if the "well-composed" lint is wanted** — *does this story have all of its subtasks in one slice?* That question cannot be asked of a heading that merely happens to have children; it can only be asked of one someone asserted was a story. With that check the property is load-bearing. Without it, it is a label with no reader, which is the thing this project deletes.

*Separately, and not a gap:* there is **no rule requiring a story's members to share a =:CATEGORY:=**. Checked 2026-09-18 — no convention text, and =bin/lint-org='s only category rule is "level-1 task has no =:CATEGORY:=" (=config.el= 14654), which never looks at children. =org_capture= makes =category= "optional under an =:ID:= target", i.e. explicitly permitted on a child, and one override already exists in the corpus (a =Tools= child under a =Meta= parent). Inheritance is already a default rather than a constraint. Worth *stating* in the revision only because the absence of a rule reads as a prohibition — which is how this came up.

*Amended 2026-09-19 against the drift analysis and the slice =f6d160c2=.* The proposal above adds three ideas to the conventions and corrects one sentence in CLAUDE.md; where each lands is now a constraint rather than a choice. The three additions go in =org-conventions.md=, which is path-scoped — and, per [[id:436e0991-c8ac-40fb-860d-a6c4c381bee5][436e0991]], loads only on a Read of an =.org= file, so the practice they describe must also survive their not being loaded (the tools' own refusals are the only rules no session has missed). The CLAUDE.md half is a *net deletion*: a one-screen compression that misled a session is evidence the map should point at the conventions rather than paraphrase them, so the fix removes "detected and never declared" instead of expanding it. Acceptance: the always-loaded line count ([[id:bf7e0b8e-f83a-4d4a-9950-3ab23a225156][bf7e0b8e]], 1,573 today) does not rise from this change, and =check-conventions= passes after re-promotion.


* TODO Let a task-only install auto-apply state transitions, behind a toggle :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2cd74268-5f54-4ac1-9fc7-2ef01e5f3edd
:CREATED:  [2026-09-18 Fri 09:03]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-09-18, while =8bbae3aa= was taking time tracking out of what ships: "if state transitions are the only thing that comes through when time tracking is disabled then review seems unnecessary."

*The premise is half right, and the half that is wrong is the interesting one.* Review cannot simply go away: the queue is load-bearing for three reasons that have nothing to do with time. Org's state-change logging breaks when driven naively — =org-add-log-note= =pop-to-buffer=s =*Org Note*= *before* it checks whether a note is wanted, so every keyword carrying a logging cookie defers through it (=3d576d29=). =org_amend= and =org_capture= defer into the queue when the human's buffer is dirty, and the queue is where that text lives. And =--pending-capture= is what lets a deferred capture's =:ID:= resolve before its heading exists (=b5f94b88=). All three survive the cut.

*But the mechanism is not what needs a human — the judgement is.* =--review-apply-state= already performs the whole non-interactive sequence in code: shadow =org-current-effective-time= (let-binding =org-log-note-effective-time= is futile, =org-add-log-setup= setqs it), clear =org-log-note-marker= *before* =org-todo=, =remove-hook= the deferred =org-add-log-note=, then drive =org-store-log-note= against an empty =*Org Note*=. Its own comments note it runs under batch. So "apply is human-triggered" is a statement about who decides, not about what org can do.

*Which matters now because the judgement content collapses with time tracking off.* What a human actually adjudicates today is span attribution — which heading a stretch of guideposts belongs to, whether an overnight interval was work. Those items do not exist in a task-only install.

*The shape the user wants, corrected 2026-09-18 from an earlier reading of this heading as a plain toggle.* Human-in-the-loop is the **debug mode**, and it is also the **default on first deployment**. Automatic is *earned* by observation, not chosen — and the earning is **invalidated by any change to the apply code path**, which forces a return to supervised mode in production for long enough to re-establish confidence. Only applies where time tracking is disabled.

That is a ratchet with a reset condition rather than a preference, and it needs two mechanisms doing different jobs:

- *The reset — a digest.* A checksum over the apply path, stored alongside the value confidence was established at, compared at apply time. Different means the code is no longer what was approved, so supervised mode regardless of the setting. A digest answers "is this the same thing I approved", never "what changed" — that is all a reset trigger needs. Two places: a **runtime guard** in the apply path (protects the consumer) and a **pin** in =bin/check-conventions= (fails *us* at commit when the digest moves and the confirmed value does not, making a reset a decision rather than an accident).
- *The graduation — something log-shaped.* "Observed enough" needs a unit. Elapsed time is the loosest reading and the easiest to satisfy vacuously; a count of applications that landed and were not corrected measures the thing confidence is actually about. The queue is already log-shaped and the =.applied= watermarks already mark passes, so the raw material may largely exist.

*What the digest covers is the design problem, not the hashing.* Everything lives in one 17,769-line =config.el=, so a file digest is useless — it would change on nearly every commit. The apply path is about eleven =defun=s, and =--append-to-drawer= is shared across four call sites in four sections, only two of them apply. Drawer writing is where both of this project's data-loss incidents lived (=ba8249c1=, =b74e0f19=, per =c084553c=), so it is the worst possible thing for a digest to miss. A =config.el= split along its 45 existing =;;;= sections would turn the enumeration into a boundary — new functions included automatically instead of needing to be listed — which is a real argument, though not on its own a reason to restructure.

*And one boundary no file arrangement reaches:* =--review-apply-state= calls =org-todo= and =org-store-log-note= directly, so an org upgrade changes behaviour with nothing of ours changing. =3d576d29= hit exactly that class when a live trigger hook swallowed a note that batch had written fine.

Open questions:

- *What happens to a transition org refuses.* =b693c444= established that apply must verify the transition landed before consuming its event; auto-apply has no human to re-offer it to, so a refusal needs somewhere to go.
- *Whether captures and amends follow.* A deferred amend exists precisely because a human was mid-edit, so auto-applying into a dirty buffer is the collision the deferral avoids. Likely they stay queued even in this mode, which makes the toggle narrower than "no review".
- *Whether the =@= keywords participate.* =WAITING= and =CANCELLED= prompt for note text; auto-apply would supply the queued =note=, which is what =--review-apply-state= already does — so probably yes, but the agent's note becomes the permanent record with no human pass over it.
- *Whether this is wanted at all.* =d3149a81= should report first: if a task-only review buffer is merely short rather than burdensome, the ratchet is machinery in search of a problem, and this project's precedent is observation before machinery.

Not a member of =8bbae3aa= and not prioritized: this is backlog, to be promoted into a future slice when completing it matters. The subtasks named above — the digest reset, the graduation threshold, the =config.el= split — belong under this heading rather than beside it.

*The gating question is answered, 2026-09-19, and the answer is the opposite of what this body predicted.* "Whether this is wanted at all" deferred to =d3149a81=, which closed having measured **correctness** — that a guidepost-free queue yields the right items and drains — and never measured *burden*. So the question had no owner. Measured now, across all 74 queue files in =~/.claude/org-updates/= and its =archive/=:

: task events (todo/capture/amend)   970
: time events (guideposts, clocks)  4287

Task events map **1:1 to review items** — the builder pushes one proposal per event, deliberately, so two amendments to one heading stay separately acceptable — while thousands of guideposts collapse into a handful of spans. So items per pass is a direct count:

: sessions with zero task items    40 of 74
: of those with any, median         16
: mean                              28.5
: heaviest                          96

*The burden is bimodal, not small.* Most sessions would offer nothing; a working session offers a median of 16 discrete accept/reject decisions and routinely 30–96. This body's guess — "if a task-only review buffer is merely short rather than burdensome, the ratchet is machinery in search of a problem" — does not survive its own measurement. **The ratchet is wanted.**

*Two limits on what that measures, both cutting the same way.* It counts items, not minutes: a keyword change is a faster judgement than attributing a span, so 16 task items is less work than 16 mixed ones. And it is measured on *this* repo, the most active org consumer that will ever exist; a typical consumer's sessions likely sit in the 40-of-74 bucket. Neither rescues the 96-item case.

*Scope corrected at the same time, and this heading is smaller than it looked.* The =digest= half does **not** belong here: =f12f9da4= — "The running Emacs can be running a stale =config.el= and nothing says so", an open member of =8a2eb687= — is already its home, and building it here would have made a twin. And the =config.el= split is filed standalone, because its strongest motivation is the time/task seam (5405 lines, 30% of the module) and it outlives this heading being cancelled.

*What remains here is two things*: the toggle itself, and what "observed enough" counts. Not divided, deliberately — at two subtasks the parent would be a container holding almost nothing, and the reset it depends on lives elsewhere.


* TODO Survey what has landed since August that the statusline should be using and is not :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       08d76f6a-9f4d-4a41-a2c6-05e96115237d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 16:24]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

The user's broader point 2026-09-17: the statusline "may deserve revisiting — we have not touched it in quite some time, and work in the interim may present opportunities to improve it."

*The interval is real.* Last substantive change =bc8696a= 2026-08-18, pointing it at the queue instead of the live clock; last rendering change =5c17021= 2026-08-04. Everything the project built after that — slices, spans and runs, the grouping predicate, the project dimension on guideposts, the blocker validation, the star notation — arrived without anyone asking what the statusline should show.

*Deliberately a survey, with a bounded output: a list of candidates and a recommendation, not an implementation.* The two findings that already have headings (=1c7051da= the misleading label, =d88362e7=-adjacent presentation) came out of one incident in one turn, which is weak evidence that a deliberate pass would find more — and is exactly why the pass should be a *survey* rather than an open-ended rewrite.

Candidates to consider, none argued for yet: whether a grouping should render differently from a leaf (=--grouping-heading-p= exists); whether a blocked heading should say so; whether the subtree total is the right number now that spans and runs exist and a =DOING= heading normally carries no running clock; whether the slice a heading belongs to is worth a field; and whether the =cwd= dimension matters on a line that is per-session but reads as per-project.

**Do not let this become a container for every statusline idea** — the lesson from =2a6a1355= is that one heading carrying several causes closes none of them. Each candidate the survey endorses gets its own heading.

*The survey gained its first hard data 2026-09-17, and it is larger than expected: the statusLine payload is rich and this repo reads exactly one field of it.* =--statusline-model-name= takes =model.display_name= and nothing else. The captured payload also carries:

| field | example |
|---+---|
| =session_name= | =span-owner-edges= |
| =context_window.used_percentage= / =remaining_percentage= | 22 / 78 |
| =exceeds_200k_tokens= | =t= |
| =rate_limits.five_hour.used_percentage= | 13 |
| =rate_limits.seven_day.used_percentage= | 40 |
| =cost.total_cost_usd=, =total_duration_ms=, =total_lines_added/removed= | — |
| =prompt_cache.hit_ratio=, =warm=, =ttl=, =expires_at= | 0.997, =t=, 1h |
| =workspace.repo.host/owner/name= | github.com / nbest937 / claude-code-ide-org.el |
| =effort.level=, =fast_mode=, =thinking.enabled= | high, nil, t |
| =scratchpad_dir=, =prompt_id=, =version=, =output_style.name= | — |

*And the survey's answer to most of that should be no, which is the useful part.* The user's constraint, stated the same day, is that the line has **one job** — "remind me what we are working on right now" — and that everything else is spending characters the headline should have. Nearly every field above is *interesting* and none of it is that. Recording them here is inventory, **not a backlog**: the discipline this heading was filed with was "do not let this become a container for every statusline idea", and a newly discovered buffet is exactly the pressure that rule exists to resist.

*Two that might survive the test, offered without endorsement.* =session_name= is a candidate because it names the current work at a coarser grain than the heading does, so it is arguably the same job rather than a different one. And =context_window.remaining_percentage= is the one field that changes what the user would *do next* — but it is a property of the session, not of the work, and the line is about the work.

*What the payload does **not** carry is the finding that actually blocks =63b84fe2=:* no terminal width, under any key.


* TODO Render the statusline like end matter: id first, then keyword, then title :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       63b84fe2-0066-4375-a588-60197956bd44
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 16:24]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:BLOCKER:  08d76f6a-9f4d-4a41-a2c6-05e96115237d
:END:

Requested by the user 2026-09-17: move the =:ID:= prefix to the front "to replicate the footnote presentation". The format string has not changed since =5c17021= on 2026-08-04, 44 days.

Today: =| Bulk citation owes more entri… [13aed7ca] (clocked in, 0:00 total)=

The end matter's argument applies here unchanged and was never carried across: **the id leads because it is what the eye scans for**, the keyword is second because it is the volatile field, and the title trails. The statusline puts the id in the middle, in brackets, after a truncated title — the exact inversion. One reader reading two surfaces should not have to re-orient between them.

*And the statusline can do something the footnote hook cannot: star a queued state correctly.* =footnote-check= is plain shell that greps the org files and knows nothing of the queue, so it emits the on-disk keyword by design. =--statusline-queue-state= already **reads the queue**, so it holds both the applied keyword and the pending one and can render =NEXT*= from first-hand knowledge. The star notation (=d88362e7=) therefore has a more natural home here than anywhere it has been applied so far.

Shape to aim at, not settled: =| 13aed7ca  NEXT*  Bulk citation owes more entri…  0:00=

Note the keyword is *absent* from the line today, so this is an addition rather than a move. Interacts with =1c7051da=, which is about what the third field should say once "clocked in" stops claiming a clock; do them together or sequence them deliberately, since both rewrite the same format string.

*Blocked on =08d76f6a= deliberately, and the blocker is a sequencing judgement rather than a dependency — say so if you would rather have the id move now and take the rework.* Three pending changes rewrite the same =format= call: this one, =1c7051da='s relabelling of the third field, and whatever the survey endorses. Landing them one at a time means rewriting one line three times and re-deciding the column layout each time, which is how a display string acquires an arbitrary shape nobody chose. The survey is cheap by construction — it outputs a list and a recommendation, not an implementation — so it is the right thing to put first.

The cost of being wrong here is small and worth naming: if the survey finds nothing, this change was delayed for one bounded pass. If it finds two more fields, the layout is decided once.

*Sharpened by the user 2026-09-17, and it reframes the whole heading:* "even saying 'clocked in' or anything in the statusline may be superfluous. I just want it to remind me what we are working on *right now*. I would rather see more of the headline at that moment."

So the line has **one job** — name the current work — and every other field is spending characters that the headline should have. That is a stronger claim than "reorder the fields", and it makes the chrome negotiable rather than given.

*The mechanical finding that matters, because dropping chrome alone buys nothing:* the title is truncated at a **hardcoded 30 characters** — =(if (> (length name) 30) (concat (substring name 0 29) "…") name)= — not at a width derived from the terminal or from what the rest of the line leaves over. Free up every other field and the headline still stops at 30. **The constant is the limiter; the chrome is only the excuse for it.** Any version of this change that does not derive the budget will look like it did nothing.

Rough budget on an 80-column terminal, model name "Opus 5" at 6: chrome today is about 39 characters (=" | "=, the bracketed id, and ="(clocked in, 0:00 total)"=) against 30 of title. Leading with a bare id and dropping the parenthetical leaves roughly 61 for the title — double — and about 54 if the keyword stays.

*One tension to settle rather than decide silently.* The earlier ask was to replicate the end matter, which puts the **keyword** second. The end matter's reason for it is that "misalignment is how a stale keyword goes unnoticed" — an argument about a *column of entries*, which a one-line statusline does not have. So the keyword's justification is weaker here than it is there, and it costs 4–8 characters of headline. Keep it, or drop it and let the star alone carry the pending signal? Not decided here.

*Three constraints and questions from the user 2026-09-17.*

**No alignment in the statusline.** Do not pad the keyword to a column or align anything else. The end matter aligns because it is a *column of entries* read against each other; a single line has nothing to align against, and padding there only spends headline characters to make one field start at a fixed offset nobody is scanning. This also removes the last argument for carrying the keyword at all — see the open question above — since alignment was the reason the end matter gives for it.

**Open: does the statusLine payload carry terminal dimensions?** It matters directly, because the finding above is that the hardcoded 30 has to become a *derived* budget, and deriving it needs a width. =--statusline-model-name= reads only =model.display_name= from the payload, so the rest of the schema has never been looked at here. =claude_debug.log= records the invocation ("StatusLine [...] completed with status 0") but not the payload. A temporary probe was installed in =bin/statusline.sh= on 2026-09-17 to capture one payload and answer this empirically — **revert it once read**. If no width is passed, the fallbacks are =(window-width)= inside Emacs, =$COLUMNS=, or a conservative fixed budget, and the choice should be made on evidence rather than assumed.

**Open: does the desktop app render a statusLine at all?** The user has the desktop app open alongside, sees the exchange echoed live, and *no statusline*. This repo wires it in =.claude/settings.json= as a =command= type and the plugin surface ships none, so the wiring is project-scoped and identical for any client that reads that file. Whether a given client *renders* the result is a client question this repo cannot answer from its own artifacts — do not assume it does. If the desktop app does not, that bounds the value of every improvement here to the terminal, which is worth knowing *before* the work rather than after.

*Answered by probe 2026-09-17: **the statusLine payload carries no terminal dimensions**.* The captured payload has 50-odd keys — =session_id=, =transcript_path=, =cwd=, =scratchpad_dir=, =prompt_id=, =effort=, =session_name=, =model=, =workspace= (with =repo= host/owner/name), =version=, =output_style=, =cost=, =context_window=, =prompt_cache=, =fast_mode=, =thinking=, =rate_limits= — and **not one of =columns=, =width=, =lines= or =terminal=**. So deriving the budget from the payload is off the table.

*The user then asked the right follow-up: aren't the dimensions in the calling shell's environment?* Preliminary evidence says no, and it is worth stating why rather than just concluding. Measured in a sibling process spawned the same way: =COLUMNS=0= — **set but zero**, which is worse than unset because a naive =${COLUMNS:-80}= silently yields 0; stdout is *not* a tty, so =tput cols= has nothing to interrogate and falls back to terminfo's default; and =/dev/tty= reports "device not configured", meaning the process has **no controlling terminal** and =stty size </dev/tty= cannot work either. =TERM= is set (=xterm-256color=) but names a terminal *type*, never its current size.

That is consistent with how the hook is run — Claude Code spawns it and captures stdout, so the pipe is the point. A confirming probe inside =bin/statusline.sh= itself is installed and reports on the next render; **do not treat the above as final until it does**, since a sibling process is evidence about the shape of the environment, not proof about this one.

*If it confirms, the remaining options are all approximations and should be labelled as such:* =(window-width)= inside Emacs (wrong — it measures an Emacs window, not the terminal running Claude Code); a =defcustom= the user sets once to their actual width; or a fixed conservative budget. **A =defcustom= is the honest one** — it is the only option that is *correct* rather than a guess, and the cost is one line of config for a value that changes rarely.

*Corrected 2026-09-17 by the confirming probe: **the width is available after all**, and the preliminary reading above was wrong.* The user asked whether terminal dimensions are in the calling shell's environment. Measured inside =bin/statusline.sh= itself:

: COLUMNS=[108]  LINES=[92]  TERM=[xterm-256color]
: stdin tty: no   stdout tty: no   stderr tty: no
: tput cols: 108

**=COLUMNS= is set and correct, and =tput cols= agrees.** The earlier =COLUMNS=0= came from a *sibling* process spawned differently, and generalising from it was the error — which is exactly why that paragraph said not to treat it as final until this probe ran. Retain the hedge, not the conclusion: the =defcustom= is **no longer the honest option**, because the environment carries the true value and a hand-set constant would go stale on every window resize.

=/dev/tty= is readable but =stty size= fails with "Device not configured", and no descriptor is a tty, so =COLUMNS= is the signal to use — not =stty=, not a tty probe.

*Warp specifically carries nothing extra.* The user noted we are in Warp and it may have a native mechanism. Its variables are =WARP_TERMINAL_SESSION_UUID=, =WARP_HONOR_PS1=, =WARP_IS_LOCAL_SHELL_SESSION=, =WARP_CLI_AGENT_PROTOCOL_VERSION=, =TERM_PROGRAM=WarpTerminal= and a version — all identity and configuration, **none dimensional**. So the portable answer and the Warp answer are the same one.

*One design consequence, and it bends a convention slightly.* =COLUMNS= belongs to the *shell*; the Emacs process behind =emacsclient= does not have it. So =bin/statusline.sh= — whose stated job is to shuttle bytes and hold no logic — has to pass the width into the elisp call. That is passing an *environment fact* rather than adding logic, which is defensible under "maximum Emacs, minimal shell", but it should be a deliberate note in the script rather than an unremarked argument.


* TODO The statusline says "clocked in" when it means "agent mid-turn", and the heading is merely the last one named :code:observed:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1c7051da-c020-41e1-b843-8cfc68f53e38
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 16:21]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Observed 2026-09-17: the user read =| Bulk citation owes more entri… [13aed7ca] (clocked in, 0:00 total)= as meaning that heading was clocked, and asked whether work was being booked against it. Nothing was. The queue's last clock event was =clock_out 2a6a1355=; =13aed7ca='s only event was a queued =todo NEXT=.

*The function is behaving as documented, which is why this is a naming defect rather than a bug.* =--statusline-queue-state= returns =(ID . RUNNING-P)= where ID is "the heading named by the newest event that names one -- a =todo= or =clock_in=" and RUNNING-P is "non-nil when the newest guidepost is a =resume=". So the two halves answer *different questions*: the id says what was last named, the flag says whether the agent is mid-turn. Neither is about a clock, and =--statusline-task-string= then renders the flag as the literal words "clocked in".

*Why it misleads in exactly the worst case.* Post-cutover a =DOING= heading normally has no running clock, so a reader cannot use "is a clock actually open" as a sanity check — the statusline is the only clock-ish signal on screen. And the id half moves on a mere =org_set_todo=, so setting =NEXT= on a heading nobody is working retargets the line to it. =0:00 total= is honest and is the one part that hints something is off.

*Candidate fixes, none chosen.* Relabel to what it means ("working", "mid-turn", or a bare =▸=); or split the two facts, since conflating them is the actual error; or make the id half prefer the newest =clock_in= over the newest =todo=, so a state change alone cannot retarget it. The third is the smallest and would have prevented this instance outright.

*Probably superseded rather than fixable, 2026-09-17.* The user's follow-on — "even saying 'clocked in' or anything in the statusline may be superfluous" — points at deleting the field rather than relabelling it. If that lands, **this defect ceases to exist instead of being repaired**, the way the marker modes on =2a6a1355= were retired with the markers rather than fixed.

Left =TODO= deliberately, not cancelled: the field's removal is still a proposal on =63b84fe2=, and cancelling on an intention is how a real defect gets lost when the intention changes. *If the status field survives in any form, the misleading label is still live and this heading still applies.* If it is deleted, close this as CANCELLED citing that commit, and do not spend a line relabelling something on its way out.

The analysis above stays useful either way, because it is the record of *why* the field misleads — which is the argument for deleting it, not merely for renaming it.

Divided out of =2a6a1355= 2026-09-17 at the user's direction, whose diagnosis was that the body had become "out and out metastasism": one heading carrying several causes that were never separated, so each visit appended to the pile instead of landing somewhere of its own. Ten dated revision sections in seventeen days, 3,707 words, the largest heading in TODO.org at fifteen times the median and 4.7% of the file's prose. **The size was the symptom; the failure to separate causes was the disease.**

*What the division claims, and it is deliberately a weak claim.* Two causes are filed as members because the evidence separates them cleanly: an id that arrives by *transcription* was never chosen by the writer, and an id cited in *bulk* was chosen by the writer every time. The parent heading's own unifying sentence — "every failure is an id the writer did not choose to write" — covers the first and is **false of the second**, which is what makes them two rather than one. That contradiction is the whole warrant for dividing.

*What it does not claim.* That two is the right number. Three of the five recorded modes were marker faults and are dead, retired with the markers themselves rather than fixed, so they are not causes and get no member. Whether quotation and borrowed prose are one cause or two is left open on the member that holds them. **The user is reserving judgment on the division itself until the next session**, so this structure is a proposal to be judged, not a settled taxonomy.

*Why the child's body was left intact.* Condensing its retracted third would have been policy-conformant and was declined: the accreted sections are the evidence of where the seams are, and the next pass needs to count them. Erasing them to save lines would destroy the only record of how the causes got tangled.

*Asked by the user 2026-09-01 after a third stop-hook refusal in one session:*
"check for others this session (same or different?)". *Different — all three.*

| # | id refused | what the id was doing |
|---+------------+-----------------------|
| 1 | =72463b68= | superscript ⁴ was reused for two ids; the marker existed, the footnote line was another heading's |
| 2 | eleven at once | enumerated in prose lists and a table — "phase 4 has =c60a1c53=, then =5f1068f9=/=33864a0f=" |
| 3 | =b36e6369= | inside a *quoted example* — ="add X to b36e6369"=, illustrating what a commit subject looks like |

*None of these is either recorded mode.*
[[id:d5490814-d174-480a-a9e7-b809f94d71f2][d5490814]] is *id-as-vocabulary*: the
token has become the name of a lesson, so it stops reading as a citation.
[[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]] is *decay on re-mention*:
cited correctly the first time, abbreviated after. In all three cases here the
id was neither a name nor a re-mention.

*The unifying observation, and it is sharper than "be careful".* The hook's test
is *does the response contain this token*. The writer's test is *am I referring
to a heading here*. Those agree only while an id appears **because** it refers.
They diverge whenever it appears for some other reason:

- *bookkeeping* — the id was cited, and the failure is in the numbering rather
  than the recognition (case 1). This is the one where the intent was entirely
  correct;
- *enumeration* — the id is an item in a list of work, where the eye reads a row
  rather than a reference (case 2);
- *quotation* — the id belongs to a string being *shown*, not to a sentence
  being *written* (case 3).

*So the three recorded modes now cover recognition, decay, and
reference-versus-appearance*, and only the last has a mechanical smell to it:
the hook already knows every id in the response and every footnote marker, so it
could say *which* marker is duplicated or missing rather than only which id is
unfootnoted. Case 1 would have been a one-line fix at the moment it happened.

*Worth noting what did not fail.* Every response carrying ids in *ordinary
prose* passed. The rule is not hard to follow when the id is doing the job the
rule was written for.

*Open: whether any of this is worth mechanising.* Against — the hook already
catches every instance, so nothing reaches the user, and three refusals in a
session of roughly forty responses is a low rate for a check whose whole purpose
is to be the backstop. For — case 1 is a *numbering* error the hook could
diagnose precisely, and cases 2 and 3 suggest the writer's attention is on the
sentence rather than the token, which no amount of care fixes. Deciding that is
this heading's job; it should not be assumed either way.

*A fourth mode, found 2026-09-01 immediately after filing the first three, and
it is the only one the hook cannot see.*

A response footnoted [[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]] as
"Footnote discipline decays on re-mention, not on first citation". Its actual
title is *"[1/1] Footnote discipline decays on re-mention; enforce it at Stop"*.
The wrong version was reconstructed from a *paraphrase inside another heading's
body* — [[id:d5490814-d174-480a-a9e7-b809f94d71f2][d5490814]] describes it as
"footnote discipline decaying on *re-mention*, where every offender was correct
the first time" — which reads exactly like a title and is not one.

*The hook passed it.* =footnote-check= tests whether each cited id *has* a
footnote, not whether the footnote is *right*. So this mode produces a
confidently-wrong citation that no mechanism refuses — the opposite of the other
three, where the failure is loud and the response is simply rejected.

*Which makes it the more serious of the four, on this project's own reasoning.*
A refused response costs a retry. A footnote with a plausible wrong title is
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s shape — a value that is
harder to reject than an absence — and it propagates: the next reader has no
reason to doubt it, and the footnote exists precisely for readers who cannot see
the heading.

*The instruction already anticipates this and says so in as many words* — the
standing rule is that each footnote carries the id's "*exact, full title*,
looked up rather than recalled", and that "a remembered title is where
paraphrase creeps back in". What this instance adds is that *a paraphrase in a
sibling heading's body is indistinguishable from a lookup*, because it arrives
as prose about that heading and in this project's own voice. Recalling from
memory at least feels like recall; reading a neighbour's summary feels like
reading the source.

*So the mechanisable half moves.* The first three modes argue for better marker
bookkeeping in the hook. This one argues for something different and cheaper:
the hook already resolves every cited id to check it is tracked, so it *has* the
real title and could compare it against the footnote line. That would turn the
only silent mode of the four into a loud one.

*A fifth mode, 2026-09-01: the id arrives inside borrowed prose.* A response
compressed [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]'s own sentence
— "Since =226ed53b= the gap threshold governs only grouping and display" — into
its own words, and =226ed53b= came along as part of the transcription rather
than as a citation anyone chose to make. The hook refused it, twice in one
response.

*Distinct from the quotation mode above, and worse.* That one was an id inside a
*quoted example string*, visibly not the writer's own sentence. This one reads
as the writer's prose, because it is — only a fragment of it was copied. Nothing
on the surface marks the borrowed span, so there is no cue to check.

*Which makes the pattern across all five statable, and it is sharper than any of
them individually.* Every failure is an id the writer *did not choose to write*:
marker bookkeeping, list enumeration, quoted example, a title paraphrased from a
neighbour's body, and now a sentence adapted from one. The ids that are
deliberately cited get footnoted every time — that half of the discipline has
never failed.

*So the rule the writer is actually following is "footnote what I decided to
reference", and the hook's rule is "footnote every id present".* The gap between
those two is exactly the set of ids that arrive by transcription. That is a more
useful statement of the defect than five separate modes, and it suggests the
check to want is not better discipline but a diff: the hook already knows every
id in the response and every id in the footnote list.

*Asked 2026-09-01: could footnoting be done by a subagent? It should not, and
the reason is that most of it is already done by a script.*

*=bin/hooks/footnote-check= already performs every step but the last.* It
extracts the =\b[0-9a-f]{8}\b= candidates from the response, filters them by
grepping =^:ID: +$id= across TODO.org and DONE.org — so it *has located the
heading* — and checks which are missing from the footnote block. What it never
does is read the *title* sitting a few lines above the =:ID:= line it just
matched.

*Which makes the fix small and the model unnecessary.* Emitting id, current
keyword and exact title for each unfootnoted citation would make the fourth mode
— a title reconstructed from a neighbour's paraphrase — *structurally
impossible*, because the correct string would be on screen at the moment the
footnote is written. That is the only one of the five modes the hook cannot
currently catch, and it is the only one that produces a confidently wrong answer
rather than a rejected response.

*A subagent would be the wrong instrument for three reasons.* The task is a
lookup, not a judgement — there is nothing to weigh. It would be
non-deterministic where the answer is exact. And it could *paraphrase*, which is
precisely the failure being fixed; a =grep= cannot.

*The real constraint is what a hook may do, and it is worth stating because it
bounds every version of this.* A =Stop= hook can refuse and inform; it cannot
rewrite the response. So no mechanism here can *write* the footnotes — it can
only hand over the exact lines. That still removes two of the three error
classes: detection (the hook already sees every id) and lookup (it would now
carry the title). What remains is *placement* — the superscript markers — which
is where the marker-collision mode lives and which nothing but care can fix.

*So the honest scope: two of five modes become impossible, one becomes loud, and
two stay.* Enumeration and borrowed prose are already caught by the existing
check; the marker collision stays a hand error; the paraphrased title stops
being possible. That is a good return on adding one field to a message the hook
already prints.

*Refined 2026-09-01: not done by a subagent — assisted by one?* The distinction
is real, so the question is what a model could add that a script cannot.

*Almost nothing, on inspection.* Detection, lookup, presence-checking, ordering
by first appearance and comparing a written footnote against the true title are
all exact operations, and =footnote-check= already performs the first three. A
model would be slower, non-deterministic, and able to paraphrase — the failure
being fixed.

*One candidate is genuinely model-shaped, and it is the inverse of the rule the
hook enforces.* The hook asks "is every id present also footnoted". It cannot
ask "was every heading *discussed* also cited" — a response can describe a
heading at length, by subject rather than by id, and owe a footnote nobody can
detect by scanning for tokens. That is a judgement about aboutness, which is
exactly what a regex cannot do.

*Measured before proposing it, and the result is a clean negative.* Across *314
assistant responses* in this session, scanning for a heading whose four-word
title phrase appears while its id does not: *zero*. The failure this assist
would catch did not occur once.

*But the measurement cannot see the case the model would be best at, and that
limit is the finding.* The test requires a *verbatim* title fragment. Discussion
that paraphrases — "the ceremony trigger", "the read-only guard" — matches
nothing and is invisible to it. So the zero rules out verbatim uncited
discussion and says nothing about the paraphrased kind, which is precisely where
a model would earn its place. *Measuring that needs a different instrument, and
the obvious one is the model itself* — which makes the evidence circular unless
a human adjudicates a sample.

*So the ordering is clear even though the question is not settled.* Extend the
hook first: it is cheap, exact, and closes the only mode that currently produces
a wrong answer rather than a refusal. Revisit assistance only if an
uncited-by-paraphrase instance is ever *observed*, rather than building for a
failure with no recorded occurrence — which is
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s standing argument, and
applies to a check as much as to a guess.

*Marker numbering shipped 2026-09-16, immediately after
[[id:33c3eabc-00f2-4cf1-afb7-4071aa256d62][33c3eabc]] and for the mode that
heading explicitly could not reach.*

The hook now assigns each emitted footnote line a marker **carrying on from the
footnotes the response already has**, so a pasted line cannot collide with one
in use. That addresses case 1 — the reused superscript — at its cause rather
than its symptom: there is no longer an occasion to pick a number by hand, and
the refusal that used to say "this id is unfootnoted" now hands over a line
already numbered.

*Counted by footnote lines carrying an id, never by parsing the superscripts.*
Counting is exact in a byte-oriented shell; taking multibyte markers apart is
not. It assumes footnotes run from one without gaps, which is the convention
every response here follows and which the hook now maintains by construction.

*The bug worth recording is not the feature.* The first implementation wrote the
superscripts as literal characters and **silently lost every three-byte one** —
=U+00B9/B2/B3= are two bytes and survived the editing pipeline, =U+2070= and
=U+2074..2079= are three and arrived corrupted. The hook then emitted lines with
**no marker at all** while all 23 existing tests passed, because not one of them
looked at the marker. Rewritten as UTF-8 byte escapes: pure ASCII in the source,
so no editor, heredoc or quoting layer can damage them. Two tests added, either
of which would have caught it.

*Two incidental fixes found the same way.* The new message contained the word
"continue", which the suite greps for as its no-JSON proxy — a real collision
between prose and a test's chosen sentinel. And the final rc check reused
session id =s9=, already spent by an earlier case, so its loop-guard sentinel
was set and the check measured the guard rather than the block. Both were
passing-looking failures.

*Scope, stated plainly, because this heading is not finished.* Of the five
modes: enumeration, quoted example and borrowed prose were always *detected* and
are now merely cheap; the paraphrased title became impossible with =33c3eabc=;
the marker collision is now *prevented* rather than diagnosed. **What remains is
detection rather than prevention** — the hook still cannot say "you have used
⁴ twice" about markers already written, because that needs multibyte parsing
this shell does no well. Whether that is worth having once collisions are
prevented at the source is this heading's remaining question, and the honest
answer may be no.

*Measured 2026-09-16, because the user asked whether observing the new
behaviour to the end of a session would settle the remaining question. It would
not, and the measurement says why — then answers the question outright.*

**Observation could not have worked, for a stated reason.** A marker collision
only *blocks* when it also leaves an id unfootnoted. Two prose citations sharing
=⁴= while both ids appear in the footnote block pass the id-presence check
silently. So "no collisions this session" is indistinguishable from "collisions
happened and nothing looked" — the failure is undetectable by construction,
which is this project's own silent-failure class. A second gap makes it worse:
**numbering only applies when the hook fires**, and a response footnoted
correctly the first time is never numbered at all. That is the majority of
responses.

*The history is on disk, so waiting was unnecessary.* Scanned **6749 assistant
responses, 924 carrying a footnote block**:

| fault | occurrences |
|---|---|
| duplicate marker within the footnote block | **0** |
| footnote line whose marker never appears in the prose | 77 |
| prose marker with no footnote line | 20 |

**The fault this heading proposed detecting has never occurred.** Zero in 924
footnoted responses. By =7771fc63='s standing argument — and by this heading's
own precedent, which measured the subagent question over 314 responses and
declined on a clean negative — **collision detection should not be built**.
That closes the remaining question with a no.

*But the scan found a different fault, ten times more common and never filed.*
97 responses (roughly **10% of all footnoted ones**) have a marker and a
footnote that do not correspond: 77 where a footnote line's marker appears
nowhere in the prose, 20 where a prose marker has no footnote line. Spread
across a month — 18 on 08-27, 14 on 08-25, 5 on 09-15 — so it is not an
artefact of one session's habits, and only 1 falls on the day of the scan.

*And the objection that blocked the original idea does not apply to this one.*
Detecting a *duplicate* needs the markers taken apart and compared as numbers,
which a byte-oriented shell does badly. Detecting *correspondence* needs only
**fixed-string matching**: take the leading marker off a footnote line as a byte
string and =grep -qF= it against the prose. No character arithmetic, no
multibyte parsing, no locale dependence. The check that is worth having is also
the cheap one, and the expensive check is the one the evidence says to drop.

*Filed rather than built here*, since it is a different check from this
heading's subject and deserves its own decision.

*Retracted 2026-09-16: **the marker modes are retired rather than fixed**, and
checking the premise found they were never the convention.*

=~/.claude/CLAUDE.md= — the only place the footnote rule lives, the repo and
plugin carrying no copy — mentions superscripts **zero times**. It says "cite
the identifier in the prose and append a footnote list mapping each one to its
exact, full title". The hook mentioned them **six times**. So the markers were
the *hook's* accretion, and case 1 of this heading's three — a superscript
reused for two ids — was a defect in a requirement no governing document ever
made.

**The argument that settles it: the identifier is already the key.** A marker
exists to link a citation to its note when the citation has no natural handle.
An =:ID:= *is* that handle — the prose says =c19fbbf5= and so does the line. The
marker was a second and weaker index over data the id indexes perfectly, which
is why it kept breaking.

*Two false positives, both mine, both from machinery added the same day:*

- The **dangling** check could not tell a citation from a superscript being
  *shown*; quoting the hook's own message tripped it. Retired within the hour.
- The **orphan** check then fired on a response whose citation sat in a text
  block *before* the tool calls. =last_assistant_message= carries only the final
  block, so a marker placed earlier reads as orphaned. The check could not see
  the whole response it was judging.

Both are marker faults. Neither can occur without markers.

*What the new form is.* End matter after a =---= separator, one line per
identifier, **sorted by identifier**: backticked id, TODO keyword padded to a
column, exact title including any cookie. The id leads because it is what the
eye scans for; the keyword is column-aligned because it is the volatile field
and misalignment hides staleness; the cookie belongs to the title because
=[8/9]= and =[16/16]= are the part most often wrong when recalled.

*A detail worth keeping: the emission was already id-sorted.* =sort -u= on the
candidate ids does it, so "ordered by first appearance" lived only in the
instruction text and never in the output. The convention had been describing
something the mechanism did not do.

*Scope of the retraction.* Of the five modes, the three that were **marker
faults cease to exist**. Enumeration and borrowed prose were always detected by
the id check, which is untouched and is the one that was always doing the work.
So this heading closes not because its modes were fixed but because the
category most of them belonged to was removed.

*Shipped 2026-09-17: an id already paired with its exact title in the body discharges the debt — which is this heading's central observation acted on from the hook's side.*

The sentence this heading turns on is that "the hook's test is *does the response contain this token*; the writer's test is *am I referring to a heading here*", and that the two diverge whenever an id appears for some other reason. The **enumeration** mode is the live case: a status summary lists each id beside its exact title, the eye reads a row rather than a reference, and the end matter then repeats all of them verbatim. One unwind on 2026-09-17 cited twelve ids that way and was asked for all twelve again — the user's second report of the hook being "way too much".

*But the enumeration mode was mis-described here, and the correction matters.* This heading recorded enumeration as a case that "was always detected" and is "now merely cheap" — treating detection as the whole job. It is not: a listed id paired with its title has **already satisfied the convention**. The prose is readable and the reference is recoverable, which is the entire purpose. Demanding a separate entry is duplication, and the duplication is what the user was seeing.

*The test costs nothing.* =footnote-check= already resolves each id's exact title in order to print the suggestion, so the check is one fixed-string comparison against a value in hand — no parsing, no arithmetic, the same cheapness argument that chose correspondence-checking over collision-detection above.

*Same line, deliberately.* An id in one paragraph and its title in another is the ordinary unfootnoted case: a reader scanning for the id does not find the title beside it, which is the guarantee being protected. Both directions are asserted — paired on one line passes, split across lines still blocks — and the passing case was proved red before the hook changed. 26 tests, was 24.

*Recorded here rather than on its own heading at the user's direction*; =1203168b= is cancelled as its twin.

*Measured 2026-09-17, against this session's own transcript: the discharge rule shipped and the demands did not fall.* 57 fires. Mean ids demanded per fire was 1.4 before 14:00Z and 4.3 after, peaking at 15 — the rise tracks the subject matter, not a regression: the afternoon was portfolio bookkeeping, where a response *is* a list of headings.

The working discharge rule landed at 17:33Z (commit =f608123=, the =-F -e= fix). Fires after it averaged 4.3 against 3.5 before, so it bought nothing measurable. Two candidate causes, and the hook is exonerated: driven directly with a scratch queue dir, a message citing three bare ids demands all three and exits 2, while the same id paired with its exact title on one line exits 0. The mechanism works. **What did not happen is my using it** — the large fires are bare id lists, which is the *enumeration* mode this heading already names. No new mode.

One thing the rule does not reach, and it is worth writing down rather than treating as a miss. At 12 or 15 ids the inline form means 12 or 15 full titles inside the prose, which is precisely the unscannable paragraph the convention exists to prevent. There the end matter is the correct form and the block is the convention working. The discharge rule is a remedy for the two-to-five-id case; above that the volume is inherent to talking about that many headings at once.

*Judgment reserved by the user 2026-09-17, and the =REVIEW= should not be read as agreement that this is finished.* Their position: **the root cause has not been identified yet.** If it turns out there are *multiple distinct root causes*, this heading "deserves to become a story so that each cause can be addressed individually, even if through a common mechanism" — which is mitosis via =org_divide=, the original staying as the child. Their word for the body's present state: "out and out metastasism."

*That reframes the volume measured above.* 3,707 words, ten dated revision sections in 17 days, largest heading in TODO.org at 15x the median — that was read here as a writing-habit problem. The user's reading is that it is a *structural* signal: a body metastasises when one heading is carrying several causes that were never separated, and each visit appends to the pile instead of landing somewhere of its own. On that reading the size is a symptom to diagnose, not prose to condense — which is also why condensing it now would be the wrong move, since it would erase the evidence of the seams.

*So the next session's first question is not "close it?" but "how many causes are in here?"* Candidate seams visible in the body, offered as a starting point and not as an answer: the marker faults (retired with markers, so possibly not a cause at all); enumeration; borrowed prose and quotation, which may be one cause — an id arriving by transcription; the paraphrased title, already made impossible by =33c3eabc=; and the volume/discipline question raised today, where the mechanism works and goes unused. Whether those are distinct causes or one cause seen five ways is exactly what has not been established.

*Closed 2026-09-23, the user's verdict: every cause this heading collected
now has a heading of its own.* The reused-marker faults were retired with
the markers on 2026-09-16. Enumeration, including a mechanism that works
and goes unused, is =13aed7ca=. Quotation and borrowed prose, an id
arriving by transcription, are =490e6737=. The title paraphrased from a
neighbour's body was made impossible by =33c3eabc=. The final-block blind
spot is =c247d8f3=, and discussion by subject is =eadb3607=. The body is
left as it stands: its size is the evidence of where the seams were.

Composed 2026-09-21, when =de6de108= was committed to close within =8a2eb687=.

The open question is the body's own: should a response that is *structurally a
list of headings* owe end matter at all? Three shapes:

1. *Accept the end matter above about five ids.* The body already concludes that
   at twelve or fifteen ids the end matter is the correct form and the block is
   the convention working. Nothing is built; the rule text says so.
2. *Discharge a list shape.* A list whose every item pairs its id with its exact
   title already discharges, and that shipped 2026-09-17. The failure is that
   bare-id lists get written. Change the writing guidance, not the hook.
3. *Stand the hook down* on a response whose ids all sit in list items. That is
   cheap, but it loses the guarantee for a list of bare ids, which is the
   enumeration mode exactly.

Recommendation: 1 with 2. The end matter stays the rule for many ids, and the
citation rules say plainly that a status list should pair each id with its title
inline. Decline 3.

Decision (user): which shape. The session asks before its step.

here the writer **did** choose every id. A status summary or a portfolio list
cites ten or fifteen headings deliberately; none of them arrives by copying. So
the transcription account does not reach it, and the parent heading's unifying
claim — that every failure is an id the writer did not choose to write — is
false of this case.

Measured 2026-09-17 across this session's 57 hook fires: mean ids demanded rose
from 1.4 before 14:00Z to 4.3 after, peaking at 15, tracking the subject matter.
The discharge rule shipped at 17:33Z and bought nothing — 4.3 after against 3.5
before. The hook was exonerated by direct test: three bare ids demand all three
and exit 2, an id paired with its exact title on one line exits 0.

**The mechanism works and goes unused, which is the actual defect and is not a
discipline problem.** At twelve or fifteen ids the inline form means twelve or
fifteen full titles inside the prose — precisely the unscannable paragraph the
convention exists to prevent. So the remedy is unusable exactly where the debt
is largest, and the writer correctly declines it. The open question is whether a
response that is *structurally a list of headings* should owe end matter at all,
or whether the hook should stand down on that shape.

The prose caveat was the expensive half: =DOING= plus a trailing sentence makes a reader hold two states and a qualifier, where =REVIEW*= is the state that will be true, flagged as not yet true, in one glyph. The queued state is also the one the reader needs, being what the session just did.

*The user's sharper point, which is the part worth keeping:* the star **takes no note of its own**. A parenthetical naming which entries are queued is a footnote to the footnote — it re-opens in prose the very thing the mark exists to close, and it grows with the number of starred lines while the marks do not.

*What differed from the request.* One thing was added unbidden and deliberately: a note that =footnote-check= emits the on-disk keyword and never a star, and that this is **correct**.
