** NEXT footnote-check reads only the turn's final text block, so an id cited before a tool call is never checked
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c247d8f3-9f3f-464a-b3a0-543b1bb9de4f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 13:25]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-23 Wed 19:51] \\
  its measurement informs 490e6737 and the division's verdict
:END:
:PLAN:
*The design, approved 2026-09-24 (the user), on the bounded path it stepped up to from a spike.* Divided out of =2a6a1355= on 2026-09-21: the Stop hook sees only =last_assistant_message=, the turn's final text block, so an id cited in a text block before a tool call is never checked. The spike measured whether that happens before building anything (=7771fc63='s standing argument), and it does. The count is in the body.

*Two sources, because the transcript lags.* The hook's header records that Claude Code writes the transcript asynchronously, so the *final* block may not be on disk when Stop fires. Blocks written before a tool call are safely flushed, because the tool call came after them. So:
- The final block still comes from =last_assistant_message=, and end matter is read only from it, as today.
- The earlier blocks come from =transcript_path=: every assistant text block since the segment's start, which is the last user prompt or the previous =end_turn= message. If the transcript already holds the final message, it is recognised by matching =last_assistant_message=, so it neither starts a new segment nor is counted twice.
- Ids are collected from every block, with fences stripped block by block, and checked against the final block's end matter and same-line pairings under the rules the hook already applies.

*Cost and robustness.* The transcript is read from its end, in a window that grows until it reaches the segment's start, never whole. Parsing is =jq=, and the hook stays plain shell, as the queue hooks must survive a stopped Emacs. With no =transcript_path=, or one that cannot be read, the hook behaves exactly as today. The loop guard, the demand sentinel and the three-block bound are unchanged. Transcript pruning (=4acd8ad0=) does not bite, because the current turn's transcript exists by construction.

*Testing*, in =bin/footnote-check-test=, each against a fixture transcript:
- an id in an earlier block, absent from the end matter, blocks;
- the same id footnoted passes;
- an id inside an earlier block's code fence is ignored;
- blocks from before the previous =end_turn= are ignored;
- a final message not yet in the transcript is still scanned from the payload;
- no transcript gives today's behaviour.

Each is shown to fail against HEAD's hook. Then the spike's probe is re-run against the new hook: the 107 caught segments should fall to the 3 known end-matter false positives.

*Docs.* The "What enforces it" section of =skills/org/references/org-footnote-citations.md=, which says the hook "carries only the turn's final text block", is updated and re-promoted with =bin/claude-org-setup=.

Files: =bin/hooks/footnote-check=, =bin/footnote-check-test=, and that reference.
:END:

=footnote-check= reads =last_assistant_message=, only the turn's final text block, so footnote debt in any earlier block passes unseen. The 2026-09-24 spike measured that it happens, so this stepped up to bounded: the hook will also read the segment's earlier text blocks from =transcript_path=.

*Established (2026-09-24):* of 1,576 Stop segments across this repo's 60 transcripts, 107 (6.8%), in 19 sessions, passed the hook on their final block and would have blocked on the whole segment, owing 213 entries across 133 ids. Spot checks found real citations in the narration written between tool calls. Method: the real hook run twice per segment, with a temporary queue and fake session ids. Caveats: 3 segments carried end matter in an earlier block, so a few are likely false positives, and ids were resolved against today's files.


** TODO A heading discussed by subject and never by id owes a citation no token scan can see
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       eadb3607-aa3d-41ee-b8cc-e293cc924f26
:CREATED:  [2026-09-21 Mon 13:25]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-24 Thu 09:21]--[2026-09-24 Thu 09:22] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-24 Thu 09:21]--[2026-09-24 Thu 09:22] brainstorm the aboutness sampling spike
:END:
:PLAN:
Divided out of =2a6a1355= 2026-09-21. The hook asks whether every id present is footnoted. It cannot ask whether every heading *discussed* was cited, because a response can describe a heading at length by its subject and never write its id. That is a judgement about aboutness, which no regex makes.

*What is already measured:* 0 occurrences across 314 responses of a heading's four-word title phrase appearing while its id did not. That rules out verbatim uncited discussion and says nothing about paraphrase, which only a model can detect. So the evidence is circular unless a human adjudicates.

1. Run one pass with a subagent over a sample of recent responses (about 100), flagging each heading it believes a response discussed without citing.
2. The user adjudicates the flagged cases, which should be about ten.
3. Zero confirmed: close CANCELLED with the sample and the verdicts. Build nothing for an unobserved failure (=7771fc63=).
4. One or more confirmed: file the mechanism as its own heading and close this one on the measurement.

Decision (user): the adjudication in step 2. Everything before it is the session's.
:END:

A response can discuss a heading by subject and never write its id. It then owes a citation that no token scan detects. Verbatim uncited discussion measured zero in 314 responses, and the paraphrased kind has never been measured. Settle it with one adjudicated sample, and build only if a case is confirmed.


* MAYBE Revisit building the footnote block with org-ql or the agenda, once Emacs stops wedging :research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3e08732f-f737-4558-938d-b91276a70b8a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:51]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:BLOCKER:  489d6f61-0cd8-4b0c-9f9a-9abcf0cf3938
:END:
:PLAN:

Asked by the user 2026-09-17 and deliberately parked rather than declined. =bin/hooks/footnote-check= resolves ids by grepping =TODO.org= and =DONE.org= with awk, which is why it can emit a keyword and an exact title without Emacs. Org's own facilities — =org-ql=, the agenda, =org-map-entries= — would do it better and would answer questions awk cannot: whether a heading has keyworded children, which is what separates a **story** from a task and is the one field =bf00d36a= had to leave out.

*The blocker is not preference, it is availability.* The hook belongs to the survive-without-Emacs tier, alongside the queue-append family, "precisely so a stopped Emacs costs nothing". Routing it through =emacsclient= would block **every turn** whenever Emacs is unavailable — and on 2026-09-17 alone Emacs deadlocked six times, with the hook working throughout because it reads files directly. Under current conditions the change would trade a correct field for an unusable session.

*So this waits on =489d6f61=*, recorded as a =:BLOCKER:= rather than as prose so it wakes by itself: a blocker on a =MAYBE= heading enforces nothing today and becomes live the moment this takes a working keyword. If the deadlock is fixed by an Emacs upgrade, a mac-port change or anything else, this is the consequence worth collecting — and the condition to re-test is narrow: how long the hook may block a =Stop= when Emacs is healthy, against the ~10 s hook timeout.

*Worth deciding at the same time, if it is ever taken up:* whether the hook should fall back to awk when Emacs is unreachable rather than choosing one path forever. That keeps the guarantee and gets the better field when it is available, at the cost of two code paths that can disagree — which this project has twice found to be the expensive kind of duplication.
:END:


* TODO Add :KIND: to the footnote and keep the cookie, but 84 cookie-shaped tokens in prose stay rewritable :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       bf00d36a-7a26-4f1a-bc98-fb0822d60065
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:39]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 11:36]--[2026-09-17 Thu 11:37] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-17 Thu 11:36]--[2026-09-17 Thu 11:37] assigned at review
:END:
:PLAN:

*This retracts a convention shipped 2026-09-16*, which said the end-matter line carries "the exact title *including any cookie*", on the reasoning that =[8/9]= and =[16/16]= "are the part most often wrong when recalled". The better answer is that they cannot be wrong if they are not there.

*A cookie is derived state, not part of a name.* =749301a0= read =[8/9]=, =[10/12]=, =[11/13]=, =[12/13]= and =[13/14]= within one session on 2026-09-17 — five different "titles" for one heading in a day. The conventions already say the cookie "is not yours to remember"; it is computed by machinery from children and checkboxes. Citing it makes a footnote stale within hours and identifies nothing, which is the opposite of what the exact-title rule is for.

*And it puts rewritable text into prose, measured 2026-09-17.* 115 cookie-shaped tokens across TODO.org and DONE.org; **31** sit on headlines where they belong, **none** on checkbox lines, so roughly **84 are in body prose** — quoted titles, mostly, because the footnote convention put them there. =org-update-statistics-cookies= with a non-nil ALL argument rewrites every one, recomputing a quoted =[1/1]= to =[0/0]= because no children sit beneath a paragraph. The damage reads as plausible text, not as corruption. Observed by the user as a buffer/disk diff at TODO.org line 4271, where a quoted title had been zeroed.

*Exposure is narrow but real.* The ceremony is safe — it runs drawer consolidation and heading separation, not cookies. =org_divide= and the slice refresh are safe, updating at point or through the refresh path. What is exposed is a human pressing =C-u C-c #= in either file, or an agent running the command without thinking, which is exactly how the instance above happened.

*The fix has three parts.* The convention drops the cookie from quoted titles, in =~/.claude/CLAUDE.md='s successor and in the shipped =org-footnote-citations.md=. =bin/hooks/footnote-check= strips it when emitting, since it currently hands back the title as read from the file. And the existing footnote lines in bodies are left alone — they are historical statements, already stale, and rewriting them is churn of the kind =5fc7b934= declined for line-number citations.
:END:

A statistics cookie is derived state recomputed from children, not part of a heading's name — =749301a0= wore five different ones in a single day — so quoting it in a footnote makes the citation stale on arrival and identifies nothing. It also puts cookie-shaped text into prose: 115 such tokens exist across the two files, only 31 of them on headlines, and a buffer-wide =org-update-statistics-cookies= silently zeroes the rest. Retracts the 2026-09-16 convention that the quoted title include the cookie; the hook must strip it too.

*Corrected by the user 2026-09-17, within the hour, and the =:PLAN:= above is left standing with its wrong conclusion because the turn is the useful part.* The proposal there was to drop the cookie from quoted titles. The user does not want that: the cookie is what lets them tell a **task** from a **grouping** at a glance in the end matter, and they named the residual confusion themselves — story versus slice, which the cookie alone cannot separate.

*So the cookie is being read as a type sigil, not as a count, and that dissolves most of the staleness objection.* If only presence carries meaning, a =[8/9]= that should now read =[13/14]= still says "grouping" correctly. Being wrong about the number costs nothing when the number is not what is being consulted.

*Measured on the open corpus, 170 headings, no exceptions:*

: slice   cookie + :KIND: slice     5
: story   cookie, no :KIND:         6
: task    no cookie               159

So cookie and =:KIND:= are a two-bit classifier and each carries a bit the other does not. =:KIND:= cannot do it alone, because a **story is emergent and never declared** — it is detected by having keyworded children (=2e660571= measured its way out of declaring what the structure already states), so there is no property to read.

*The change, then, is additive:* =bin/hooks/footnote-check= emits =:KIND:= alongside the keyword. It already greps the property drawer for =:ID:=, so reading one more property on the same pass costs nothing and needs no Emacs. What it must **not** attempt is deriving "story" — that needs org's entry boundaries, and reconstructing those in awk is precisely the failure the org-dev skill §0b exists to prevent. The cookie supplies that bit instead.

*What survives from the original filing, unchanged:* 115 cookie-shaped tokens across the two files, 31 on headlines, so roughly **84 sit in body prose**, and =org-update-statistics-cookies= with a non-nil ALL silently recomputes every one of them to =[0/0]=. That hazard is independent of whether a citation should carry a cookie — it follows from quoting titles at all, which the footnote convention requires. The exposure is a human pressing =C-u C-c #=, or an agent running it without thinking, which is how the instance at TODO.org:4271 happened.

*Placement, decided 2026-09-17 so it is not re-decided:* the *definition* — a cookie is derived state, not part of a name — goes in =org-conventions.md= beside the passage introducing the cookie, together with the rewrite hazard, since both matter when an =.org= file is open and that file is path-scoped to =**/*.org=. The *instruction* — emit =:KIND:=, keep the cookie — goes in =org-footnote-citations.md=, which carries no path scope because ids are cited with no org file in play. State it once in each and cross-reference; two files each holding a full account is how =~/.claude/CLAUDE.md= and its promoted twin diverged within hours.

Dividing =e28933e9= on 2026-09-17 produced a parent reading =* TODO Make the corpus searchable by tag, … by decision where it cannot [0/1]= — cookie at the end of the title.

The convention is explicit that it goes **immediately after the TODO keyword**, and it is not cosmetic: "a trailing cookie is the part a narrow agenda window or a folded outline truncates away — which defeats the one thing the cookie is for, that progress is readable *without* unfolding". The user has already reported a slice as "missing its cookie" on exactly that basis when it was present at the end of a line they could not see the end of.

*This is the same defect, in a second inserter.* =--ensure-statistics-cookie-at-point= wrote a trailing cookie and was fixed when the corpus was measured 13:1 against it (=:ID:= 28415ca8's reasoning). =org_divide= was not, so the fix landed in one of the two places that insert a cookie. Worth checking at the same time whether any third path does.

Cheap, and the corpus check is already done and recorded, so this needs no re-measurement — only the same placement =--ensure-statistics-cookie-at-point= now uses.

*Design, approved 2026-09-23 (the user). It supersedes the paragraph
above that ends "only the same placement … now uses", and settles its
open question.*

*No third code path exists.* Every other insertion (=org_set_property='s
=KIND=slice= branch, the slice refresh) already goes through
=--ensure-statistics-cookie-at-point=. =claude-code-ide-org-divide= is the
only place that places a cookie by hand. It builds the parent line as
state, title, then =" [/]"=. The corpus holds no trailing cookie today:
the one on ee3e0fa2 was repaired by hand.

*Two places teach the wrong form, and both change with the fix.* The test
=claude-code-ide-org-test-divide-carries-everything-to-the-child= asserts
=^\* TODO A story now \[0/1\]$=, pinning the defect. The org skill's cookie
example (=skills/org/SKILL.md=, "Pack for the trip [2/5]") shows the
trailing form, and a hand-written heading copies it.

*The change.* =claude-code-ide-org-divide= inserts the parent line without
a cookie, then calls =--ensure-statistics-cookie-at-point= on the new
parent before =org-update-statistics-cookies=. One function then owns
placement, and the rule has no second copy. The helper leaves a title that
already carries a cookie alone, and works on a keyword-less parent.

*Testing.* The divide test expects =^\* TODO \[0/1\] A story now$=. A
second case covers a keyword-less parent. Both are shown to fail against
the old line construction. The skill example becomes
=*** TODO [2/5] Pack for the trip=, with one sentence on why the cookie
leads. Then =bin/test= and =bin/lint-org= run green.
:END:

`org_divide` appends the statistics cookie after the title text, so a
narrow agenda or folded outline truncates it with the end of the headline.
Seen on `ee3e0fa2`, the parent the divide of e28933e9 created. It is the
only inserter left doing this: every other path places the cookie through
`--ensure-statistics-cookie-at-point`, after the keyword. A test and the
org skill's example both still teach the trailing form. The fix routes
divide through the helper; the design is in `:PLAN:`.


* TODO [0/2] Make the corpus searchable by tag, by measurement where it can be and by decision where it cannot
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ee3e0fa2-d4a6-4575-a5fb-e4e25c49431a
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:26]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:PLAN:

Divided out of =e28933e9= on 2026-09-17, which becomes the child and keeps its id, clock and history. The two halves want different schedules and that is the whole reason for the split: the **survey** is deliberately parked as expensive in time and tokens, while a tag adopted **by decision** is a same-day act. Holding the ready half hostage to the parked one is what =8bbae3aa= exists to stop one tier up, and the same argument divides this heading.

The division also names a real seam rather than a convenience. =e28933e9= derives a vocabulary *from* the corpus — "a measurement first and a taxonomy second", in its own words. Its sibling declares one *for* a purpose, with no measurement available to it: whether a defect was observed in use is a fact about how it was found, not a pattern latent in the text. A survey cannot recover it and should not try.

What they share, and why one parent: both answer "which tags make this corpus worth searching", and both land in the same two places — the =#+TAGS:= line and the org conventions' §Tags section. An adopted tag from either half constrains the other, so they should not drift apart in separate parts of the file.
:END:

The corpus answers "what did we decide" badly: a decision lives in one body and nothing indexes it. Two children carry the halves — tagging what can be measured, and tagging what can only be decided. Whether `org-agenda` or `org-ql` can build the view natively is an open question deferred until Emacs stops wedging.

:END:

Captured 2026-09-09 at the user's ask, deliberately *not* started: the
survey is expected to be expensive in time and tokens, so this heading
parks it until it is worth spending.

*The ask:* survey the whole task history — TODO.org and DONE.org, some
four hundred headings — for the keyword clusters actually present, and
derive from them a tag vocabulary on which tasks can usefully be
searched and filtered. The current declared set (=code comms research
review=) predates almost all of the corpus.

*Two seed judgements from the user, to test against the data rather
than assume:* =:org:= for Org syntax/structure/mark-up work, which
recurs constantly and has no tag today; and the observation that
=:code:= is close to universal — not useful as a positive filter — but
may earn its place through its *negation*, "not code" being the rarer
and more interesting query.

*Shape notes for whoever picks it up:* the survey is a measurement
first (title and body keyword clusters, existing tag usage counts,
what queries were actually run via =org_query='s history if any
survives) and a taxonomy second — the =:CATEGORY:= exercise
(=29439196=) is the precedent, including its finding that compound
labels hide behind single words. Any proposed set should state, per
tag, the query it exists to answer, and the =#+TAGS:= line and
=org-conventions.md= §Tags are where an adopted set lands.

*Three tags adopted ahead of this survey* (the user, 2026-09-22):
=:spike:=, =:bounded:= and =:arch:=, the brainstorming skill's paths
(4ae7a04b). They are set only by that skill at capture, at most one per
heading (=bin/lint-org= checks it). Each answers one query: which work was
classified how, and whether a spike's code was ever kept. The user's first
idea, folding "bounded" into =:code:=, was set aside because =:code:= sits
on 529 headings and earns its place through its negation. The survey
should take these as given, not re-derive them.

*A fourth tag adopted ahead of this survey, and the first worked example*
(the user, 2026-09-22): =:prose:=, for a heading whose deliverable is prose
rather than code. Its first carrier is
[[id:878d2cf5-e08e-4bcb-8db1-a601b9b08f55][878d2cf5]], which edits a rules
file and touches no elisp; it was captured =:code:= out of habit and
re-tagged, which is itself the evidence that =:code:= is applied by reflex.

*This is the positive form of the negation judgement recorded above.*
=:code:= earning its place through =!tags:code= only works if the
complement is a real category rather than a residue; naming it makes the
query direct instead of inverted, and makes the reflex visible at capture.

*Census taken 2026-09-22 through =org-get-tags= over both files*, which
should be the survey's starting point rather than re-measured: =code= 538,
=research= 27, =observed= 8, =bounded= 4, =arch= 2, =spike= 2, =docs= 2,
=comms= 1, =analysis= 1.

*Two findings in that census bear directly on the survey's method.*
=:docs:= already exists and both its uses are human-facing documents, a
=DOOM.md= procedure and a =README=, matching the =Docs= category's "prose
written for a human reader" — so =:prose:= is the general word and =:docs:=
a narrower one, and whether the narrower survives is the survey's to
decide, not something to settle by fiat now. And =analysis= is *declared in
=#+TAGS:= and used once*: a coinage that never took, sitting in the
vocabulary looking adopted. A tag used once is the failure mode this survey
exists to find, so the count per tag belongs in its output beside the query
each tag answers.

=prose= is declared in TODO.org's =#+TAGS:= line. =observed= and =docs= are
*not*, though both are in use — another thing for the survey to reconcile.


** NEXT Adopt an `observed` tag for a defect seen in use, as the severity signal the keyword set does not carry :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8b945c02-7ebe-40a2-b4cc-e35811afe57f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:30]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-09-17 Thu 11:30] \\
  the cheap half; the survey stays parked
:END:
:PLAN:

*The observation, 2026-09-17.* The user noted an implicit severity range among open TODOs, by impact on usability and correctness. Measured against the corpus's own declared priority mechanism — "priority is expressed through keyword choice" — there is none: time-tracking work sits at =MAYBE= 9.1% against 8.2% for everything else, and both are 80% plain =TODO=. The keyword is not ranking anything, because four headings in five hold the same one.

*The recommendation: one tag, adopted by decision rather than derived by survey.* =:observed:= marks a defect **seen in use** rather than filed speculatively. The query it exists to answer — which this parent's sibling requires of any proposed tag — is *what is likely to break a consumer*, which is the question every slice composition has had to answer by re-reading the backlog.

*Why a tag and not a =:SEVERITY:= property.* This project's evidence is that a declared field nobody derives goes stale invisibly: the slice checkbox is derived from the referent's keyword for exactly this reason, =:DROPPED:= replaced bare cookie-deletion because absence was ambiguous, and a =:BLOCKER:= on a =MAYBE= reads as enforcement while enforcing nothing. A severity field over 128 open headings needs triage now and re-triage forever. =:observed:= needs neither: it is applied at filing when the knowledge is freshest, and "was seen in use" never changes.

*And why it does not wait on the survey.* The survey is deliberately parked as expensive; this is a same-day decision. Holding the cheap ready half hostage to the expensive unfinished one is what =8bbae3aa= exists to stop one tier up, and the same reasoning divides this heading from its sibling.

*Scope, if adopted:* add to =#+TAGS:= in both files and the templates, a line in the org conventions' §Tags saying what it means and when to apply it, and a backfill decision — probably **no** blanket backfill, since "observed" is a claim about how a defect was found and cannot be reconstructed for old headings without re-reading them, which is the survey's job anyway.
:END:

The keyword set carries no severity, so a defect someone actually hit reads the same as one reasoned about. An `:observed:` tag is the cheapest signal that distinguishes them, and it is a fact rather than a judgement — either it was seen in use or it was not.


* TODO Clock brackets of seconds around work of an hour, so nearly all real time lands in unowned gaps :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a2fac2e7-bf88-4f6f-8ada-846e3bafde11
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:02]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:PLAN:

Measured on 2026-09-17's own queue. Every clock bracket the agent wrote that morning:

: 08:52:05 -> 08:56:31  27bb4b08  4m 26s
: 09:51:32 -> 09:51:47  83773daf     15s
: 09:58:03 -> 09:58:34  507754ba     31s

Between the first two lies a 55-minute stretch containing **24 guideposts** — the body restoration, filing =17f93758=, wrapping twelve headings. Real work, none of it inside any bracket.

*The mechanism is the shape of the calls, not forgetfulness.* =org_clock_in= and =org_clock_out= were issued in the same batch of tool calls, so the bracket encloses the *announcement* of the work rather than the work, and everything after it runs unclocked until the next heading is announced. Fifteen seconds is not a plausible duration for anything; it is the round-trip of two queued events.

*Two consequences, and the second is why this is filed against practice rather than left as a habit.* Every heading's recorded time is understated by roughly the whole of its work. And the unbracketed remainder becomes the "unowned" gaps that =--span-complement= offers for review — which is what filled the user's buffer with spans they could not attribute, and what made them ask three separate times what a span represented. =5a9d877e= stopped those gaps spanning days; nothing can make them meaningful while the brackets they are measured against are seconds long.

*The standing rule covers the open and not the close.* "Clock in before the first thing that writes" is followed; what is missing is clocking **out** when the heading is actually left. The correction: =clock_out= belongs at the moment attention moves to another heading, not at the end of the announcing turn, and a stretch of work on one heading should produce one bracket that covers it.

*Checkable rather than resolved, which is the point of filing it.* The ratio of bracketed time to guidepost-covered time is computable straight from the queue, so "did this improve" has an answer that does not depend on anyone remembering. Today's figure is the baseline: three brackets totalling 5m 12s against a morning of work.
:END:

The agent's clock brackets enclose the announcement of work rather than the work: three brackets on 2026-09-17 totalling 5m 12s, one of them 15 seconds, against a morning whose unbracketed gaps held 24 guideposts. Every heading's time is understated by most of its work, and the remainder becomes the unowned spans that fill the review buffer. The standing rule covers clocking *in*; the correction belongs at the close — =clock_out= when attention leaves the heading, not when the announcing turn ends.


* TODO A span with no readable evidence prints nothing about itself, so absence is the only signal :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       cf483f5d-2d25-4454-a580-db7935c8cb81
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 11:01]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:PLAN:

=--review-written-summary= returns the phrase "0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns" for a span whose events carry a readable =:kind=, and **nil** otherwise — printing nothing at all. So an item with no usable evidence renders exactly like an ordinary one minus its summary line, and the reader is left to notice an absence.

It does not work. The user asked three times in one morning what such a line represented — of =[15:17]--[18:28]=, of =[14:55]--[15:16]=, and of =[08:56]--[09:51]= — each time saying they could not tell because no minutes, runs or turns were reported. Three asks about three instances of one thing is the measurement.

*The function already promises the opposite, which is what makes this a defect rather than a missing feature.* Its own docstring closes: "Says the runs are absent rather than falling silent when none survives. That is a real outcome … and it is the one a human most needs before marking, since the item will otherwise look applied and leave no CLOCK line behind." The branch that would say it is never reached, because the guard =(and (plist-get item :suggested) (--span-kinds-known-p (plist-get item :events)))= short-circuits first.

Proposal: when =:suggested= but the kinds are unknown, return a phrase rather than nil — naming *why* there is nothing to report, since the two causes want different answers. No events inside at all means the item is bounded by clock brackets rather than by turns, and =d= will refuse it (=445fe577=). Events present but kind-less is the hand-built or fixture case. Either way the line should say what it is, not leave a gap where a summary goes.

Worth deciding at the same time whether such an item should carry a visible marker in the item line itself, not only in the summary, since the summary is what a skimming eye passes over.
:END:

A span whose events carry no readable =:kind= gets no summary line at all, so it renders as an ordinary item minus its evidence and the reader must notice an absence — which they do not: the user asked three times in one morning what such a line meant. Proposal: when the kinds are unknown, say so and say which cause, since "no events inside at all" and "events without kinds" want different answers from the human.


* TODO Recenter after `m` advances, so the item under consideration is never at the bottom of the window :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       396fd1c3-70e0-4e1f-8839-670b5293a7d3
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 10:52]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:51]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:52] =>  0:01
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:51]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:51] =>  0:00
- <2026-09-17 Thu 10:51>--<2026-09-17 Thu 10:51> assigned at review
- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:51]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:52]
:END:
:PLAN:

Requested by the user 2026-09-17. =m= marks and advances — that advance is what makes marking a run =m m m= rather than =m n m n m=, and it was "the single biggest complaint after the first real by-hand apply". But point walks steadily down the window, so after a few marks the item being decided sits at the bottom edge with no following context, and the human is reading the one line they are about to judge with everything after it off-screen. Do the equivalent of =C-l= once the advance has happened, bringing the next item to the centre.

*Implementation note that will bite if missed: the recenter must come after the re-render, not before.* =--review-set-mark= re-renders the buffer on every keystroke, and centring against the pre-render line number would scroll to the wrong place — silently, and only sometimes, since the two agree until a render changes a line's height or position.

*Prefer =(recenter)= to =(recenter-top-bottom)= despite the request naming =C-l=.* =recenter-top-bottom= deliberately *cycles* centre → top → bottom by consulting =last-command=, which is right for a human pressing =C-l= repeatedly and wrong here: called from Lisp after =m=, its behaviour would depend on what the human pressed previously, so a run of marks could start scrolling to the top instead of the centre. =(recenter)= always centres, which is what the request actually asks for.

*Two open questions rather than assumptions.* Should =u= do the same? It advances identically, and symmetry argues yes, but unmarking is a correction and the eye may want to stay where it was. And should the recenter be suppressed at the end of the list, where there is no next item and centring would scroll the final line away from the bottom for no reason?
:END:

After `m` marks an item and advances, the next item can sit at the bottom of the window with none of its context visible. Do the equivalent of `C-l` (`recenter-top-bottom`) at the end of everything `m` already does, so the item under consideration is centred.


* TODO `c` claims an envelope whatever runs it contains, so one keystroke can record 3h11m over 16 minutes :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       aee8487e-0650-4396-bc5c-193ca3efcecb
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 10:08]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:07]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:08] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:07]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:08]
:END:
:PLAN:

=claude-code-ide-org-review-claim-envelope= guards three things — that the item is a span, that it is still =:suggested=, and that it is not zero-width — and nothing about whether the span carries runs. But =2deb090f= built it for the **0-run** case, where the envelope is all there is; its docstring argues from "a span whose guideposts yield no run writes an annotation and no CLOCK line at all". On a span that does carry runs, =c= clears =:suggested=, so =--review-intervals-to-write= returns the displayed interval instead of the runs, and the whole envelope is recorded.

Found in use 2026-09-17, with numbers. The pending span =[2026-09-16 Wed 15:17]--[2026-09-16 Wed 18:28]= is a complement segment whose endpoints are owned-bracket edges — a =clock_out= on Review and planning at 15:17:09 and a =clock_in= on =27788fd1= at 18:28:15 — so clustering's 20-minute threshold never applied to it. Inside are three runs totalling about sixteen minutes, and a **2h53m hole with zero events** between 15:34:07 and 18:27:11, which the user confirmed was time away from the keyboard. Pressing =c= there records 3h11m of attention: a ratio of roughly twelve to one.

The ordinary path is safe and that is the point — apply writes one CLOCK line per run while the item stays =:suggested=, so accepting it normally records the sixteen minutes correctly. Only =c= inflates, and it does so silently and in one keystroke.

Proposal: refuse when the span has runs, or require a confirmation naming both numbers ("claim 3:11 where 0:16 was observed?"). The second is probably right — claiming an envelope over observed runs is occasionally legitimate, since a human may have been working without producing turns, and that is exactly the assertion =c= exists to let them make. What is wrong is making it without being told what it costs. Related bound already recorded on =445fe577=: emitting these gap spans is the feature, and the sharp edge is a large one.
:END:

=claude-code-ide-org-review-claim-envelope= checks that the item is a span, is still =:suggested=, and is not zero-width — nothing about the evidence inside it. =2deb090f= built =c= for the 0-run case where the envelope is all there is, and complement segments broke that assumption: a 0-run span can now be a three-hour gap between clock brackets. Proposal: a confirmation naming both numbers rather than a refusal, since claiming an envelope over sparse evidence is sometimes the right assertion — what is wrong is making it without being shown the cost.


* TODO The PLAN nag counts lines while the tools write paragraphs, so it cannot fire on their output :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       17f93758-694b-4f80-9b21-f21679b0699f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 09:16]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:PLAN:

=bin/lint-org= warns on a finished heading with a substantial body and no =:PLAN:= drawer, and =claude-code-ide-org--lint-substantial-body-p= defines substantial as ten or more lines of prose. But =org_amend= writes each paragraph as a single unwrapped line, so a body the tools produced measures a handful of lines however long it is. Measured on =a3ee7033=: body of 1,084 characters across two prose lines — 836 and 248 — against a threshold of 10, so =substantial= is nil and the rule cannot fire. All five headings closed this session satisfied every other condition (finished keyword, CLOSED present and recent, no PLAN drawer, not a slice) and none was reported. Proposal: measure the body in characters, or in filled lines, rather than in physical newlines — the intent is "a body worth wrapping", and the current measure answers "a body someone typed with hard wraps". Related to =d90a02f2= by the same root: unwrapped paragraphs are also what drive maximum line length, TODO.org's longest being 2,717 characters.
:END:

=bin/lint-org= warns on a finished heading with a substantial body and no =:PLAN:= drawer, but measures "substantial" in physical lines while =org_amend= writes each paragraph unwrapped — so a 1,084-character body counts as two lines against a threshold of ten and the rule cannot fire. Five headings closed in one session met every other condition and none was reported. Proposal: measure characters, or filled lines, rather than newlines.


* DOING Doom drops org files out of org-mode at 15,000 lines, and TODO.org is 1,600 away :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d90a02f2-6fa5-4a62-869a-7484fb9b2dc2
:CREATED:  [2026-09-17 Thu 08:40]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 08:40]--[2026-09-17 Thu 08:41] =>  0:01
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-09-17 Thu 08:40] \\
  trigger diagnosed; the one-line fix is the user's config
- [2026-09-17 Thu 08:40]--[2026-09-17 Thu 08:41]
- [2026-09-17 Thu 08:40]--[2026-09-17 Thu 08:42] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 08:48]--[2026-09-17 Thu 08:49] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-17 Thu 08:48]--[2026-09-17 Thu 08:49] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:
:PLAN:

The recurring warning =Warning (org-element): 'org-element-at-point' cannot be used in non-Org buffer #<buffer DONE.org> (so-long-mode)= is not cosmetic: while it holds, DONE.org is not an Org buffer and every org operation against it is degraded rather than merely noisy.

*Trigger nailed 2026-09-17, and it is line COUNT, not line length.* Doom replaces the stock =so-long-predicate= with =doom-so-long-p=, which fires when the longest line exceeds =so-long-threshold= **or** the buffer's line count exceeds its match in =doom-file-lines-threshold-alist=. That alist is a single entry, =(("." . 15000))= — the pattern matches every file — while the docstring says the default is 20k. Measured: DONE.org is **37,036 lines**, longest line **1,422**, mean 52.6; =so-long-threshold= is 5,000. So the length test never fired and never could; the count test has been true for a long time. Running the predicate against the live buffer returns =t=.

*Two details that explain why it looks intermittent.* =org-mode= derives from =text-mode=, which is in =so-long-target-modes=, so org buffers are in scope at all. And =so-long-detected-p= is =permanent-local=, so it survives =revert-buffer= — the buffer can be back in =org-mode= with the flag still set, which is exactly the state observed.

*The urgent half is TODO.org, not DONE.org.* It is **13,366 lines** — roughly 1,600 under the same threshold, on a file that grows with every apply and every amend.

*Fix shape: raise the threshold for org files in =~/.config/doom/config.el=*, by adding an =\\.org\\'= entry to =doom-file-lines-threshold-alist= ahead of the ="."= catch-all. One line, no blast radius, and it is the user's file so it is theirs to apply. Per the org-dev skill this is =after!=/config territory — verify on a fresh Emacs, not a live reload.

*Splitting DONE.org by month was considered and is NOT this fix.* It would not help TODO.org, which is the nearer problem, and it costs real breakage: eleven scripts hard-code the two-file layout, =bin/hooks/footnote-check= among them, which resolves every cited id against exactly =$repo/TODO.org= and =$repo/DONE.org=. Split the archive and citations to older headings silently stop resolving. Revisit chunking on its own merits, not as a workaround for a threshold.

*Correction, 2026-09-17: the "urgent half" framing above overstated it, because TODO.org has a drain and this heading did not account for it.* The user pointed out that finished headings are archived into DONE.org each day tasks were closed. Measured rather than conceded: TODO.org is **13,455 lines**, of which 7 finished top-level headings hold **345 lines** — so today's pending archive would leave it at **13,110**, still about 1,900 short of the 15,000 threshold. The drain is real and roughly 2.6% of the file; it moves the date out, it does not remove the cliff.

*And the drain makes the global picture worse rather than better, which is the part worth keeping.* Archiving relocates those lines into DONE.org — already **37,036 lines**, 22,000 *past* the same threshold and permanently over it. So the one file with a drain empties into the one without. Nothing about the archive cycle fixes so-long for either file; only the Doom threshold does. That is an argument for the one-line config fix and against treating archiving, or monthly chunking, as the remedy.
:END:

Doom's =doom-so-long-p= drops any file past 15,000 lines out of its major mode, so DONE.org at 37,036 lines is not an Org buffer and every org operation against it is degraded rather than merely noisy. The trigger is line count, not line length — the longest line is 1,422 against a 5,000 threshold. Fix: one =doom-file-lines-threshold-alist= entry exempting =.org= from the count test while leaving the long-line test in force.


* TODO The unassigned-mark guard is conjunctive, so a clock item with no id marks and then fails at apply :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       653fea66-f9d5-4431-8ce3-5d355560b475
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 21:14]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:PLAN:

Observed 2026-09-16: an apply of 67 items reported "2 failed", both =Error: no org heading found with :ID: "nil" [clock unassigned …]=. The quotes are a red herring — the id is genuinely nil and =(format "… :ID: \"%s\"" id)= renders it that way.

=claude-code-ide-org--review-markable-p= refuses a mark only when the item is flagged =:unassigned= **and** its =:id= is nil: =(not (and (plist-get item :unassigned) (null (plist-get item :id))))=. A clock item with a nil id that does *not* carry the flag is therefore markable, and apply then looks a heading up by nil. Its own docstring names this outcome as the thing it exists to prevent — "refusing the mark is what makes that visible rather than letting apply fail later with a confusing error" — so the guard is not missing, it is one conjunction too narrow.

A nil id on a clock item is expected rather than exotic: =clock_out= carries no id, the same asymmetry that returned zero from the first orphan matcher earlier the same day. Proposal: test the id alone — an item with no heading cannot be applied whatever flags it carries — and say so in the refusal the way the unassigned-span branch already does. Worth checking at the same time whether =:unassigned= should be *derived* from a nil id rather than set alongside it, since two fields encoding one fact is what let them disagree.
:END:

=--review-markable-p= refuses a mark only when an item is flagged =:unassigned= **and** carries a nil =:id=, so a clock item with no id and no flag marks cleanly and then fails at apply with =no org heading found with :ID: "nil"= — the exact outcome the guard's own docstring says it exists to prevent. A nil id on a clock item is ordinary, since =clock_out= carries none. Proposal: test the id alone, and consider deriving =:unassigned= from it rather than storing both, since two fields encoding one fact is what let them disagree.


* TODO No tool retitles a heading or removes a property, so both reach for a raw emacsclient :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9714f875-4a12-44e4-8f30-dfbd1b6e555e
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 14:50]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:

Both gaps surfaced within a minute of each other while correcting =37bca83a=. Retitling has no tool at all: =org-edit-headline= exists in =config.el= but only inside the cookie inserters, so a rename goes through a hand =emacsclient= call, which binds nothing and dies on a read-only buffer — precisely the signal =org-mcp-tools.md= says means "the tool surface is missing something, not a licence to clear the flag". Removing a property has a near-miss: =org_set_property= accepts an empty value and *sets* it, leaving =:BLOCKER:= as a dangling empty line rather than deleting it, so lifting a blocker took a line-number =sed= and a buffer revert. Proposal: an =org_set_heading= (title only, keyword and tags untouched), and a delete mode on =org_set_property= — empty value should remove the line, since there is no case where an empty property says anything. Both are the shape =:ID:= 1cd2df62 and =420816ec= keep finding: the discouraged path stays cheaper than the preferred one until a tool closes it.
:END:

Nothing in the tool surface retitles a heading, and =org_set_property= sets an empty value rather than removing the property — so both operations fall back to a hand =emacsclient= call, which binds nothing and dies on a read-only buffer. Proposal: an =org_set_heading= that changes the title alone, and a delete mode on =org_set_property=, since an empty property says nothing worth storing.


* TODO org_outline's max_depth filters out the scoped heading itself, and the front matter reads as a result :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       80d8264e-5181-48a8-84e9-1c47c0c68956
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 14:50]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:

=max_depth= is an *absolute* level test, deliberately (=:ID:= 9f3c35c7 — it is what makes the argument mean the same thing for a file and for a subtree). Nothing handles the case where the scoped heading is itself deeper than the limit: =scope=0fa403f4= with no =max_depth= prints the heading, and the identical call with =max_depth=1= prints the =:CREATED:=/=:CATEGORY:= front-matter block and nothing else. DONE.org's datetree is where it bites, those entries sitting at level 7, and the output is indistinguishable from a heading that simply has no children — an empty result that reads as a fact about the corpus. It falsified =98908aff='s one passing claim about this knob, that "=max_depth= is safe by contrast: it only ever drops the deepest lines, truncating leaves rather than severing an ancestor"; severing the scope root is exactly what it does. The ancestor-preservation =--outline-map= gained for =active_only= does not reach it, because a scoped root has nothing kept beneath it to hold it in. Proposal: a scoped call always emits its scope heading, whatever =max_depth= says — the caller named it, so it is not a line they filtered out.
:END:

A scoped =org_outline= call whose heading sits deeper than =max_depth= filters out the scope itself and returns the front-matter block alone — indistinguishable from a heading with no children, so an empty result reads as a fact about the corpus. DONE.org's datetree is where it bites, those entries sitting at level 7. Proposal: a scoped call always emits the heading the caller named, whatever the depth limit says.


* TODO Rebuild the 101 truncated review-attention intervals from the audit log :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b5448133-4021-46b9-a90e-d88e149ae65d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 13:57]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Split out of [[id:e3d1531d-7562-4bad-bef2-fc819630c5f7][e3d1531d]] 2026-09-16 so it does not end up as residue inside a closed heading. That heading is the *code* fix — stop the clock being truncated; this is the *data* repair of the 101 intervals already written, and the two are independent enough that closing the first should not bury the second. **Do not start this before the truncation is fixed**: repairing history while the leak is open repairs downstream of it, and the next hundred intervals arrive wrong the same way. =2deb090f= got that order right. *What is reconstructible, measured.* Extending each interval to the **last =org_review_apply= record in the burst that closed it** — a moment the human demonstrably was still in the pass, since they were applying — moves the total from **9.05 h to 20.12 h, +11.07 h across 97 of 101 intervals**. The record currently understates review attention by more than half. *What is not.* The stretch between the last apply and the bury is unrecorded entirely: once apply closed the clock, =--attention-stop= writes nothing, because its annotation sits inside an =(org-clocking-p)= guard. So the reconstruction is a **floor**, and if it is written it must be labelled as one — an unlabelled floor is =7771fc63='s objection exactly, a plausible value being harder to reject than an absent one. *The evidence is durable but machine-local.* =.claude-code-ide-org-audit.jsonl= is append-only, never consumed and never rotated, so unlike =2deb090f='s consumed queue events this does not expire — but it is **gitignored**, so it lives only in this working copy and no push or clone carries it. That is a weaker guarantee than "permanent" and is the one thing that could force the decision early. *Open, and it is the same question the fix owes:* whether a repaired interval should end at the last apply (defensible floor), or whether the pass end should be reconstructed some other way, or whether the right answer is to leave the drawer alone and treat 9.05 h as a known undercount with this heading as its explanation. The third option is cheaper than it sounds and should not be dismissed.


* TODO The audit log answers attribution questions nobody asks it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e9ee1a50-085f-4c37-b064-4aec71cecb41
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 13:47]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Found 2026-09-16. =.claude-code-ide-org-audit.jsonl= holds **5053 records, 1.7 MB**, accumulated since August in the repo root (gitignored, =.gitignore:4=), and 2026-09-16 was the **first time anything ever read it**. It was built for one job — catching a mutation that did not reach disk, which is why each record carries before/after =sha256= and a =result= of =saved= or =UNSAVED-MISMATCH= — and it turns out to answer a second, larger class for free: **which tool changed which heading, when**. It settled [[id:e3d1531d-7562-4bad-bef2-fc819630c5f7][e3d1531d]] in one query after two purpose-built measurements had failed on instrument grounds, and it did so from data already on disk for weeks. *The gap is not more logging.* Four times in one session the answer was already built — =org-ql='s predicates behind a wrapper that dropped them, =--transcript-longer-sibling='s re-key detection, =--merge-time-intervals= duplicated as =--merge-overlapping-runs=, and now this. Adding instrumentation without a reader grows a file nobody queries; the standing habit to fix is reaching for construction before interrogation. *Proposed, in order of cheapness.* A one-line recipe in the org-dev skill — "before building a measurement, query the audit log" — with the two shapes that work: count by =tool= for a heading, and correlate a =timestamp= against an interval. Then, if it earns it, a small reader: =claude-code-ide-org-audit-report= taking an =:ID:= and returning tool counts and a timeline, so the query is a command rather than a =python3= one-liner reconstructed each time. *One real limitation, stated so the reader is not over-trusted.* It records that a tool touched a heading, never what it did to it. =e3d1531d= was answerable only because apply has no reason to touch =Review attention= except by closing its clock — an inference from the mechanism, not from the record. A log entry naming the *operation* would make that class of question direct rather than lucky, and that is the one place more instrumentation is genuinely warranted.


* TODO The review-attention clock stops at the first apply, so 101 intervals measure the wrong thing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e3d1531d-7562-4bad-bef2-fc819630c5f7
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 13:26]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:26]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:33] =>  0:07
- <2026-09-16 Wed 13:26>--<2026-09-16 Wed 13:33> assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:33]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:34] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-16 Wed 13:33]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:34] bound the evidence for the truncation claim
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:34]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:43] =>  0:09
- <2026-09-16 Wed 13:34>--<2026-09-16 Wed 13:43> assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:43]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:44] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-16 Wed 13:43]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:44] confirmed from the existing audit log
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:46]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:48] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-16 Wed 13:46]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:48] confirmed from the existing audit log
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:48]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:57] =>  0:09
- <2026-09-16 Wed 13:48>--<2026-09-16 Wed 13:57> assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 13:57]--[2026-09-16 Wed 13:57] =>  0:00
- <2026-09-16 Wed 13:57>--<2026-09-16 Wed 13:57> split the retcon out; record the fix shape
:END:

Found 2026-09-16 when the user asked why only some =Review attention= LOGBOOK entries carry annotations. The annotations were the symptom; the clock is the defect. **=--attention-start= runs once, when the review buffer is created, and nothing ever restarts it.** =--review-write-one-interval= opens with "Close any running clock FIRST" — =(when (org-clocking-p) (org-clock-out nil t))= — and during a pass the running clock *is* the attention clock. So every attention interval measures **buffer opened → first interval applied**, not the pass. Of 101 CLOCK lines most read 0:01–0:05, while the handful that ran long (1:12, 1:01, 0:48, 0:29) are the passes that applied late or not at all — which is the signature, not a coincidence. The full taxonomy, measured against the drawer and confirmed against the code: **(1) a CLOCK line with no annotation — 86 of them** — the clock was closed by something other than =--attention-stop=, overwhelmingly apply; =--attention-stop= then finds =(org-clocking-p)= nil and writes nothing, since its annotation is inside that guard. **(2) an annotation matching its CLOCK line — 7** — the pass lasted a minute or more and ended by burying with its clock *intact*, which means no clock interval was applied during it. **(3) a zero-width annotation with no CLOCK line — 8** — the pass opened and was buried **within the same minute**: the clock existed and was closed normally, but =org-clock-out-remove-zero-time-clocks= is =t= (verified live) so org discarded the CLOCK line, while the annotation survived because =--append-to-drawer= writes it separately, and both its timestamps round to the same minute. Note what this rules out: shape (3) is **not** "started a review and quit without applying" — that is shape (2). The discriminator is *duration under one minute*, not whether =x= was pressed. *Backfilling annotations is the wrong remedy and would make things worse*: it would render a complete-looking record over intervals that are themselves truncated, which is the shape of the 850 uncounted minutes on =2deb090f= — present, readable, and not what it appears. *Shape of the fix, not chosen.* Either apply saves and restores the attention clock around each write, or attention is suspended for the duration of an apply and resumed after, or the pass is measured by something other than a native clock. The first fragments one pass into many CLOCK lines; the second needs a definition of when an apply ends; the third reopens =961f15b6='s decision. **What a review pass *is* has to be decided before the measurement can be fixed** — it is the same question as where the pass ends, which =961f15b6= answered for burying and never for applying.

*Correction the same day: **this heading's title overstates what is
established**, and the user's question is what exposed it.*

Asked whether concurrent agent activity might be stealing the clock instead.
**It cannot**, and that half is answerable by inspection: every native clock
call in the system is =--trigger-auto-clock-in= (fires on a *hand* =C-c C-t=,
and apply binds =--auto-clock-in-active= to suppress it), the apply path,
=--attention-start=/=--attention-stop=, and =--clock-out-if-clocking= on
=kill-emacs-hook=/=suspend-hook=. None is reachable from an MCP tool.
=org_clock_in= queues and opens nothing, and since the cutover no hook reaches
Emacs at all. A concurrent session cannot close this clock.

*But asking the question properly meant trying to measure it, and the
measurement does not support the title.* Two attempts, both failing on
instrument grounds rather than returning a negative:

- *Correlating attention-clock ends against other headings' CLOCK lines is
  **invalid**.* Apply writes **backdated** intervals — both endpoints supplied
  up front from when the work happened — so an apply-written line carries
  timestamps nowhere near the moment of applying. The 34 of 101 that appeared
  to match are noise.
- *Correlating against real apply-**pass** timestamps gives **0 testable
  cases**.* Only 17 such stamps exist, none overlaps an attention interval, and
  most watermarks predate the field
  ([[id:21c91613-76e0-4590-affe-e2af9f1321ca][21c91613]]) entirely.

*So the honest position:* the code path is real — =--attention-start= runs once
and =--review-write-one-interval= closes any running clock with nothing
restarting it — which establishes apply as **a** cause. It does not establish
it as **the** cause, and **what closes the other intervals is unknown**.
=kill-emacs-hook=, a hand clock-in elsewhere, and =org-clock-out-when-done= (set
=t= in the Doom config) are all candidates and nothing here separates them.

*One adjacent agent-caused risk is real and already filed:*
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][53b0047d]] — a direct write to a live
org buffer can desync =org-clock-marker=. That corrupts *where* a clock-out
lands rather than *whether* one happens, so it does not explain a closed
interval, but it is the one way agent activity touches this machinery at all.

*What would actually settle it* is instrumenting the clock-out rather than
inferring it: record which path closed the attention clock at the moment it
closes. That is a one-line addition wherever the clock is stopped and it turns
the whole question into a lookup — the same move
=21c91613= made for apply, and the same move
[[id:33c3eabc-00f2-4cf1-afb7-4071aa256d62][33c3eabc]] made for the footnote
hook. Until then this heading should be read as a hypothesis with a mechanism,
not a diagnosis.

*Confirmed 2026-09-16, and the correction above is itself corrected. The
evidence existed the whole time, in a file nothing had ever read.*

=.claude-code-ide-org-audit.jsonl= records which tool mutated which heading.
Queried for this heading: **246 records — 138 =hand-edit=, 108
=org_review_apply=**. Apply has no reason to touch =Review attention= at all
except by closing its clock. Correlated against the drawer: **95 of 101
attention clock ENDS coincide with an =org_review_apply= record on this heading,
6 with =hand-edit= only, and 0 with no record at all.**

*So this is a diagnosis after all, not a hypothesis.* The hedge added an hour
earlier was right about my two failed measurements and wrong about the
conclusion — I treated "my instruments cannot see it" as "it is not
established", when a third instrument was already on disk with five thousand
records in it. Both errors are worth keeping: the overstatement, then the
overcorrection.

**What the truncation costs, measured.** Extending each interval to the *last*
=org_review_apply= record in the burst that closed it — a moment the human
demonstrably *was* still in the pass, since they were applying — gives:

| | |
|---+---|
| currently recorded | **9.05 h** |
| extended to last apply | **20.12 h** |
| difference | **+11.07 h**, across 97 of 101 intervals |

So the record understates review attention by **more than half**.

*Can the drawer be retconned? Partially, and it should not be yet.* The
extension above is a **floor**, not the truth: it is evidentially grounded at
its new end, but the stretch between the last apply and the bury is unrecorded
entirely — once apply closed the clock, =--attention-stop= writes nothing. A
retcon would produce 97 rewritten CLOCK lines that are *less wrong and still
wrong*, and would then read as authoritative, which is =7771fc63='s standing
objection to a plausible value over an absent one.

**The decisive argument is order: the mechanism still truncates.** Repairing
history while the leak is open repairs downstream of it, and the next hundred
intervals arrive wrong. =2deb090f= got this order right — it stopped the leak
with =c= and declined the history.

*But unlike that case, deferring costs nothing, and the asymmetry is worth
naming.* =2deb090f='s history is unrecoverable because consumed queue events are
never re-offered. This evidence is **append-only and permanent**: the audit log
is not consumed, not rotated, and not in git's way. The retcon is available
whenever it is wanted, on exactly the same terms, so there is no deadline
forcing the decision now.

*The data repair is split out as
[[id:b5448133-4021-46b9-a90e-d88e149ae65d][b5448133]]*, so closing this heading
cannot bury it. This one is the code fix.

*And the fix has a smaller shape than the three options above suggested.* Those
were "save and restore around each write", "suspend attention for the apply", or
"measure the pass some other way" — the first fragmenting one pass into many
CLOCK lines, the second needing a definition of when an apply ends, the third
reopening [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]].

**None of that is necessary, because org can reopen a clock at its original
start.** =--review-write-one-interval= already calls =(org-clock-in nil start)=
two lines below the clock-out that causes this — supplying a start time is a
call the file makes to itself. So the repair is: before closing the running
clock, note its marker and =org-clock-start-time=; after the interval is
written, reopen it **at the original start**. The pass then yields **one**
CLOCK line covering it end to end, exactly as if apply had never intervened,
and no new concept of "when an apply ends" is needed.

*Two things to check when building it, neither obvious.* Whether reopening
re-enters =--trigger-auto-clock-in= (it is bound off during apply, so probably
not, but the binding's scope should be confirmed rather than assumed). And
whether a pass with *many* applied intervals ends up closing and reopening
dozens of times — correct but noisy, and worth stashing once for the whole
apply rather than per interval if so.

*Note what this does not fix:* the annotation still only fires when a clock is
running at bury, but once the clock survives the apply that condition is met,
so the 86 unannotated intervals stop accumulating as a side effect rather than
needing their own change.

*Correction, 2026-09-24.* The description above of the audit log as "append-only and permanent … not in git's way" overstates it. =b5448133= established that the log is gitignored, so it exists only in this working copy: a fresh clone or a deleted checkout loses it, and with it the source for rebuilding the 101 intervals. Found by the twin pass that judged these two headings to be deliberate siblings, not twins.


* DOING The complement's gap spans carry no events, so half the review buffer cannot be dismissed :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       445fe577-01ce-4e68-ab5f-c349c40a4643
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 09:24]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:BLOCKER:  5a9d877e-b492-46c7-a672-fd5971ffb0b0
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-16 Wed 18:29]--[2026-09-16 Wed 18:34] =>  0:05
- [2026-09-16 Wed 18:29]--[2026-09-16 Wed 18:34]
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-09-17 Thu 10:14] \\
  blocking the review pass; dismissal by bounds
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:17]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:18] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:17]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:18]
