- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:24]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:26] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:28]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:31] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:28]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:31]
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:33]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:34] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:33]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:34]
CLOCK: [2026-09-17 Thu 10:34]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:34] =>  0:00
- [2026-09-17 Thu 10:34]--[2026-09-17 Thu 10:34] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:

Regression introduced by [[id:c54c4215-90e3-4e0c-9de1-990c0406a2b2][c54c4215]]'s complement, found 2026-09-16 when the user asked what the undismissable items were. **7 of 14 currently offered items carry no =:events=**, and =claude-code-ide-org-review-dismiss= refuses them: "Nothing to dismiss: this item carries no queue events". The refusal is correct on its own terms — dismissal works by marking an item's backing events consumed, and with none there is nothing to record, so refusing beats appearing to work. *What they are, confirmed against the raw queue rather than inferred:* every one is bounded by a =clock_out= at its start and a =clock_in= at its end — the gap after one heading's clock closed and before the next one's opened. They are exactly what this heading's parent set out to surface, and they carry no events **by construction**: their boundaries are bracket *edges*, not guideposts, and any guidepost inside them would sit inside a bracket and so already be owned. *The trade I made without noticing.* Before the complement, a split emitted zero-width *endpoints* sitting on real guideposts — those had events and could be dismissed. The complement replaced them with real-duration gaps that cannot. I measured the minutes recovered (12.64 h) and never checked what happened to reviewability; half the buffer is now undismissable. That is =355fa608='s "offered forever" in new clothes, and that guard does not reach it because it keys on *zero width* while these have real duration. *Two consequences, one benign.* =d= is unavailable, so the only exits are accepting the item or applying the sibling segments that share the cluster's guideposts, which dissolves the cluster and takes the gap with it. Applying is at least idempotent — =--recorded-intervals-at-point= subtracts what is already written, so a replay writes nothing and there is no double-count. *Shape of the fix, not chosen.* Either dismissal keys on the span's bounds rather than its events, which means a dismissal record that is not per-event and so a second shape in the watermark file; or the item carries its bounding =clock_out=/=clock_in= as read-only =:events= it may reference but must never consume, since consuming them would break the brackets they belong to. The first is more honest about what is being dismissed; the second reuses the existing path. Worth deciding against the 86-minute example the user surfaced (=0086614a= -> =c60a1c53=), where the gap is large enough that dismissing it is a real choice rather than tidying.

*Reproduced in batch 2026-09-16, and it carries a second consequence worse than the first* — found by the PR #25 review, confirmed here rather than taken on trust. Two owned brackets at 10:01--10:05 and 10:06--10:10, guideposts at 10:00 and 10:11, threshold 1200:

: spans emitted: 3
:   10:00:00--10:01:00  events=1  kinds-known=t
:   10:05:00--10:06:00  events=0  kinds-known=nil
:   10:10:00--10:11:00  events=1  kinds-known=t

The middle span's endpoints are *bracket edges*, so no guidepost lies within it and =--span-events= returns nil. Dismissal needs a queue item to mark, which is the half already filed. The new half is =claude-code-ide-org--span-kinds-known-p=: it is =(and (seq-find ...) t)= over the event list, so an empty list answers nil, and its own docstring says what apply then does — "turns back into the single whole-span line it wrote before this existed". So accepting such an item writes **one CLOCK line for the whole envelope**, with no run structure, because there are no kinds to read.

*Severity is narrower than it first reads, and worth stating so the fix is not overscoped.* Emitting the gap is the feature =c54c4215= shipped: the minute between two owned brackets is genuinely unaccounted, and offering it is the point. Nor is a whole-envelope line wrong for a one-minute gap. The sharp edge is a *large* gap, where writing the envelope whole claims every idle minute in it as work — which is precisely what =claude-code-ide-org-span-idle-floor= exists to prevent, and the run-splitting that would enforce it is disabled by the same empty list.

*So the two halves want one fix, not two.* Whatever gives a gap span a queue item to dismiss is also what would give it events to split on. Attaching the bracket endpoints themselves as synthetic =block_start=/=block_end= events is the obvious shape and is not yet argued — it would make =kinds-known-p= true and the runs computable, at the cost of inventing events, which this project has refused before.

*Measured 2026-09-17, and the title understates it.* Of nine spans offered for yesterday afternoon, **eight carry zero events**; across the whole pending set it is thirteen of fifteen. The two exceptions are instructive: the ones that still report minutes are those whose guideposts have *not* been consumed by an apply — =13:58--14:15=, which survived only because it was one of the three items that failed with =:ID: "nil"=, and today's =09:58= and =10:08=.

*What is actually being offered.* =[2026-09-16 21:14]--[2026-09-17 08:40]=, eleven hours overnight, suggested against =489d6f61= because that heading held the last clock before it. =[18:34]--[21:14]=, two hours forty. =[15:17]--[18:28]=, the AFK stretch. These are not gaps *within* a span of activity — they are the interval between every adjacent pair of clock brackets, emitted whether or not a single turn occurred inside.

*That is not what =c54c4215= set out to recover*, and its own title says so: "the unowned gaps between the clock pairs **that split it**" — gaps interior to a span that real guideposts had formed. An inter-bracket interval containing no guidepost was never part of any span, so there is nothing in it that was previously dropped.

*Consequences, all of which the user hit in one morning.* Such an item cannot be dismissed (no events to record against), reports no minutes, runs or turns (=--review-written-summary= short-circuits on =--span-kinds-known-p=), writes no CLOCK line if applied while =:suggested=, consumes no watermark, and so returns at every pass forever. Where it inherits a suggested heading it looks answerable and is not.

*Fix direction, stated but not implemented.* Emit a complement piece only when it carries at least one guidepost — the same conservative shape =355fa608= used for the stranded single point, and with the same justification recorded there: "Nothing is lost: the guidepost itself stays in the queue file, which is the durable record. Only the *proposal* is dropped." Dismissal-by-bounds, considered earlier today, treats the symptom and would leave the human retiring overnight gaps one at a time forever.

*Not yet established, and it should be before the fix lands:* whether an eleven-hour overnight interval can form a cluster at all, or whether these pieces escape clustering entirely. The =resume= -> =pause= exemption never splits however long the gap, and an interrupted turn emits no =pause= — so a crashed session may be leaving exactly the adjacency that lets a cluster span a night.

*Mechanism settled 2026-09-17 by probe, and it is upstream of everything recorded above.* Running =--aggregate-guideposts= on the orphan guideposts **with no exclusions at all** yields clusters spanning days:

: 08-24 12:33..08-31 18:13  inside=7
: 09-02 15:41..09-03 13:17  inside=3
: 09-16 13:58..09-17 10:27  inside=35

Seven days holding seven guideposts. No gap threshold of 1200 s can produce that, so the =resume= -> =pause= exemption is what joins them — "a turn *running*, so it never splits however long it is". The premise fails on the **orphan stream**: two events are adjacent there only because everything between them was bracketed and attributed elsewhere, so the exemption reads a days-long hole as one continuous turn. Adjacency in a filtered stream is not consecutiveness in time.

*The code half-knows this already.* The comment above the call warns that two orphans "are *adjacent* in the orphan stream, because everything between them is bracketed… A =resume= -> =pause= adjacency never splits however long the gap, so the span would straddle all four brackets by construction". The mitigation chosen was to subtract the brackets afterwards — which removes the bracketed intervals but leaves the cluster straddling days, and the complement then carves that into inter-bracket pieces. Those pieces are the event-less spans measured above. So the complement is the messenger, not the cause.

*Fix shape, with a location:* the exemption should require the two events be adjacent in the **full** event stream rather than in the filtered one the function was handed — the caller knows both, the callee currently sees only one. No invented threshold and no new storage, and it restores =c54c4215='s complement to what its own title says: the gaps between clock pairs *that split a span*, not the interval between every adjacent pair of brackets in the corpus.

*Probe limitation, stated because it bounds the claim:* only three brackets survive in the live queue, the rest consumed by applies, so step 2 of the probe could not reproduce the current item list and is inconclusive. Step 1 needs no brackets and is what the finding rests on. A synthetic fixture is what the fix's test should use.


* TODO Should a short 0-run span default to claimed, and where does the bound fall? :research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a865f83c-ea37-40c4-a104-7cc50d2c20dd
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 08:44]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Asked by the user 2026-09-16, straight after =c= shipped: "will the 0-run/0-minute spans offered always be mine to claim? if so, why not offer them as such?" The first half is answered and the answer is **no** — [[id:996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3][996fd2bd]]'s known negative settles it, the 2026-08-25 16:56--17:50 sleeping-laptop gap being byte-identical in shape to an hour of careful reviewing. A =pause=--=resume= gap says only that the *agent* was idle. So a blanket default-to-claimed trades a silent undercount for a silent overcount, and gains nothing in visibility: a wrong CLOCK line looks exactly like a right one, so the error is no more catchable than the one it replaces. **But the population actually on offer makes the question narrower and more answerable than that.** Measured the same day: 13 zero-run spans totalling 27 minutes, envelopes of 8, 5, 3, 3, 2, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1 and 1 minutes — median one minute. At one minute, "I walked away" is not a competing explanation the way it is at 54; the human had just been handed output and the next turn started a minute later. So the arguable proposal is a **width-bounded** default: claim automatically below some envelope, ask above it. Three things to settle, none of them mine to decide. **Where the bound falls is a reporting decision**, and this project has form for naming such a knob without turning it (=claude-code-ide-org-span-minimum-interval=, "a named no-op, deliberately"). **Whether a defaulted claim should be marked as such** — a =(claimed)= line says a human asserted it, and a defaulted one did not, so it may want a third rendering rather than borrowing =c='s. **And whether it is worth it at all**: the entire exposure is 27 minutes, so this can wait behind anything with a larger number. Note the interaction with =c= itself: =c= exists precisely so the assertion is an act rather than an inaction, and a default undoes that property for the range it covers — which is the strongest argument against, and the reason this is filed as a question rather than a plan.


* DOING org_query silently text-searches a sexp query, so the wrong language reads as no matches :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       37bca83a-e83b-4070-9058-6318a58a24db
:CREATED:  [2026-09-16 Wed 06:41]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:
*The design, approved 2026-09-23 (the user), brainstormed on the bounded path.* =org_query= keeps one language, org-ql's plain strings (the user, 2026-09-21). What its parser does not recognise it now refuses, instead of silently turning it into full-text terms that come back as an honest-looking "No matches." No predicate is dropped and none ever was, so surfacing the misreading is the fix.

*Three refusals, none counted as a miss* (the user, 2026-09-23). The refusal table's own rule, from =63713df3='s review, is that a query-language slip is not a miss, because the caller broke no rule it had been given, and the refusal already removes the harm. So nothing is added to =queue-append='s table. Each refusal is an =Error:= naming what was wrong and what to write instead.
- A sexp: a query whose first non-blank character is =(=. The refusal offers the plain-string equivalent where one is obvious (=(tags "x")= becomes =tags:x=, =(property "K" "v")= becomes =property:K,v=), and otherwise names the mini-language. A sexp is never evaluated. =4ae3cdef= kept org-ql's sexp language unreachable because it amounts to arbitrary elisp on a localhost HTTP endpoint, and a validated sexp subset was considered and not filed (the user, 2026-09-23).
- An unknown predicate: a bare =name:= or =!name:= token whose name is not in org-ql's own registry (=org-ql-predicates= and its aliases). The parser turns such a token into a full-text term, so =kind:slice= silently searched for the literal string (measured in batch 2026-09-23). The list is read at run time, never kept by hand, so an org-ql upgrade cannot make it stale. A quoted token such as ="kind:slice"= is still searched as literal text.
- A bare =OR=, =AND= or =|=. The parser turns each into a full-text term, so =todo:NEXT OR tags:spike= narrows instead of widening (measured live 2026-09-23). The refusal explains that a comma gives OR within one predicate, and points at =match=.

*A =match= argument* (the user, 2026-09-23): =all=, the default and today's AND, or =any=. It is passed as the combining operator to =org-ql--query-string-to-sexp=, which already takes one. It gives flat OR only; grouping stays unavailable.

*Show the reading.* An empty result names the parsed query, for instance =No matches for (and (todo "next") (tags "CODE"))=, and says that =todo:=, =tags:= and =property:= match case exactly while =heading:= and bare words do not. So a misreading the refusals miss is still visible.

*Description rewrite*, in the tool's =:description= and its =query= argument, where it is read at the moment of the call. It covers:
- bare words as full-text search (=rifle=, over the entry and its outline path);
- =regexp:= and =property:KEY,VALUE=, the documented form (=KEY=VALUE= parses to a keyword argument and works only by accident);
- a trailing colon for zero-argument predicates (=blocked:=, =done:=), with the caution that =blocked= also counts unfinished children and unchecked checkboxes, not only =:BLOCKER:=. That closes =5acca91b=;
- =parent:=, =children:=, =ancestors:= and =descendants:=, which take text rather than nested predicates, since =children:todo:NEXT= parses to =(children "todo:NEXT")=;
- the boolean rules: space is AND, comma is OR within one predicate, =!= is NOT, and there is no cross-predicate OR except =match=any=;
- which parts are case-sensitive, and that DONE.org is searched too.

*Testing*, in =config-test.el=: one case per refusal; known predicates and aliases passing; a quoted literal passing; =match=any= returning the union; the reading shown on an empty result. Each is shown to fail against HEAD's version. Then a live reload, and the refused queries of 2026-09-22 and 2026-09-23 re-run through the tool.

Files: =config.el= (=claude-code-ide-org-query=, its docstring, its registration and the section commentary) and =config-test.el=.
:END:
:LOGBOOK:

:END:

=org_query= takes org-ql's plain-string language, and whatever that parser does not recognise becomes a full-text term. That covers a sexp, an unknown predicate such as =kind:slice=, and a bare =OR=. Each then comes back "No matches." and looks exactly like an honest empty result. Designed 2026-09-23 on the bounded path: refuse all three, add =match=any= for OR across predicates, show the parsed reading on an empty result, and rewrite the description so it states the language as it really is.

*Established:*
- The wrapper drops no predicate. For ten hours this heading claimed it did, but every measurement had compared a sexp passed to =org_query= with the same sexp given to =org-ql-select=, which is two different languages. That claim, and the block on e28933e9 that rested on it, were withdrawn 2026-09-16.
- A wrong-language empty result cost real work: it was read as "no slice carries =:KIND:=" and re-answered with a regex scan of heading boundaries.
- The schema under-described the language. Bare =todo:= and =property:= were added to it on 2026-09-22 after a session enumerated keywords and silently dropped two =MAYBE= headings.
- Measured 2026-09-23 against the installed org-ql: =todo:=, =tags:= and =property:= are case-sensitive, while =heading:= and bare words are not; =property:KEY,VALUE= is the documented form; there is no cross-predicate OR; and =parent:=/=children:= take text, not nested predicates.


* TODO The re-key bridge reads transcripts Claude Code prunes, so the alias decays silently :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       4acd8ad0-4d26-420b-bf84-f66ae8a9dfc5
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 18:38]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Split out of b57c7515 on 2026-09-15 rather than built there. That heading's fix reconciles a mid-work session re-key by grouping sessions whose transcripts share a first-entry =uuid= — measured exact over the corpus, 7 groups, 14 of 65 sessions. But the signal lives in =~/.claude/projects/*/*.jsonl=, which Claude Code prunes on its own schedule (=cleanupPeriodDays=), and the queue events carry no conversation key of their own. So the alias is exact for recent work and degrades to nothing for old work, with no symptom at the boundary — a re-key whose transcripts have aged out simply looks like two unrelated sessions again, which is the failure this whole line of work exists to remove. Proposed: have =queue-append= record a stable conversation key on the event itself (the transcript's first =uuid=, or the =transcript_path= the hook payload already carries), so the bridge stops depending on a file someone else garbage-collects. Note the constraint that shapes it: the hook family is plain POSIX shell precisely so a stopped Emacs costs nothing, so whatever is recorded must be one cheap read of the transcript's first line, not a parse.


* TODO [0/4] No org tool edits a phrase in place: org_amend appends or replaces the whole body :code:arch:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       dd4b9a9f-ba8a-4f1f-a3da-38388e0cdb76
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 13:25]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:
Designed 2026-09-23 through the brainstorming skill, classified =arch=:
bounded at first, stepped up when retiring =org_amend= in the same move
made it an interface every session and three hooks depend on.

*A new tool, =org_edit=, replaces =org_amend= in one move* (the user,
2026-09-23). Its arguments are the =Edit= tool's: =id=, =old_string=,
=new_string=, and an optional =drawer=. The familiar names are the
cheapest defence against invented arguments (=bbf9fb77=). There is no
=note= (the user, 2026-09-23): once the queue path goes, its only reader
would be the miss counter, which reads who caught a miss from the line
itself instead.

*The contract.* The region is the heading's own body, below its drawers
and above its first child, or with =drawer= set to =PLAN= or =DEBRIEF=,
the inside of that drawer. An empty =old_string= appends =new_string= at
the end of the region, creating the drawer when absent, and a list item
continues a list as it does today. A non-empty =old_string= must occur
exactly once in the region and is replaced. If it is absent, the call is
refused naming it; if it occurs more than once, it is refused with the
count. A whole-region rewrite is the case where =old_string= is the
region's full text. It takes no flag and needs no commit first, because
the replaced text travels in the call and the reply echoes what changed.

*The guards*, which refuse even when the text is found once. A match
counts only inside the region, so one spanning into a drawer or a child
is absent. Only =:PLAN:= and =:DEBRIEF:= are editable drawers. A finished
heading's =:PLAN:= is read-only, being the record of what was intended;
its body and =:DEBRIEF:= stay editable. An edit touching a slice's
=Planned:= checklist or =Incidental:= section is refused, since the
refresh owns those lines (=7ee3b71a=). A =new_string= line starting with
an asterisk at column zero outside a block is refused rather than
escaped, since it would become a heading (=c0c5e015=). Id links are
resolved as =org_amend= resolves them: a prefix is expanded and an
unresolvable id refused.

*An unsaved buffer is refused, and the agent waits on the save* (the
user, 2026-09-23). The waiting cannot happen inside the tool: Emacs is
single-threaded and the call runs in it, so a tool that polled would
freeze the editor the human needs in order to save, a deadlock. So
=org_edit= refuses at once, with a reply distinct from its permanent
refusals, and sets a header line on the buffer asking the human to save,
cleared by an after-save hook. It stays visible until acted on and is
never modal. The agent then runs a wait script shipped with the plugin,
which polls =buffer-modified-p= on the Fibonacci schedule (1, 1, 2, 3, 5,
8 s, twenty seconds in all) and exits on the save or at the cap. Run in
the background, its exit wakes the agent to retry. Past the cap, the
agent ends its turn saying what it is waiting for. It never falls through
to =Edit=. This is =ec1e6839='s mechanism, built here with =org_edit= as
its first user.

*Retiring =org_amend=.* Its registration and entry point go; the
internals =org_edit= reuses stay. =queue-append= loses its deferred-amend
branch, and its miss attribution moves to =org_edit=, reading "Caught by
the user" or "Self-caught" from the first line of =new_string= and
falling back to "unmarked". =clock-target-check=, =settings.json= and
=hooks.json= swap the name. =--review-apply-amend= and the review
buffer's amend items stay as a legacy reader for amend events already
queued. The prose moves too: both skills, the four promoted rules
regenerated from their references, =CLAUDE.md=, =next-session.md=, and
every reply string in =config.el= that tells a caller to use
=org_amend=, all inside a word budget with 27 words of headroom. Live
headings naming =org_amend= are renamed with =org_edit= itself, its first
real use; closed headings and =plans/= stay as history. A session that
started before the change and then calls =org_amend= hangs until
=ce012ad4= or =ecbbec2e= lands. Such sessions are few and short-lived,
so neither blocks this (the user, 2026-09-23).

*Four children, in order.* First =org_edit= and its guards. Second the
wait on save, blocked by the first. Third the retirement, blocked by
both, since without the wait an append against unsaved changes would be
refused with no prompt and sessions would fall back to =Edit=. Fourth the
deletion of the legacy amend reader, blocked by the third, and due only
once no amend event remains in any queue. =c0c5e015= and =5b46fbfd= are
blocked by the third, their defects being =org_amend='s; =ec1e6839= is
blocked by the second.

*Verification.* ERT covers each behaviour above:

- append to a body and to an absent drawer;
- a single replacement, and the absent and ambiguous refusals;
- a whole-region rewrite that keeps the separation before the next
  heading (=5b46fbfd=);
- a spanning match, and both org-managed drawers;
- a finished =:PLAN:= against its =:DEBRIEF:=, and the slice checklist;
- a headline-shaped line, and id resolution;
- the temporary refusal, with its header line set and then cleared.

Each refusal test is shown to fail against =org_edit= with that guard
removed. Shell tests cover miss attribution, the absence of a deferred
event, and the wait script against a fake =emacsclient= whose buffer
saves and one whose never does. =check-brainstorming-skill= then refuses
any =org_amend= left in that skill once the registration is gone.

*Ruled out.* A forwarding stub for =org_amend= (the user, 2026-09-23): a
fresh session is only offered registered tools, so only sessions older
than the change could call it. Queueing a replacement while the buffer is
unsaved: it would have to find its old text again at apply time, after
the human may have edited that very sentence, and it would change the
queue event, the apply pass and the review buffer. A =replace= flag that
rewrites a drawer without the caller naming the text it replaces, where a
literal replacement cannot lose what it was not pointed at. Keeping
=note=.
:END:

- [2026-09-23 Wed 10:33]--[2026-09-23 Wed 10:35] record the org_edit design, its children and blockers

No org tool edits text in place: =org_amend= appends, or replaces a whole
body, so a one-sentence correction in a body or in a written =:PLAN:=
goes behind Emacs or not at all. On 2026-09-15 the fallback was a
hand-written =emacsclient= search-and-replace that had to bind
=inhibit-read-only= itself, and on 2026-09-22 three sessions edited
=TODO.org= around Emacs for want of it. The design, in =:PLAN:=, is a new
=org_edit= with the =Edit= tool's =old_string= and =new_string=, over a
heading's body or its =:PLAN:= or =:DEBRIEF:=. It appends, edits one
occurrence, or rewrites the region; it waits on the human's save rather
than queueing; and it retires =org_amend= in the same move, carried by
four children in order.

The new tool, with =id=, =old_string=, =new_string= and an optional =drawer=, over the heading's body or its =:PLAN:= or =:DEBRIEF:=. An empty =old_string= appends, a unique one is replaced, and the region's full text rewrites it. It carries the guards in the parent's =:PLAN:=, and each refusal test is shown to fail with its guard removed. It ships beside =org_amend=, which the third sibling retires.

A wait script shipped with the plugin polls =buffer-modified-p= on the Fibonacci schedule and exits on the save or after twenty seconds. The agent runs it in the background and retries when it exits. * TODO Nothing answers who blocks whom; :BLOCKER: is a forward index with no inverse :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0eecf8e7-a8d5-4da7-9d6d-f397542b2139
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 11:10]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

A :BLOCKER: on X names Y, so "what is X waiting for" is one property read, while
"what is waiting on Y" requires scanning every :BLOCKER: value in both files and
inverting. org_outline already marks [blocked: id ...] per read; the mirror,
[blocks: id ...], would answer the other direction from the same scan. The user
proposed a :blocker: tag alongside a :blocked: one for this, and a tag is the
wrong shape for the reason a7f4b40c cancelled the first: blocked-ness is
derivable and a tag is stored, so it goes stale the moment the last blocker
finishes -- and a pair of tags must be maintained in step with each other AND
with the property, which is three copies of one fact. Derive it per read
instead.


* TODO org_query cannot ask what is blocked, though org-ql has the predicate :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5acca91b-b7bf-4046-9379-cd9a8f40f116
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 11:10]
:CATEGORY: Tools

Closes with =37bca83a='s work (the user, 2026-09-23), with nothing built of its own. The predicate is already reachable: =blocked:= with a trailing colon parses to =(blocked)=, and =!blocked:= to =(not (blocked))=. Only a bare =blocked= falls through to full-text search, which is what the 2026-09-15 measurement compared (measured in batch 2026-09-23). =37bca83a='s description rewrite teaches the colon form and carries this heading's caution: =blocked= is the union of unfinished children, unchecked checkboxes and =:BLOCKER:=. Verify after that reload by running =blocked:= live and checking that it returns the 10 headings the sexp returned, not the 26 of the text search.
:END:

org-ql defines a blocked predicate and org_query does not expose it. Its parser
documents todo:, tags:, priority: and heading:, so a bare "blocked" falls
through to full-text search: measured 2026-09-15 over TODO.org, the sexp
(blocked) returns 10 headings and the plain string returns 26, the difference
being bodies that merely mention the word. Proposed: pass blocked (and its
negation) through, which is the whole change. Worth pairing with a caution in
the tool description, because the derived answer is a union of three mechanisms
-- unfinished children, unchecked checkboxes, and :BLOCKER: via org-depend --
and a reader asking "what is blocked" usually means only the third.


* TODO The attention report renders to a buffer and persists nothing, so every reading is thrown away :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       8070db26-b11a-4008-9fb9-5dcd017d798f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-15 Tue 10:53]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:
:PLAN:
*Four questions, and the order is roughly their difficulty.*

**1. Where does an interval live?** Three candidates, each with a real
objection. *On the slice heading* — but a slice carries no clock by
convention, and though an attention interval is not a clock, putting
durations on a grouping re-creates the ambiguity =64d34a64= measured, a
parent's own share indistinguishable from its subtree's. *On the
meta-work datetree's day node* — it is already the place a day's record
gathers, already dated, and =Review and planning= is already half the
label vocabulary; but a slice-labelled interval is not meta-work and
would sit there under a foreign parent. *In its own file* — clean, and
it puts the agenda's view of the human's day outside the tracker, which
may be right or may be exactly the split that makes it get forgotten.

**2. Written, or recomputed on demand?** Recomputation is always
current and always loses the human's corrections, which is the one
thing the derivation cannot supply. Writing keeps them and creates the
idempotency problem =295cde3a= met head-on: a second run must recognise
what it wrote last time rather than duplicate it.

**3. How is an attested interval distinguished from a derived one?**
This is the contract =4f8500e6= settled — *propose what is measured,
ask for what is not* — and it survives only if a reader can tell which
is which after the fact. A corrected interval and a freshly proposed
one must not render identically; a marker is the cheap answer, and
=:CLOSED_SOURCE: git= is the precedent for marking how a value was
derived without building a provenance system.

**4. What happens when the labelled slice closes and archives?**
Intervals attached to a slice travel with it into DONE.org, which may
be right — the record of a day's attention is history — or may put the
agenda's data somewhere the agenda never looks.

*Related and deliberately not merged into this.*
[[id:01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0][01849bef]] gives the review buffer a
key for flipping a span's timestamps to active, which is the *manual* path to
the same artifact and belongs to
[[id:6521dd56-8772-416b-9435-6da78bb86b06][6521dd56]]. If both are built, they
should write the same shape to the same place, and whichever lands first sets it
— worth saying now, because the second one landing will otherwise discover the
constraint as a conflict.
:END:

Follow-up to 4f8500e6, which shipped the derivation and deliberately stopped
short of writing anything. M-x claude-code-ide-org-attention-report fills a *org
attention* buffer and that is the whole of its output, so an attested reading
survives only as long as the buffer does and the next run starts from nothing.
Since the intervals are proposals a human corrects against a calendar,
discarding the corrections is the one outcome that makes the whole exercise
pointless.

*No =:BLOCKER:= between this and
[[id:01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0][01849bef]], deliberately, and the
reason is worth stating so nobody adds one later.* The relation is a
**coordination constraint, not an ordering**: both write an active interval, so
they must agree on shape and destination, but neither needs the other finished
first and either may land first. A =:BLOCKER:= would assert an order that does
not exist — and pointing them at each other would assert a cycle. Whichever
lands first sets the shape; the second reads what the first wrote.

:PLAN:
Designed 2026-09-22 through the brainstorming skill, on the =arch= path the
heading already carried.

*The change is to what a lost checklist costs, not to how many writers know
about it* (the user, 2026-09-22, choosing this over closing the two known
holes). A slice declares one thing — the work it undertakes, in order — and
declares it in body prose, where it is indistinguishable from prose and
unbounded at its end. That declaration moves to a *=:MEMBERS:=* property and
the body checklist becomes a rendering the refresh rebuilds. A writer that
destroys the checklist then costs a refresh, not the membership, and the
=[0/0]= failure the conventions call the worst direction becomes unreachable:
emptiness is a property that is missing, not a list that is absent.

*The property.* Full ids, space-separated, in render order, exactly as
=:BLOCKER:= does and for the same parsing reason. Measured: 32 members on the
largest live slice is about 1184 characters, against a 1011-character
=:BLOCKER:= already in these files — the same order, not a new problem.
=:DROPPED:= is unchanged.

*The rendering*, rewritten wholesale from =:MEMBERS:= between the =Planned:=
lead and the =Incidental:= lead or body end. A member whose parent is also
named by the membership renders indented beneath a *parent row* the renderer
inserts for provenance — a member is shown under its parent because that is
where it came from, not because the slice undertook the parent (the user,
2026-09-22). A parent row *never* carries a checkbox and is never counted;
the leaves carry the boxes and the headline cookie counts exactly those.
That is the only self-consistent form, since
=--update-slice-cookie-at-point= counts checkbox-bearing lines, so a box on
an uncounted row would put the rendering and the cookie in disagreement by
construction. A slice undertaking a *whole* story names the story alone,
lists no children, and the story is then a counted leaf like any other.
Parent rows being derived, =bin/lint-org= flags a =:MEMBERS:= entry that is
an ancestor of another entry as redundant, so there is one way to say each
thing.

*What the renderer may ask, and nothing more.* One new query: the parent of a
member id. =--slice-referent-index= gains it — the scan already visits every
headline, so it records level and the enclosing id as it goes; today it is a
flat id-to-(KEYWORD . TITLE) hash that carries no parentage at all, which is
why nothing can compute this now. The renderer does *not* ask whether a
member is a story (=--container-heading-p= answers that and stays out of this
path), does not compute whether the slice covers a story, and has nothing to
do with whether closing the slice closes the story — that is slice-close
semantics, living in the conventions and on the apply path in
=--review-settle-slices=, and if it is wrong it is a different heading.

*=:BLOCKER:= becomes a property-to-property derivation*: =:MEMBERS:= minus
=:DROPPED:= minus referents whose keyword maps to no box, with no prose parse
at all, where =--slice-blocker-ids= reads the body today. It stays
materialised on disk because =org-depend-block-todo= reads the property and
there is no hook where a function could answer instead. This also retires the
grouping-label exclusion the blocker inherits from rendering: parent rows are
not in =:MEMBERS:=, so they can neither reach the blocker by accident nor be
excluded from it by one — which is what made the original catch luck, since
the stale =:BLOCKER:= only disagreed because the prose it came from had been
destroyed.

*The writers.* =--replace-body= on a =:KIND: slice= splices the planned
region out before replacing and re-inserts it after — local, needing no
referent index. Every member scan gains a drawer boundary, so a =:PLAN:=
drawer's id bullets can never be read as members again. =--generated-line-p=
keeps protecting the lines from the fill sweep. None of these is load-bearing
for the declaration any more; they keep the file tidy between refreshes.

*Migration: all sixteen slices* (the user, 2026-09-22, choosing one shape
everywhere over live-only). Write =:MEMBERS:= from each current checklist —
4 in TODO.org, 12 in DONE.org — with parent rows excluded, being derived. The
proof is byte-identity: the regenerated rendering must equal the current text
for all sixteen. When it holds, =--slice-planned-member-ids='s
start-at-the-top fallback is *deleted* rather than kept, which is what "all
sixteen" buys.

*The guard that stops being luck.* =bin/lint-org= gains a rule firing for
every slice whether or not it has a =:BLOCKER:=: =:KIND: slice= implies
=:MEMBERS:=; every id resolves; no entry is an ancestor of another; and the
rendered checklist agrees with the property as a set and in order. That check
would have caught both of this week's defects when they happened.

*Verification.* ERT against scratch org files in the shape of the existing
slice tests: property round-trip; a parent row derived and rendered boxless;
a whole-story member rendered as a counted leaf; =replace=true= on a slice
leaving the checklist intact; a scan refusing bullets inside a =:PLAN:=
drawer; a lead-less closed slice after the fallback is gone. The migration
runs against real copies of both files under the byte-identity check. Prove
the =replace= test discriminates by copying the fix aside and restoring
HEAD's =--replace-body=.

*Ruled out.* A =:MEMBERS:= *drawer* placing the checklist above the body
prose, where =--replace-body= structurally cannot reach it: unnecessary once
the checklist is derived, and it folds away from a human reading the file.
Prose delimiters plus one shared bounds predicate: cheapest and most visible,
but ownership stays invisible to a writer that does not ask, which is the
outcome rejected at the outset. Storing the indent depth in the property
instead of deriving it: a hand-maintained structural fact beside a derived
one is what the =:DROPPED:= history says goes stale and sticky. And a
checkbox on a parent row when the slice closes that story, which was proposed
and walked back the same day (the user, 2026-09-22) once the cookie
arithmetic showed an uncounted box contradicting the count.

*Kept as one heading* rather than divided (the user, 2026-09-22): a
half-applied cutover leaves the property and the checklist disagreeing, so
the property, the renderer, the blocker and the migration are reviewed and
abandoned as a unit. The two cheap guards — the drawer-bounded scan and the
=replace= splice — are separable and could have landed first; that option was
considered and declined here.

Open for the implementing session: whether the DONE.org migration is one
commit or one per slice, and whether =org_slice_add_member='s =after= stays
line-relative or becomes an index into =:MEMBERS:=.

*Correction to "The rendering", from the plan self-review (2026-09-22).* The
trigger stated above — a member whose parent is "also named by the
membership" — is wrong twice over: parent rows are derived, so the parent is
normally *not* in =:MEMBERS:=, and the test has to work when it is absent.
The correct rule is that *a parent row is inserted when the member's parent
heading carries a TODO keyword*. A keyword-less parent yields no row and the
member renders flush left.

Checked against all 32 members of the live slice rather than reasoned about:
six are children of a =DOING= story and take a parent row, while three
(=542924c1=, =72463b68=, =d2a0f54c=) are level-4 headings under the
meta-work datetree's day node =2026-09-02 Wednesday= and render flush left
today. "Any member that is a child" would give those three a day-node parent
row and break the byte-identity proof; "two or more members share a parent"
fails the same case, since all three share that node. The keyword test
reproduces the current file exactly.

This narrows the index change further: =--slice-referent-index= needs each
heading's *parent id*, and the parent's keyword is already what it stores for
every heading. It also qualifies the boundary stated above — the renderer
still never asks whether a member is a story, but it does read one fact about
a member's parent, and reading the parent's keyword is not the same as the
descendant scan =--container-heading-p= performs.

*The titled defect retires with =org_amend=, and this plan does not*
(2026-09-23). =dd4b9a9f='s =b2d4012c= removes =replace=true=, and its
replacement, =org_edit=, refuses any edit that touches a slice's
=Planned:= checklist or =Incidental:= section. So the writer named in
this heading's title will be gone. The plan's argument never depended on
that one writer, though. It moves membership into a property so that
whatever writer destroys the checklist costs only a refresh, and hand
edits and the refresh itself can still do that. It stands on its own.
The title should be restated once =b2d4012c= lands, since by then it
names a tool that no longer exists.
:END:

Found 2026-09-15 composing 35582d95. replace=true rewrites the body's prose
wholesale, and a slice's Planned: lead and member lines ARE body prose, so
composing a slice in the documented order — add members, then write the body —
silently deletes the membership. The docstring's promise is about drawers
("rewrites only the prose below every drawer, so :PROPERTIES:, :LOGBOOK: and a
:PLAN: drawer cannot be destroyed"), which is true and beside the point: the
checklist is the one thing a slice actually declares, since every other field is
derived. Caught by bin/lint-org, which noticed the :BLOCKER: naming ids that
were no longer members — so the guard worked, but only after the loss. Same
shape as the fill sweep wrapping member lines the same week: a tool rewriting a
region another tool owns, where ownership is invisible in the text. Proposed:
refuse replace=true on a :KIND: slice heading, or preserve the member region
across the rewrite the way the drawers are preserved.

*Not a regression from [[id:afea7e4f-41b3-4eda-83f1-39e1b9e7a61a][afea7e4f]],
checked rather than assumed (the user's question, 2026-09-15).* =replace==
arrived in =28d68db= on 2026-08-26, two weeks before that slice existed; both
=claude-code-ide-org-amend= and =--replace-body= are **byte-identical between
=origin/main= and the slice's HEAD**; and the one branch commit naming
=--slice-member-regexp= (=e8f18e0=) only *reads* it, in the new
=--generated-line-p= predicate. The defect is latent since the revision mode
shipped.

*What changed was practice, not code.* =replace== exists for a body that
has become a transcript, which is a *task* heading accumulating
corrections; a slice's body is written once at composition, so the
combination had never arisen. On 2026-09-14 the user asked for
=295cde3a='s forty paragraphs to be compressed, =replace== was the right
tool for that, and hours later the same move was reached for while
composing a slice. A habit formed on tasks migrated to slices inside one
session — which is how a latent defect gets found, and worth recording
because no code change would have predicted it.

*Two defects this week share a shape, and the pair is the argument for how
to fix this one.* The fill sweep wrapped member lines (=496b665e=) and
=replace== erased them (here). **Neither was a code regression.** Both
were existing writers meeting slice member lines for the first time,
because a member line *looks like ordinary body prose* and nothing in the
text says it is owned. =bin/lint-org= caught both — after the loss.

*So the durable fix is not a fourth special case in a fourth writer.* It
is that the member region should be identifiable to **any** writer, which
is what =--generated-line-p= started doing for exactly one of them.
Teaching =org_amend= to refuse =replace== on a =:KIND: slice= heading
closes today's hole and leaves the next writer to rediscover it.

*And the guard that caught this was luck rather than design.* The error
came from the =:BLOCKER:= naming ids that were no longer members — so it
fired only because the slice *had* a blocker. A slice whose members are
all finished carries no =:BLOCKER:= at all (=0086614a= shrinks it to
nothing), so destroying its checklist would leave cookie and blocker
agreeing at empty and nothing to object. The conventions already record
that exact shape as the worst direction to fail in: a slice whose members
cannot be found "reports =[0/0]= as though it were empty rather than
broken".

*Until this lands, the mitigation is a practice and should be stated as
one:* do not call =org_amend= with =replace== on a slice. Compose the body
first and add members after, or amend rather than replace.

*The inverse, observed 2026-09-21: the member refresh destroyed a region it does
not own.* 8a2eb687's new =:PLAN:= drawer listed its members as =- [[id:…][…]]=
lines. The apply pass's slice refresh took all twenty for members, because
=--slice-member-regexp= makes the checkbox optional and nothing excludes
drawers. It rewrote each line to a member line, put a =Planned:= lead inside the
drawer, and took the cookie from [3/23] to [3/43]. The plan prose survived only
in git ([[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::259e07b][259e07b]] records the damage, and the following commit restores it with
plain ids). So ownership is invisible in both directions: other writers cannot
see the member region, and the member scan cannot see where that region ends.
Whatever marks the region fixes both, and the scan should at least skip drawers.

*The design is settled and lives in =:PLAN:= (2026-09-22).* It takes neither
option the "Proposed:" sentence above offers: membership moves to a
=:MEMBERS:= property and the body checklist becomes a rendering, so a writer
that destroys the checklist costs a refresh rather than the membership. That
sentence is superseded and is left standing only as the record of what was
believed at discovery.


* TODO A bookkeeping burst reads as many headings worked at once, and inflates every window it lands in :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       369ad62a-e2ce-4cab-b2ee-0e83ad24f249
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 17:16]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Residue from 295cde3a. The window allocator counts a heading as worked when a
write tool named it, and org_capture had to leave that list because a filing
burst creates a dozen headings inside one window and read as a dozen headings
being worked — 15 in the worst window on 2026-09-10. The same shape survives in
org_set_property and org_slice_add_member, which touch many headings during
slice composition. Proposed: decide whether a bookkeeping write on a heading is
work *on* that heading, and if not, split --worked-tool-names into work and
bookkeeping so a composition pass lands on review and planning whole rather than
a minute each across the headings it filed.


* TODO Make =d= mark for dismissal rather than dismiss, so a shared reason can be given once :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       ee3c22c9-f99c-4d41-9c3d-8f39af916fff
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 12:15]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Asked by the user 2026-09-14, immediately after dismissing seven items that
shared one reason: =d= should leave a mark the way =m= does — a *different*
mark, =D= suggested — so dismissal becomes a bulk operation instead of seven
separate acts. The motivating case is concrete: all seven were state transitions
that had already landed, dismissed for the identical reason, and the pass had no
way to say that once. Dired is the precedent the review buffer already follows
for =m=/=u=/=t=/=U=, and dired marks for deletion with =d= and executes with =x=
— so this would bring =d= into the same idiom rather than inventing one, and =x=
already means "act on what is marked" here. Worth deciding together: whether =x=
then executes both marks (apply the =m=s, dismiss the =D=s) or whether dismissal
needs its own execute key; whether a =D= mark can carry a typed reason once for
the whole set, which is the part that would actually have saved work; and how
=D= interacts with the auto-marking =b6e229c7= does, since an item arriving
pre-marked =m= and then marked =D= must not be both. Supersedes the narrower
cursor question in [[id:e95cc5d1-f8ae-4aad-b9cd-5bd0277e1d4b][e95cc5d1]] if it
ships — a mark does not remove its line, so the advance becomes correct by
construction — but that heading stands on its own until then, since it is a live
defect and this is a design.


* TODO =d= advances point in the review buffer, onto whatever moved up to replace the dismissed item :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e95cc5d1-f8ae-4aad-b9cd-5bd0277e1d4b
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 12:15]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:

Reported by the user 2026-09-14 after dismissing seven stale state items in one
pass. =claude-code-ide-org-review-dismiss= advances point the way =m= does, but
the two are not analogous: =m= leaves its item in place, so advancing lands on
the *next* item, whereas =d= removes its item, so the next one moves *up into
the vacated line* — and advancing then skips it. The user must move back by hand
after every dismissal, which is exactly the friction =m='s advance exists to
remove, inverted. Proposed: =d= leaves point where it is, since the line now
holds the item that would otherwise have been skipped. Note this is a different
question from where point lands when the *list* is rebuilt (=9e80a32d=): nothing
is rebuilt here, one line is removed from a rendered buffer.


* TODO Nothing fills prose on write, so body text arrives as one long line and the human reflows it by hand :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b52df20b-45c7-41aa-9d77-44ccf06c647f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-14 Mon 10:30]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:PLAN:

Reported by the user 2026-09-14: new headings arrive with single-line bodies and
are being reflowed with =M-q= by hand. Measured rather than assumed — **nothing
in =config.el= fills anything**: no =fill-region=, =fill-paragraph=, =auto-fill=
or =fill-column= anywhere, so =org_capture='s note and =org_amend='s text are
written exactly as sent. The wrapping in older bodies was therefore never a
feature; it came from sessions happening to embed newlines in the text they
sent, and it has decayed as that habit did. Across TODO.org by heading creation
month, the median *longest* prose line runs 70 (July), 186 (August), 257
(September), with the share of headings carrying a line over 300 characters
going 20% → 34% → 46% — a steady drift, not a break, and understated at the
recent end because the user has already reflowed the newest bodies. Note the
tempting diagnosis is wrong: =204d1b0a= (2026-09-11) made =org_capture= stop
discarding its note, which looks like the genesis and is not — =org_amend= wrote
verbatim throughout and the trend predates it by two months. Proposed: fill in
the *write* path, so the property holds regardless of who or what is sending the
text — the project's own precedent is that a rule depending on being remembered
is the wrong rule, and "the agent should wrap its prose" is exactly that.
=org-fill-paragraph= already knows not to touch tables, blocks and links, so the
mechanism is org's rather than ours; what needs deciding is the column and
whether a body that arrives *already* wrapped is left alone.

*What the fill must not touch, learned 2026-09-21.* A slice's member
lines are list items whose titles run past any column, and filling them
breaks the checklist: the refresh then finds continuation fragments it
does not own. That is 496b665e's defect, and it recurred in 8a2eb687's
list the same week. So the write-path fill covers paragraphs only.
List items, tables, blocks, drawers' property lines and any =- [[id:=
line are left exactly as sent. The regression test asserts both
directions: a long paragraph arrives filled at =fill-column=, and a long
member line arrives untouched.

*Where it lands:* in =8a2eb687=, beside =7ee3b71a=, since both are about
a writer meeting a region it does not own.

*Both write paths, not one* (learned from 00aa6a85, 2026-09-21). The
queue holds a tool's raw input, written by =queue-append= from the hook
payload before any elisp runs, so a fill applied only where
=org_amend= and =org_capture= write directly leaves every deferred write
unfilled. Fill in =--review-apply-amend= and =--review-apply-capture= as
well, as the block-headline escaping now does, and test both paths.
:END:

=org_capture= and =org_amend= write text exactly as sent, so paragraphs
arrive as one long line and the human reflows them with =M-q=; the
share of headings with a line over 300 characters rose from 20% to 46%
between July and September. Fill paragraphs in the write path, and
leave list items, tables and blocks untouched, so the property holds
whoever sends the text.


* TODO Unplanned work in flight belongs to no slice: planned is writable, incidental is closed-only :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9bab114d-44fa-488e-92bd-ff7c3fd8065d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 19:27]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-14 Mon 12:44]--[2026-09-14 Mon 12:46] =>  0:02
- [2026-09-14 Mon 12:44]--[2026-09-14 Mon 12:46] assigned at review
:END:

Work has three states relative to an open slice and only two can be represented.
Planned-and-open is a member line; unplanned-and-*closed* is picked up
automatically, since =--slice-incidental-ids= defines an incidental as "a
heading whose close this slice owns" and is derived from closes, never curated
(=58e6c6a0=, deliberately — a hand-kept list goes stale by the second day). But
unplanned-and-*still-open* appears nowhere: not planned, because it wasn't; not
incidental, because it hasn't closed. The consequence is the sharp part — such
work cannot enter the derived =:BLOCKER:=, so a slice can reach =DONE= while
work it caused is still open, which is =e80f6ccf='s class arriving from a second
direction. Note what is *not* wrong: nothing forbids adding a member to an open
slice — =org_slice_add_member= refuses only a CLOSED one — so the sanctioned
move already exists, and the conventions already say review residue and
incidental work "both become member lines, so the distinction lives in the
debrief rather than in the list". What misleads is the vocabulary: =Planned:= is
a structural anchor for the member parser (=a43cfaa0=), not a claim of
foresight, and reading it as foresight makes the one writable list look
unavailable for the very work that most needs it. Proposed: say so explicitly in
the conventions — a mid-flight addition is a *declaration of membership*, not a
retroactive claim to have foreseen it — and decide whether in-flight unplanned
work should be marked as such at all, or left to the debrief as the
review-residue rule already does. Raised by the user 2026-09-11 as "a stark
inconsistency we should not abide".

*The user's ruling, 2026-09-11, which settles the open question above and
inverts the current default:* "issues that are **discovered** during the course
of a slice are already by nature incidental. unless there is a reason to defer
them, they **should** become a member, albeit unplanned, of the slice, because
they have some bearing on achieving the desired end state of the slice."

*What that changes.* An incidental is currently defined by its **close** —
=--slice-incidental-ids= lists "a heading whose close this slice owns" — so
unplanned work is adopted retroactively, only if it finishes inside the window,
and never blocks anything. Under this ruling adoption happens at **discovery**,
which is the only version that puts the issue into the derived =:BLOCKER:= and
therefore stops the slice closing over it. Default becomes adopt; deferral
becomes the exception that needs a stated reason. Mechanically it needs no new
tool: =org_slice_add_member= already accepts an open slice, and
=--slice-incidental-ids= excludes ids the slice already names, so an adopted
member is not double-listed when it later closes.

*The test that bounds it, and it is in the ruling's own "because" clause.* Read
as a justification, "they have some bearing on achieving the desired end state"
explains why adopt is the right default; read as a **test**, it is what keeps a
slice from sprawling. The session that raised this is the counterexample:
working =afea7e4f= produced five discoveries (=c9940558=, =2deb090f=,
=b57c7515=, =3f4fd744=, =f1e36a65=), every one of them incidental by discovery
and *none* bearing on the transcript join, which shipped complete without them.
Adopting all five would have left that slice at =[0/9]=, unable to close, held
open by three unrelated subsystems. So "no bearing on this slice's end state" is
a legitimate reason to defer — and on this evidence it is the common case, not
the rare one.

*Which leaves one genuinely open sub-question:* whether an adopted-unplanned
member is *visibly* distinguished from a planned one. The conventions' existing
answer for review residue is that both become member lines and the distinction
lives in the debrief; the ruling's phrase "a member, albeit unplanned" may want
more than that. A third parsed lead (=Adopted:=, beside =Planned:= and
=Incidental:=) is the obvious shape but is a real parser change — the member
scan bounds itself on those leads — so it should be decided rather than assumed.

*Sharpened by the user, same day, and it corrects the paragraph above.*
"Relationship" is the right word, not "bearing" — the sentiment to make
matter-of-course is: **we understand it now, it is in context, the costs and
risks are low, so we might as well take it on and be done with it.** Expected to
be the rule rather than the exception: if feature A is landing shortly, the
feature H that surfaced while working A — or while looking ahead to B through G
— is very likely wanted too.

*My counterexample was overstated and is withdrawn in part.* It tested the
narrow end state ("does the transcript join need this to ship?") rather than
relationship to the work in hand. Re-read under the looser test, three or four
of the session's five discoveries qualify: =c9940558= was discovered *by* the
tracker annotation and lives in the same render path, and =b57c7515= bears on
the join directly — the prompt lookup keys on =session_id=, and a re-key leaves
two transcripts for one conversation. Only =3f4fd744= is genuinely unrelated. So
"no bearing" is not the common case; the marginal cost of adjacent work while
the context is already loaded really is the dominant term.

*What survives is not a filter on adoption but the absence of a stopping point.*
If adoption is the rule, membership grows monotonically while the slice runs,
and every adopted member can itself produce discoveries. The termination
condition is then not "the work ran out" but "we decided to stop adopting" — and
nothing in the conventions says when that is. That, rather than over-adoption,
is what turns the sentiment into scope creep: the two are distinguished by the
*existence of a closing gate*, not by the decision to take something on.

*Proposed gate, because it costs nothing and reuses a line that already exists:*
**adoption is open while the slice is =DOING=; entering =REVIEW= closes it.**
=REVIEW= on a grouping already means every member terminal and the work not yet
integrated (=e80f6ccf=), so it is exactly the moment membership should stop
moving. A discovery after that goes to the next slice or stands alone — which is
also what gives the *next* slice its natural seed.

*One legibility consequence to expect rather than be surprised by.* Adopting at
discovery inflates the cookie's denominator before its numerator, so a slice's
progress visibly regresses — =[3/6]= becomes =[3/9]=. Incidentals never did
this, being done on arrival. It is honest and it will read as a defect to anyone
who has not been told, so it belongs in the convention text.

*Approved as a trial, 2026-09-11:* run the depth-one rule on the slice in hand
"and one or two more to see how it affects us". So it stays **provisional** in
the conventions rather than hardening — the exit condition is two or three
slices' experience, and what to look for is whether adoption actually stops at
depth one or whether the distinction between a planned member's discovery and an
adopted member's discovery turns out to be unreadable in the moment.

*The user's own risk analysis, which is the part that makes the trial cheap:*
"the worst that can happen is that future open slices (not =DOING=) get
=CANCELLED= or entirely reformulated — same effect once a new slice with any
members that were in a =CANCELLED= slice is created." That is right, and it
names a release valve the conventions had not: **cancelling and reformulating an
open slice is a normal move, not a failure.** A =CANCELLED= slice keeps its
body, which the composition section already says is usually the part worth
keeping, and its members are free to be composed into a successor. The cost of
an over-adopted slice is therefore bounded by one reformulation.

*And that valve has a named trigger:* "that should always be an option when
feature Q is suddenly needed so that work on another project can proceed". Not
hypothetical — see [[id:6a207b00-8fa1-441e-8ee5-354702bc979b][6a207b00]]:
adopting the plugin in agentic-euchre is blocked by unresolved work here, and a
slice standing between that and the user is a slice worth reformulating rather
than finishing out of order.

*The user, 2026-09-14, correcting this heading's own framing and mine:* "I
thought *incidental* means *not planned*, supported by absence from the
operative version(s) of =next-session.md=. Otherwise, what's the meaning of the
=Planned:= marker?"

*That supplies an objective test the conventions did not have, and it is
checkable in one command.* Measured against =afea7e4f= the same day: =325679af=,
=96ddf1ef=, =295cde3a= and =fbaf8009= each appear two or three times in the
operative =next-session.md=; =f1e36a65=, =f3a53bd4=, =40159d43=, =b23e5bcc= and
=169df26b= appear **zero** times. So five of nine members sat under =Planned:=
having never been planned — including =f1e36a65=, adopted hours before the other
four and mislabelled the same way.

*Which retires the claim recorded above that =Planned:= is "a structural anchor
for the member parser, not an assertion of foresight".* That was read off
=a43cfaa0='s title — "member anchor, insertion point, self-heal" — three
*structural* jobs, from which semantic emptiness does not follow. A lead that
were only an anchor would read =Members:=. It reads =Planned:=, and the plan
file is what makes the word verifiable rather than decorative.

*So adoption must not write into the planned list*, and the asymmetry this
heading names is sharper than first stated: the writable list is the one whose
label adoption falsifies, and the correctly-labelled list is derived-only. Using
the first is not a workaround, it is a false statement about what the slice
foresaw.

*Proposed mechanism, mirroring =:DROPPED:= exactly rather than inventing a
shape.* A =:ADOPTED:= property on the slice naming ids declared incidental at
discovery, with the refresh rendering the =Incidental:= section as the **union**
of those declared ids and the ones derived from closes. That keeps both section
names meaning what they say, makes "incidental" writable at the moment of
discovery — which is what "see them accumulate along the way" needs — and
follows the project's standing pattern of declaring the exception and deriving
the rendering. It also subsumes the third-lead idea floated earlier: no new
section is needed, because =Incidental:= already means the right thing and only
lacked a way to be written.

*Until it exists, the five sit under the wrong lead and the slice's body says
so*, which is the honest interim: visible and wrong beats invisible.

*Declined by the user, 2026-09-14, and the reasoning retires the proposal above
rather than deferring it:* "I'm inclined to move the other way and just allow
the slice to grow according to convention without regard for membership. If
anyone ever cares to discriminate then the grep can happen on demand. Another
case of over-regulation on my part without a clear necessity."

*That is the better call, and it follows this project's own precedent more
closely than the =:ADOPTED:= proposal did.* The planned/adopted distinction is
**derivable** — the plan file says who was planned — so storing it would be a
second copy of a fact that already exists, and the project's standing objection
to derived-facts-stored (=a7f4b40c=) applies exactly. =:DROPPED:= earned its
property because a bare cookie deletion was genuinely *ambiguous* between three
readings (=1b727475=); nothing is ambiguous here. And thirteen slices have run
without anyone once needing the distinction at a glance, which is the
demonstrated-premise test =7771fc63= set.

*So the =Planned:= lead keeps its name and a slice simply grows.* Its literal
accuracy for adopted members is the price, and it is a small one against a
property, a refresh branch, a lint rule and a convention section — none of which
anything has asked for.

*One prerequisite had to be met for this to be safe, and it was not.* The grep
is only durable while the operative plan version stays pinned, and
=next-session.md= is rewritten in place. =afea7e4f= carried **no** =orgit-rev=
revision link at all — three days into the slice — so the discrimination was
derivable from the *current* file and would have become guesswork the moment the
next slice rewrote it. The link is now on the slice. **The convention that
matters here is therefore not a new property but the existing one: link the plan
revision when the slice is picked up**, and this is its first recorded failure.


* TODO Nothing can correct a closed slice's record, even when the correction is close-time truth :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a508c59e-475e-4d27-84c9-6c3ded98e15c
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 17:17]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:END:

Second time the closed-slice guard has blocked a legitimate act.
=org_slice_add_member= refuses a closed slice and =refresh-slice= skips one,
both correctly — but on 2026-09-11 =c19fbbf5= needed two checkboxes ticked, a
keyword text corrected and a stale =:BLOCKER:= removed, all of which were *true
at its close* rather than later change. With no sanctioned mechanism the fix
went through the Edit tool, straight to disk behind Emacs's back, which is
exactly the buffer/disk divergence the queue architecture exists to prevent and
required a manual revert. Proposed: a narrow repair path — a refresh variant
that recomputes a closed slice against its referents' keywords *as of the
slice's CLOSED stamp*, or an explicit correct-closed-slice tool that writes
through Emacs. The distinction to preserve is the one the guard exists for:
re-deriving a closed slice against referents' keywords *today* is the
falsification =30a340fd= refused; deriving against their state at close is not.
Complements =a00ace04=, which is the detection half.


* TODO lint-org cannot see a slice checkbox that disagrees with its referent :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a00ace04-99bd-414a-8dc7-df8305126589
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 17:17]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:

Found 2026-09-11 when the user spotted =c19fbbf5= sitting at =[17/19]=
with two members that had been =DONE= since before it closed. Nothing
mechanical could have caught it: the existing rule compares the slice's
=:BLOCKER:= against its *checklist*, and =--slice-blocker-ids= derives
that set from the checkbox cookies — so both halves read the same stale
boxes, agreed, and reported zero errors. The lint can detect a blocker
disagreeing with the boxes; it cannot detect boxes disagreeing with their
referents, which is the failure that actually occurs.

Proposed: a rule comparing each member's checkbox against the referent's current
keyword, per the conventions' derivation table. It must scope to *open* slices
only — on a closed one a disagreement is the record correctly refusing to track
later change (=30a340fd=), which is the opposite of a defect. Complements
=a508c59e=, which is the repair half; this one is detection.* TODO org_query
exposes four of org-ql's thirty-five predicates, so structured reads fall back
to text scanning :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       61f05e56-9d05-4f3e-9131-5eb899f95b8f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 16:16]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

The user asked on 2026-09-11 why a session reaches for =sed=/=awk= against
TODO.org when =emacs-tools= exists; the answer is that the two questions at hand
were not expressible. =org_query= advertises =todo:=, =tags:=, =priority:= and
=heading:=, while the loaded org-ql defines 35 predicates (measured, not
recalled: =(length org-ql-predicates)=) including =property=, =closed=, =ts=,
=category=, =level=, =blocked=, =children=, =descendants=, =ancestors= and
=rifle=. So "which headings carry =:KIND: slice=" and "what closed after this
timestamp" both became hand-rolled awk — and the first attempt silently
mis-paired ids with headings, because =CLOSED:= precedes the property drawer and
the script did not model that. The engine already supports what is missing; this
is about the exposed surface. =property=, =closed= and =category= look
highest-value here, =blocked= notable since this project has bespoke code for
it.


* TODO apply-detect reports an apply when the review pass only dismissed events :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       7b4f4f14-6c76-4822-84c1-67589bc74b82
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-24 Thu 07:50]--[2026-09-24 Thu 07:53] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-24 Thu 07:50]--[2026-09-24 Thu 07:53] brainstorm apply-detect's dismissal false positive
:END:
:PLAN:
*The design, approved 2026-09-24 (the user), brainstormed on the bounded path.* The fix is in the hook, not the writer (the user, 2026-09-24). A separate marker file touched only on apply was the alternative. It was declined because it would give =--queue-watermark-write='s one-writer, both-facts design a companion to keep in step.

*The discriminator.* Each =applied= entry in =<session>.applied= maps an event to the time of the pass that applied it, for example ="2026-09-23T15:13:13-0500"=. A dismiss-only pass rewrites the file, which is why the mtime lies, but it leaves those values unchanged. =bin/hooks/apply-detect= keeps the mtime test as a cheap first filter. Then one =jq= call over just the watermarks that moved prints each file's newest =applied= value, and a watermark counts as applied only when that value *differs* from the one recorded for it. Inequality rather than order, so the local offsets (=-0500=, =-0600= across DST) never need parsing.

*The stamp becomes a record.* =<session>.apply-seen= holds one line per watermark, its name and last-seen value, written by temp file and rename, and only after the hook's output succeeds, as the =touch= is today. The first prompt of a session, and a legacy empty stamp (all 44 on disk on 2026-09-24), record every watermark's current value silently: pre-session history is SessionStart's to report. A full scan of the 71 watermarks in one =jq= process takes well under a second, so that initial case is cheap too.

*Edge cases.* A watermark missing from the record counts as applied, so a session's first apply is still reported. An empty =applied= set has the value =""=. A file =jq= cannot parse falls back to today's mtime judgement, so an unreadable file never silences a real apply. The message counts watermarks whose value changed.

*Testing*, in =bin/apply-detect-test=: a dismiss-only rewrite reports nothing; an apply reports; a legacy empty stamp initialises silently; a new watermark reports; an unparseable file falls back to mtime; a record written before a DST change still matches. The suite ends with =94e6b615='s standard final line. Each test is shown to fail against HEAD's hook.

Files: =bin/hooks/apply-detect=, =bin/apply-detect-test=, and the sentence in =skills/org/references/org-session-tracking.md= that says the hook "compares their mtimes", re-promoted with =bin/claude-org-setup=.
:END:

=bin/hooks/apply-detect= reports "the review queue was applied" whenever a watermark file's mtime moves. But =--queue-watermark-write= rewrites the file on every pass, including one that only dismissed events, so the hook tells a session the record on disk is fresh when nothing on disk changed. Designed 2026-09-24 on the bounded path: the hook compares each moved watermark's newest =applied= pass time with the value it last recorded, and reports only a change.

*Established:* found by PR22's review, and introduced with [[id:165ce65a-f87e-431c-8ee4-f0fbfffe14da][165ce65a]]. Each =applied= entry carries the time of the pass that applied it, and a dismiss-only pass leaves those values unchanged (read 2026-09-24).


* TODO Two tracked repos sharing a directory basename collide on one MCP session id :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       965f94eb-1558-4d00-9857-5727eb86fe6f
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-21 Mon 15:28]--[2026-09-21 Mon 15:29] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-21 Mon 15:28]--[2026-09-21 Mon 15:29] re-scope to the basename collision
:END:
:PLAN:
The fix still needs choosing: a session id that stays unique when two repos
share a basename, and that a consumer's generated =.mcp.json= can name
statically. Measure first the basename plus a short hash of the absolute path.
(Re-scoped to the collision by the user, 2026-09-21; the ordering half closed
in =8bbae3aa=.)
:END:

The standalone wiring registers each tracked repo under its directory basename
as the MCP session id, and =claude-org-setup --org= writes that same basename
into the repo's generated =.mcp.json=. Two tracked repos that share a basename
therefore collide: the later registration overwrites the earlier, and both
static configs name the one surviving id, with nothing to tell them apart.
Nothing reports the collision.

Found by PR #22's review. The warp alias and ordering half of the original
filing closed in 8bbae3aa ([[orgit-rev:claude-code-ide-org::d5c9398][d5c9398]]); this is what remains.


* TODO A deferred targetless capture loses its project routing when the queue is applied :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5e731a23-7708-4248-b055-9b4a531f147d
:CREATED:  [2026-09-11 Fri 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:

Found by PR22's review pass. =--review-apply-capture= resolves a nil target
through =--capture-target-file=, which consults the MCP session context — but
upstream =let=-binds =--current-session-id= around the HTTP request only, so
during =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= it is nil and the heading falls back to
the global capture file. So: capture with no target from project B while B's
TODO.org has unsaved edits, the call defers and answers "top of TODO.org", a
human applies, and the heading lands in project A's tracker. This is
[[id:d718f6b6-5a33-4889-8b5a-8ea82553ccea][d718f6b6]] surviving on its queued
half. The queue event already carries =cwd= (=bin/hooks/queue-append:229=) and
nothing on the apply path reads it, which is the likely fix.
