*Two constraints it inherits, and the first is sharper here than anywhere else
in the story.*

- *Never label it in a way that invites a productivity reading*, because a
  column heading is exactly where such a reading attaches, and the number is the
  *inverse* of productivity under autonomy. See
  [[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]], which owns that
  question.
- *Never present a total that looks complete.* The tool sees Claude sessions and
  nothing else, and an aggregate table is the most convincing possible way to
  imply a full accounting of a day. The gaps are precisely where meatspace went.


** TODO Allocate attention across concurrent sessions, which is zero-sum :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:44]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:BLOCKER:  b0e478f7-e894-4ac7-b0b6-b3c890b5b32a
:END:

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31.
*The substantial half, and the one that actually answers the question the parent
was asked.*

Attention is zero-sum across concurrent sessions. If the human was driving
session B while session A's agent ground away, A's forty minutes should not be
credited to A's heading at all — the attention belongs to B. Annotating each
span with its own turn count is the easy half; apportioning an hour among the
sessions live in it is the half that answers "what did I attend to".

*Two preconditions, both recorded in the parent and neither met:*

- *Hooks are project-scoped*, so only sessions in this repo write queue files
  and the directory sees one project.
  [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] is therefore a
  *precondition* rather than a neighbour — portability work motivated for an
  entirely different reason that turns out to be load-bearing here.
- *Queue events record no =cwd=.* The hook payload carries one (verified
  2026-08-14), so this is one line in the =jq= object rather than new
  instrumentation. Deriving project from
  =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= is a fragile join where a recorded
  field is free.

*The shape of the answer is constrained, and the constraint is the parent's:*
the tool informs a judgement, it does not compute an allocation. Attention
intervals are constructed manually by the human on review; "hourly resolution"
is a rule of thumb for how coarse the answer needs to be, *not* a bucketing
scheme, and an earlier paragraph proposing to bucket resume events by hour was
corrected as describing an automation this project is not trying to build.

*Whatever is built must not present a total that looks complete* — the tool sees
Claude sessions and nothing else, and the gaps are exactly where meatspace went.

*Newly relevant:* [[id:445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb][445ec1f1]], where
the user concluded much of the logged activity is bogus. Cross-session
allocation is one of the few things that could make a span's attribution
*verifiable* rather than merely plausible, so this heading may be more
load-bearing for that A/B than it looked when the parent filed it.

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]
on 2026-08-31, and narrowed: the =cwd= field this heading named as a
precondition is now [[id:5461c349-fd22-4b75-a508-50efbe4b87f8][5461c349]] on its
own, since it is a concrete one-line change that should not wait for a judgement
this size.*

*The reframing that makes this the substantial half.* Attention is the *primary*
measure for assigning attention intervals post hoc, not a supplement to clock
duration. Long agent-work intervals do reflect agent productivity — but they are
precisely the intervals in which the human attends to something else: email,
post hoc review, or another Claude Code session in another project. Intense
back-and-forth means the human was at the keyboard interpreting responses, often
alternating between tasks and meta-tasks inside one session.

*So the metric inverting under autonomy is the signal, not a caveat.* A low-turn
hour is evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being
measured.

*Hourly resolution is a real simplification, and it is not a bucketing scheme.*
An earlier draft proposed bucketing resume events by hour per session and
comparing; the user corrected it. "Hourly" is a rule of thumb for how coarse the
*answer* needs to be — "what did I attend to today, roughly" — and does not mean
splitting anything on the hour for presentation. The attention intervals are
constructed *manually*, by the human, on review of the gathered data.

*Which fixes the shape of whatever gets built:* the tool informs a judgement, it
does not compute an allocation. Constructing intervals depends on reflection
about meatspace events this tool cannot capture and never will. What it *can*
capture should be as informative as possible — *moderated by keeping those
judgement calls a lightweight exercise*. That moderation is a first-class
requirement rather than a nicety: a presentation that is more accurate but
heavier to read is a worse presentation, and the tradeoff resolves in favour of
lightness every time.

*Explicitly out of scope, so it is not mistaken for an oversight:* predicting or
constructing attention intervals automatically, given more history, external
signals and practice. That may become possible; it is not being attempted.

*The remaining precondition is
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]].* Hooks are
project-scoped, so only sessions in this repo write queue files at all and the
directory sees one project. Moving hooks to =~/.claude/settings.json= is what
gives cross-project allocation anything to compare — portability work motivated
for an entirely different reason that turns out to be load-bearing here.

*Deferred to packaging, 2026-09-08 (the user).* The named
preconditions are satisfied in the letter — =9ae4b17e= classified,
=5461c349= shipped — but not in the substance: hooks are still
project-scoped until [[id:b0e478f7-e894-4ac7-b0b6-b3c890b5b32a][b0e478f7]]
ships them user-level, so the queue directory still sees one project
and there is nothing to allocate *between*. =:BLOCKER:= re-pointed
accordingly. Meanwhile the =cwd= field accumulates (recording since
2026-09-04); the judgement design happens when there is real
multi-project data to judge against, not before.


** TODO Decide where the caveat lives that turn count is not productivity :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:44]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31,
where it is a demand the parent makes and nothing yet satisfies.

*The claim:* turn count must never be labelled or read as productivity, because
under autonomy it is the *inverse* of it. A forty-minute agent turn counts 1,
the same as "yes", so turns/hour drops precisely when the agent is working
hardest. The parent settled that this is the metric *working* rather than
failing — a low-turn hour is evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which
is the thing being measured — but that reading is not self-evident from a bare
number.

*Nothing on the review line says any of this.* =0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6
turns= shipped 2026-08-31 with no caveat attached, on the argument that
juxtaposition carries the meaning and that a classifier would need an invented
threshold. That argument covers the *recall-cue* reading; it does not cover a
reader who imports a productivity reading from elsewhere.

*Worth deciding before the count reaches a second surface*, since a =:LOGBOOK:=
annotation ([[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]]) or a
clocktable column ([[id:97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0][97503194]]) is
read further from context than a review line is, and a column heading is exactly
where a productivity reading would attach.

*Candidate answers, none chosen:* the review buffer's key legend; the tool
descriptions; a line in CLAUDE.md's session-tracking section; or nowhere, on the
grounds that the only reader is the person who set the convention. The last is a
real option — this project has one user — and it should be rejected on evidence
rather than reflex.

*Related:* [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the standing
precedent that a plausible-but-wrong number is worse than no number, which is
the same worry one step earlier: here the number is right and the *reading* is
what goes wrong.

*Analysis inherited from [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]]
on 2026-08-31, where it was recorded as a con, then reframed as the signal,
leaving exactly this residue.*

*The full arc, because the conclusion is not obvious from either end.* It was
first written down as a *cost*: a 40-minute agent turn counts 1, the same as
"yes", so turns/hour drops precisely when the agent is working hardest — correct
as a measure of human attention, badly misleading if read as productivity. The
user then reframed it: that is the metric *working*, since a low-turn hour is
evidence the human's attention was elsewhere, which is the thing being measured.

*So the caveat that survives is narrower than the original con and stronger than
it sounds:* the number must never be labelled or read as productivity, because
it is the *inverse* of it. Not "roughly unrelated" — inverted. A reader who
imports a productivity reading gets an answer that is not merely imprecise but
backwards.

*The neighbouring con, which is a different worry and belongs here too.* The
count is *measured*; the inference from it is a guess. A turn may be "continue"
or three paragraphs of design, and the difference is invisible.
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the precedent for
retiring a plausible-but-wrong number on exactly this reasoning — it applies
here with less force, since the count itself is honest, but not with none. It is
also the argument that killed the dense/sparse classifier: a threshold would be
an invented number laid on top of a measured one.

*Explicit non-goal, recorded so nothing is shaped to serve it:* measuring how
productive the human was using Claude versus not using Claude. Nothing in this
story should support that comparison.

*Nothing carries the caveat today.* The review line ships =0:11 span, 0:01 in 1
run, 6 turns= with no annotation, on the argument that juxtaposition carries the
recall-cue meaning. That argument does not cover an imported reading, and it
weakens as the number travels: an annotation
([[id:95755305-0499-4022-bd75-f325fd6828c7][95755305]]) or a column
([[id:97503194-5ef3-4a1e-b25d-676c3cb39eb0][97503194]]) is read further from
context, and a column heading is exactly where a productivity reading attaches.

*Candidates, none chosen:* the review buffer's key legend; the tool
descriptions; a line in CLAUDE.md's session-tracking section; or nowhere, on the
grounds that the only reader is the person who set the convention. The last is a
real option — this project has one user — and should be rejected on evidence
rather than reflex.


** TODO Turn count reads 0 for a background or subagent session, which is not no attention :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       9b5eb830-8825-4d6c-b701-c167c2d71fdf
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 16:39]
:END:

Split out of [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] 2026-08-31,
where it was the third of three recorded cons and the only one never answered.

*The defect.* Turn count is derived from =resume= events, one per
=UserPromptSubmit=. A background session or a subagent has no human submitting
prompts, so it counts *zero* — and zero already means something else on this
readout: a span in which the human was present but said nothing. A bare =0
turns= cannot distinguish "not applicable" from "no attention", and the two want
opposite conclusions when a human is allocating their day.

*Why it matters more since 2026-08-31.* The count is now on every suggested span
line (=0:11 span, 0:01 in 1 run, 6 turns=), so the ambiguity is in front of a
reader at the moment of judgement rather than latent in a field nobody looks at.

*The data to tell them apart is already recorded.* =bin/hooks/queue-append=
writes =agent_id= and =agent_type= on every event, so a span backed by subagent
events is identifiable without new instrumentation — the same "a view of what
already exists" argument that made the turn count itself cheap.

*Open, and the smaller half of the question:* what to render. Candidates, none
chosen — omit the clause entirely for a session that cannot have turns; print
=n/a= rather than =0=; or name the agent type, which says why. The parent's
standing requirement applies: prefer the lighter presentation, and never present
a figure that invites a reading it cannot support.

*A live instance the same day this was filed, and it widens the heading past
subagents.* With the new readout loaded, a real pending item rendered:

: 0:05 span, 0:05 in 1 run, 0 turns

*One run of five minutes, zero turns* — which reads as contradictory and is not.
The run comes from an explicit =clock_in=/=clock_out= pair, while turns count
completed =resume= -> =pause= adjacencies; that stretch was a single long agent
turn with no completed turn inside the window, so zero is literally correct.

*But it is exactly the wrong impression.* Zero turns beside five clocked minutes
reads as "set running and left" when the truth was continuous agent work on one
instruction — which is the metric's advertised inversion under autonomy showing
up as an apparent inconsistency on the line rather than as a caveat somewhere.

*So this heading is not only about background and subagent sessions.* Any span
whose window holds no completed turn reads =0 turns=, and the three causes — no
human present, a subagent, and one long turn still in flight — are
indistinguishable on the line while wanting different conclusions. The rendering
question is therefore broader than "omit the clause for a session that cannot
have turns": it has to separate *cannot have turns* from *had none complete in
this window*.

*Related:* [[id:c4bc5c70-861a-4ce0-80da-c79b6b1600f3][c4bc5c70]] owns the
productivity caveat, and this is where the caveat becomes concrete rather than
abstract — the reader does not need to import a wrong reading, the line offers
one.


** DONE Record cwd on queue events, so a span can be attributed to a project :code:
CLOSED: [2026-09-08 Tue 09:20]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5461c349-fd22-4b75-a508-50efbe4b87f8
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 16:39]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-31 Mon 16:44]
CLOCK: [2026-09-04 Fri 16:12]--[2026-09-04 Fri 16:15] =>  0:03
- State "DOING"      from "NEXT"       [2026-09-04 Fri 16:12] \\
  merging its twin and implementing the cwd field
- [2026-09-04 Fri 16:12]--[2026-09-04 Fri 16:15] record cwd on queue events, twin-merged
- State "REVIEW"     from "DOING"      [2026-09-04 Fri 16:15] \\
  cwd field code-complete and unit-tested; handed back
- State "DONE"       from "REVIEW"     [2026-09-08 Tue 09:20] \\
  review passed; cwd recording since 09-04
:END:
:PLAN:

Split out of [[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]] 2026-08-31,
because it is a concrete one-line change sitting inside a much larger judgement
and does not need to wait for it.

*What is missing.* =bin/hooks/queue-append= writes ts, kind, id, state, from,
note, title, target, tags, text, tool_use_id, session_id, agent_id, agent_type
and source — and no =cwd=. So a queue file says which *session* an event came
from and not which *project*.

*The payload already carries it.* =cwd= was verified present in a captured
=PermissionRequest= payload on 2026-08-14, so this is one more line in the =jq=
object rather than new instrumentation — the same shape as every other field
there.

*Why it is worth recording rather than deriving.* Project is recoverable
indirectly, since =~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= encodes it, but
that is a fragile join against a path convention this project does not own,
where a recorded field is free.

*What it unblocks.* Queue files are per-session and all land in
=~/.claude/org-updates/=, which is *user-global* rather than per-project — so
the directory is already the right shape for cross-session comparison, and this
is the field that makes the comparison meaningful. It is one of the two
preconditions [[id:b862fbf4-53b8-48b5-b773-1c7c0332dfb4][b862fbf4]] names; the
other is [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]], without which
only this repo writes queue files at all and there is nothing to compare across.

*Do it before the allocation work, not during it.* The field is only useful on
events written *after* it ships, so every day it is not recorded is a day the
eventual analysis cannot see. That argues for landing it early even though
nothing consumes it yet — the opposite of the usual rule against building unused
machinery, and the reason is that this datum cannot be backfilled.

Sharpened 2026-09-03 by the first concurrent worktree session: a session in
.claude/worktrees/pr8-review writes queue events whose cwd (once recorded) will
differ from the main checkout's while every id-addressed event still resolves
through Emacs against the one live tracker. So cwd is necessary but not
sufficient as project identity — the consumer must normalise a worktree path to
its repository (git worktree list, or the common .git), or events from a
worktree would read as a different project than the tracker they target. Same
session also demonstrated the fragmentation argument against moving the queue
into the repo: each worktree has its own working copy, so a repo-local queue
would scatter events across directories the review pass never reads, while the
shared user-global dir keeps every worktree's events visible to the one apply
pass — which is correct, since they all target the same tracker.

*Merged in from [[id:83b3cdd6-982a-4ae1-878c-79a8c941f638][83b3cdd6]]
2026-09-04, which was the same task written first* (2026-08-15, as
"Record the originating project in queue events"). The two bodies quote
the identical field list and the identical 2026-08-14 payload capture,
and neither cited the other — a textbook twin, caught at review when
both appeared as adjacent slice members. What it adds beyond this body:
the *motivation* (attention allocation needs turn density per project
per hour — an event can say a session was busy but not whose work it
was), the note that the field is a constant today and stops being one
the moment hooks go user-level, and the raw-cwd-versus-slug question —
which this body's 2026-09-03 note already answers: record raw =cwd=,
normalise at consumption (worktree → repository via =git worktree
list= or the common git dir, the same resolution
[[id:f8c86914-0759-4e05-9292-6f7fa5593701][f8c86914]] chose for the
convention checks).

:END:
*Shipped 2026-09-04* (commit =2d67b7e=, on
=feature/org-discipline-travel=). One field — =cwd: ($payload.cwd //
null)= — added to =queue-append='s jq object, between =session_id= and
=agent_id=. Test-first per discipline: two assertions added to
=bin/queue-append-test= (a fixture carrying =cwd= must record it; an
absent =cwd= records =null=, not ""), run red against the unmodified
script, then the field turned them green. Full suite passes.

Scope held deliberately tight: the field lands on queue events, which
is what cross-project attribution needs. Two things left for the
consumer, both already argued in the plan above rather than reopened
here — worktree-path normalisation (a worktree's =cwd= differs from its
main checkout while every id-addressed event still resolves against the
one tracker, so the consumer normalises via the common git dir, matching
[[id:f8c86914-0759-4e05-9292-6f7fa5593701][f8c86914]]'s choice), and
whether =claude-code-ide-org--log-event='s audit log should carry the
same field for symmetry. Neither blocks recording the datum now, which
is the point — it cannot be backfilled.


* TODO org_capture's target refuses an 8-character :ID: prefix that every other tool accepts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       25e7b083-360b-4282-8fd0-bb768e2a0f13
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:45]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-09-24 Thu 07:41]--[2026-09-24 Thu 07:44] =>  0:03
- [2026-09-24 Thu 07:41]--[2026-09-24 Thu 07:44] brainstorm the id-prefix sweep
:END:
:PLAN:
*The design, approved 2026-09-24 (the user), brainstormed on the bounded path.* Every function taking an =id= or =target= argument resolves it through =claude-code-ide-org--id-find=, and completeness is proved from the tool registry rather than by another search.

*The known sites, all confirmed still broken on 2026-09-24 by reading the code.* The silent ones come first, because an argument compared as a string selects nothing and reports a clean no-op.
- =org_capture='s target: =--capture-target-spec= checks the prefix with =--id-find=, then passes the *unexpanded* prefix into org-capture's =(id …)= spec, which looks it up itself and fails. The fix passes the expanded id and names the full id in the reply.
- =claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice='s own id, and =claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice-blocker='s: compared with =equal= against the heading's full =:ID:=. Both resolve through =--id-find= before comparing.
- =:DROPPED:=: =--slice-dropped-ids= splits and downcases the value but never expands it, so a prefix there drops nothing. The refresh expands prefixes when it reads them, and =org_set_property= expands and validates =:DROPPED:= values the way it does =:BLOCKER:=, refusing an id it cannot resolve.
- =org_refile='s =target_id= and =org_divide= are checked in the same pass, and any gap found there is fixed here.

*Completeness comes from the tool registry, not from a search* (the user, 2026-09-24). The first sweep found fourteen sites by call shape and missed these three, so this one does not rely on a search. One ERT test captures every =org_*= registration in batch, by rebinding =claude-code-ide-make-tool=. For each argument named =id=, =target=, =target_id=, =slice_id=, =member_id=, =after=, =parent= or =scope=, it calls the tool's function with an 8-character prefix against a scratch file, and asserts the reply is neither an =Error:= nor a clean no-op. Other required arguments come from a per-tool table of sample values, and a tool with a required argument missing from that table fails the test, so a tool added later cannot go untested. The test runs with =CLAUDE_ORG_QUEUE_DIR= pointed at a temporary directory and no session id, so the queued tools write nothing real (the 2026-09-21 lesson, and =94e6b615='s runner rule). Ids come from =org-id-locations= built for the scratch file. =--with-known-ids= is the wrong fixture for anything walking =org-id-locations=, because it leaves its ids unknown to org.

*Tests for the two non-tool sites:* the refresh command given a prefix; a prefix in =:DROPPED:= dropping its member; =org_set_property= expanding a =:DROPPED:= prefix and refusing an unresolvable one. Write each failing test before assuming a site works, and show each fails against HEAD's version; for the registry test, that means copying the capture fix aside. Then the four 2026-08-31 captures that failed are re-run with prefixes against a scratch target.

Files: =config.el= (the three sites and the =:DROPPED:= validation) and =config-test.el=.
:END:

Some =id= and =target= arguments do not resolve through =claude-code-ide-org--id-find=, so an 8-character prefix that every other tool accepts either errors or, worse, silently selects nothing. That pushes the caller back to typing a full UUID from memory, the failure prefix expansion exists to prevent. Designed 2026-09-24 on the bounded path: fix the three known sites, and prove completeness with a test driven by the tool registry rather than by another search.

*Established:*
- =org_capture='s target: four captures under =9e627dc0= failed with =Cannot find target ID= and succeeded with the full UUID (2026-08-31). The cause is that the prefix is checked and then passed on unexpanded (read 2026-09-24).
- =claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice= compares its id with =equal=, so a prefix reads as a clean =0 slices refreshed= (2026-09-01).
- A prefix in =:DROPPED:= is ignored: on 8a2eb687, =4acd8ad0= kept its checkbox for four days (2026-09-21).
- =--id-find='s docstring records fourteen sites fixed the first time. These three surfaced by use, which suggests that sweep went by call shape.

*Ruled out:* that fixing =--at-id= covered every tool taking an id. A commit claimed so, and =claude-code-ide-org-amend= did its own =org-id-find= first.


* TODO A/B the clock record across the phase-3 fixes, since much of what is logged now is bogus :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       445ec1f1-095a-462b-abbd-7a3a00925ddb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 15:10]
:CATEGORY: Clock
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 15:11]
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-31, immediately after applying a review pass:* "this
tells that much of our logged activity is bogus data. we should do an A/B
comparison with B starting after this slice or whatever to check for a
qualitative difference."

*The evidence that prompted it, which is unusually good evidence.* The apply
pass that had just been accepted contained a span on
[[id:c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b][c8a97d9d]] rendered as
=[13:53]--[14:04] writes 0:01 in 1= — eleven minutes of headline interval
crediting one minute — for a stretch that was *continuous* work with no idle gap
and no permission block. That heading's work is fully reconstructible from four
commits and a test suite that went 448 to 454, so this is not a case of guessing
whether the number is wrong. It is wrong, and by roughly an order of magnitude,
on a stretch we can independently account for.

The same pass carried =[13:13]--[13:48] writes 0:14 in 3= on
[[id:c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368][c31b6c76]], which spans an Emacs
crash and an AFK stretch and so is wrong in the *other* direction. Both were
applied before the discrepancy was noticed, so the file now contains them.

*Why this is not simply [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] or
[[id:98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d][98c302e0]].* Those two are about the
review buffer *saying* what it means — a headline interval that is not the
interval written, and a readout whose two numbers cannot be told apart. Fixing
them makes the human able to *see* the discrepancy at review time. Neither
claims the underlying attribution is right, and this heading is the question of
whether it is. A perfectly legible rendering of a bogus number is still a bogus
number.

*The design question, and it is the whole task:* what is the B in an A/B where
the thing being measured is accuracy, and there is no ground truth? Some
candidates, none settled:

- *Reconstructible stretches as the yardstick.* Work with commits, test-count
  deltas and a debrief can be bounded from outside the clock system entirely.
  Both spans above were caught precisely because such a record existed. That
  suggests sampling stretches with strong external evidence rather than
  comparing aggregates.
- *Turns per interval*, which
  [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] proposes recording
  anyway, is an independent signal: a span crediting one minute across many
  turns is self-evidently suspect without needing to know the true number.
- *Aggregate shape* — total logged hours per day before and after — is the
  easiest comparison and the weakest, since a change in total does not say which
  direction is right.

*A boundary marker exists, so B has a defined start.* A ends at commit =b34a12f=
(2026-08-31T14:05:07-05:00), the last apply before this was raised. Recording it
here because "after this slice" is not measurable later and a commit is.

*One caution worth stating now, while it is cheap.* If A really is largely
bogus, that is a claim about data already written to =TODO.org= and =DONE.org=,
and the tempting follow-up — correcting or discarding it — should not be reached
for casually. The record's value is partly that it is what actually happened,
mistakes included; [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is this
project's precedent that a plausible fabricated number is worse than an absent
one, and a retroactively "corrected" span is exactly that. Deciding what, if
anything, to do about A is part of this heading and should not be assumed.

*Measured 2026-09-01, in answer to whether this and
[[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] complement each other.
They share a corpus and a histogram; they do not share a design, and the
measurement argues against the before/after framing.*

*B is n=19.* Splitting every queue file at =b34a12f= gives *849* =pause= ->
=resume= gaps before and *19* after — one session. No comparison is available
now and none will be for weeks.

*The bigger problem is a confound, and it was found by testing the obvious
alternative rather than assuming it.* The August band (no gap between 1061s and
2070s) has closed, and the tempting explanation is undersampling: a 150-gap
histogram is sparse, so holes appear by chance. *That is refuted.* Drawing 200
random 150-gap samples from the current corpus, *zero* produced an empty band.
So the August distribution was genuinely different, not merely thinner.

*Which means the gap distribution drifts substantially month to month — and all
of that drift is inside regime A*, since 849 of the 868 gaps predate the
boundary. A before/after comparison on a calendar boundary therefore cannot
attribute a difference to the code: *when* and *what changed* are entangled, and
the =when= term is demonstrably large.

*And there is a second, independent reason to expect no signal.* Almost
everything the slice shipped was *presentational*.
[[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] says so of itself — the
fix "is presentational and deliberately does not touch what is extracted" — and
the same is true of the readout rework, the wording audit, the prefix change and
the marking behaviour. The extraction that produces recorded minutes is
unchanged. The only causal path from the slice to the *numbers* runs through the
human deciding differently at review, which is slow, mediated, and not separable
from drift.

*So the before/after framing should be dropped, and this body already contains
the better instrument.* Its own candidate list opens with /"reconstructible
stretches as the yardstick"/ — work bounded from outside the clock system by
commits, test-count deltas and a debrief. That needs no regime boundary, is
immune to drift, and answers the question actually asked: not "did the numbers
change" but "were they ever right". The two spans that prompted this heading
were caught exactly that way.

*What [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]] still contributes*,
and it is real: the same histogram feeds both, and the September occurrence has
to re-derive it anyway. The overlap is the *scan*, not the *comparison*.

*Checked rather than asserted, 2026-09-01, after the claim "nothing changed how
agent time is recorded" was questioned. It was too strong, and the correction
sharpens the conclusion rather than softening it.*

*The computation is byte-identical across the slice.* Every function turning
guideposts into a duration is unchanged against =main=: =--span-work-runs=,
=--apply-idle-floor=, =--aggregate-guideposts=, =--span-turn-seconds=,
=--review-apply-clock=, =--format-clock-line=, =--clock-minutes=,
=--subtract-intervals=.

*Two things do change what lands, though:*

- =--review-apply-item= gained the =inhibit-read-only= binding, so writes that
  previously *failed* now succeed. Not hypothetical — an apply on 2026-08-25
  reported =Applied 0 item(s); 1 failed= for exactly this reason, and the
  ceremony hit it again on 2026-09-01.
- *Auto-marking* ([[id:b6e229c7-3035-4c0e-943b-710a12bfd7d1][b6e229c7]]) means
  items arrive pre-marked, so one =x= applies more per pass — including items a
  human would previously have missed or never marked at all.

\(=--review-items-from-queue= also changed, for the stranded-span drop, but
those items write nothing by definition.)

*Both push B toward more recorded time, which is precisely the wrong direction
for this experiment.* More recorded minutes is not more accurate minutes. A
B-over-A increase in totals would be fully explained by fewer failed writes and
more items applied, with no bearing on whether any individual figure was ever
right — so the one comparison a before/after design makes easy is the one that
cannot be interpreted.

*Same conclusion by a second route:* the question is "were these minutes real",
and only an instrument outside the clock system answers it.


* DOING An Emacs outage strands every org write, because the tools are Emacs :code:bounded:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       3ad389be-2797-46c3-a51d-9f603053955a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 13:50]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:
:PLAN:
When an =org_*= call fails because Emacs is unreachable, a plain-shell hook
writes a marker naming the tool and its arguments into the session's queue
directory, since Emacs, being what failed, cannot write it. The next
=SessionStart= reports it beside the stale-interval report, so what could not
be recorded is itself recorded (the user, 2026-09-21). Declined: a CLI writer
for todo and clock events, whose synthetic events would blind the staleness
check exactly when the system is least healthy; and doing nothing, which
leaves the failure silent per call. =7fa68d5c='s capture fallback builds on the
same path afterwards.

Open for the implementing session: which hook event sees an MCP timeout
(=PostToolUse= with an error response, or a separate failure event). Measure it
against a stopped Emacs before building.

Answered 2026-09-23, superseding the paragraph above: the hook is
=PostToolUseFailure=, matcher =mcp__emacs-tools__.*=. It fires for a
transport failure only, so the hook needs no filtering: a refusal arrives as
=PostToolUse= and never reaches it. Its payload carries =tool_name=, the full
=tool_input= and =error=, which is everything the marker needs. It cannot
cover an Emacs that was already down when the session started, because the
tools never register and no tool hook fires. That case is =SessionStart='s
(the server's absence is visible there), or it goes unrecorded.

*The build, approved 2026-09-23 (the user).* It extends the first paragraph
above rather than contradicting it, and replaces its report location: the
marker no longer depends on the stale-interval report. Bounded, since it is
one hook in the existing family.

*One hook, three steps in order, each surviving the failure of the next.*
=bin/hooks/tool-failure=, plain shell, on =PostToolUseFailure= with matcher
=mcp__emacs-tools__.*=, wired in =.claude/settings.json= and
=hooks/hooks.json=. It is task tracking, so it is not gated on
=time_tracking=, and it always exits 0.

*First, the marker.* One JSON line in =<session_id>.tool-failures.jsonl=
beside the queue file, never in the queue itself, so the review pass cannot
take it for an event. It records =ts=, =tool_name=, =tool_input=, =error=,
the probe state, and =may_land=: true when the call timed out, since a
timed-out call runs on and can still write, false when refused.

*Second, the probe.* =timeout 3 emacsclient -e= reads =recursion-depth=,
the active minibuffer, and any public =claude-code-ide-org-= function on the
Lisp stack (=mapbacktrace=). Any failure to probe reads as unknown. It costs
up to 3 s, and only on the failure path.

*Third, the guidance*, returned as =additionalContext=, one per state:
- Refused: nothing was written; do not hand-edit the tracked org files;
  tell the user. When an Emacs process is still alive (the wedge
  signature), also take one =sample <pid> 1= into
  =~/.claude/emacs-samples/=, at most once per session by sentinel, for
  489d6f61.
- Probe timed out (Emacs busy): the call may still land; do not retry a
  write; tell the user.
- Alive, org tool on the stack: Emacs is waiting on a prompt; ask the user
  to answer it.
- Alive and idle: check the argument names against the tool's schema.
  This is ecbbec2e's stopgap until its own fix lands (the user, 2026-09-23,
  chose to ship it here).
- Unknown: say that the probe failed, and stop.

*The report.* =SessionStart='s ceremony report lists unreported markers,
saying for each whether the write may still have landed or did not, so
nothing is redone blindly. Reporting renames the file, so a marker is
reported once, and it waits for a session with Emacs back up.

*Out of scope.* 7fa68d5c's capture fallback, which becomes a branch of this
hook on a refused connection only, since a timed-out capture may still land.
An Emacs already down at session start, where no tool is offered and no
hook fires.

*Testing.* =bin/tool-failure-test=, with a fake =emacsclient= and =sample=
on =PATH=: one case per state; the marker's fields; the once-per-session
sample sentinel; an unwritable queue directory, where the hook must still
exit 0 and still return guidance. Shown to fail against a no-op hook. Then
one live run per state, as in the probes above, and one =SessionStart=
report.
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 13:52]
CLOCK: [2026-09-23 Wed 15:55]--[2026-09-23 Wed 15:59] =>  0:04
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-09-23 Wed 15:55] \\
  measure which hook sees an MCP failure
- [2026-09-23 Wed 15:55]--[2026-09-23 Wed 15:59] measure which hook sees an MCP failure

- [2026-09-24 Thu 09:13]--[2026-09-24 Thu 09:14] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:

The queue's premise is that work can be recorded with no Emacs running. That
holds for the hooks, which are plain shell, and fails for the org tools,
which are served from inside Emacs: when Emacs is gone or wedged a call is
refused or times out and records nothing, while the guidepost hooks keep
appending, so a total failure looks partial. The fix is one
=PostToolUseFailure= hook that records the failure, probes Emacs, and tells
the session what to do. The design is in =:PLAN:=.

*Established* (measured 2026-09-23 on Claude Code 2.1.280, with throwaway
headless sessions logging raw hook payloads):
- =PostToolUseFailure= fires on a transport failure only, a mid-session
  timeout or refused connection. A refusal from a healthy Emacs arrives as
  =PostToolUse=. The payload carries =tool_name=, =tool_input= and =error=,
  and its =additionalContext= reaches the model.
- An Emacs already down at session start offers no tools, so no tool hook
  fires.
- Claude Code does not catch a misnamed argument: it reaches Emacs and
  times out.
- A three-second =emacsclient= probe separates refused, busy (the probe
  times out), blocked on a prompt (a public org tool on the Lisp stack) and
  idle. Depth, minibuffer and echo-area readings alone do not: one
  prompt-blocked run read as idle.
- A timed-out call keeps running and completes after the client gives up,
  so a timed-out write may still land.
- =sample <pid> 1= needs no privileges: 1.6 s, about 300 KB.
- On 2026-08-31 Emacs wedged for about twenty minutes, answering
  "connection refused" from a live process, and two headings could not be
  closed. That is distinct from 72463b68, where tools hung while Emacs was
  healthy.

*Ruled out:* a CLI writer for todo and clock events, and restoring
=QUEUE_APPEND_ID/STATE/FROM=, which 46d225c7 removed as dead. A synthetic
event carries no =tool_response= to recover =from= from, so the staleness
check would go blind on exactly the events written when the system is
least healthy.


* TODO An archive pass wrote one subtree twice, and the mechanism is still unknown :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       381ea07d-633f-4640-abbb-89472a07acba
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:14]
:END:

On 2026-08-31 a live archive pass reported 65 and wrote 66. One story and its
six children landed twice in DONE.org, ids and all. It is guarded but not
understood.

*The arithmetic says where it is not.* =n= increments once per
=org-archive-subtree= call and the pass reported 65, so exactly 65 calls ran and
one pasted its subtree twice -- inside the call, on org's own paste path.
Double-consumption of a marker would have reported 66. The heading it duplicated
was also the last in the file and therefore the *first* consumed under
reverse-order iteration, so no earlier deletion existed to disturb its marker.
The marker-collapse explanation is excluded.

*It did not reproduce.* Same file, same code, fresh buffer: 65 archived, no
duplicates. So it is a property of the live buffer's state rather than of the
data. That buffer had been reverted, had its read-only flag cleared, and had
just been rewritten by drawer consolidation and separation normalisation in the
same ceremony.

*What stands in for a fix.* =claude-code-ide-org-archive-finished= now checks
the result rather than the method: after a sweep, no =:ID:= may appear twice in
the archive target, or it signals -- which the ceremony turns into a held
repeater and a reported failure. Every other signal looked right at the time,
the count and the levels included; only counting ids found it.

Worth attempting first: re-run the sweep against a buffer put through the same
sequence -- revert, consolidate, normalise, then archive -- to see whether the
combination is what does it.


* TODO [0/2] Make an archived day hold that day's record :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       f9fe9fac-a423-4ede-bc42-1d201c2c3d82
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 12:07]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:KIND:     slice
:COOKIE_DATA: checkbox recursive
:BLOCKER:  d6fc47a5-b84d-4287-b973-aa5b37410270 591e7af3-4f37-4f90-86ba-62400c6e649b
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 12:07]
:END:

Seeded 2026-08-31 from the proposal reviewed in conversation: DONE.org's
datetree should gather what happened on a day, not only what was filed. Both
members move *records* into it rather than changing what a task is.

*Its work is blocked from outside this slice.*
[[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] builds the datetree and
belongs to [[id:b36e6369-a210-4540-adc7-0c0c188e9791][b36e6369]], so nothing
here starts until that lands. That is precisely why these two were kept *out* of
b36e6369 rather than added to it: including them would have left a 27-member
slice unable to close until its own downstream consumers were done too.

*Use org's own datetree machinery, and note which entry point.*
=org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= takes a comparison function per level and
=org-datetree-comparefun-from-regex= builds one; negating its result gives the
descending order [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] asks for.
Measured in batch 2026-08-31 -- entries inserted 08-10, 08-31, 07-05, 08-20
landed in strict descending order at year, month and day. Only
=org-datetree-find-date-create= hard-codes ascending, which is what made a
hand-rolled builder look necessary at first.

*What a day node means, and does not.* It holds what org would allocate to that
date. A task's *earlier* clock intervals stay where they were recorded, so a day
node is not a substitute for a clocktable when the question is how much time
went where.

Planned:
