:END:

The =/next-session= idiom is used but not codified. One line outside the file
mentions it -- =CLAUDE.md:126= -- and it documents the *artifact* while
explicitly denying it is a convention: "a plan, not a convention". Nothing
states when one is written, what it must contain, or what "consumed" means.

*First assertion: every item states whether it carries an unresolved decision,
and whose it is.* The 2026-08-21 prompt did this correctly for its item 5 ("the
user decides; do not pick silently") and not for item 1, where "Possible shapes,
none chosen" sat in the heading body instead -- so the executing session chose a
shape with no checkpoint. That is the gap in one sentence: the prompt planned at
the *sequencing* altitude (survey, slice, order, cut line) and not at the
*design* altitude, and only the second needs a marker.

*Evidence this is a recurring class rather than one slip.* =* Documentation=
already holds six headings of the "a convention is nowhere written down" shape:
[[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]],
[[id:9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75][9651d4c8]],
[[id:241a4c54-ed59-4780-b602-2a86efee978a][241a4c54]],
[[id:bba1088c-d8e1-49f7-bef8-b070860f5257][bba1088c]],
[[id:8707de36-93a7-4e34-b4ba-48008c7e1e3b][8707de36]] and this one.
[[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]] is the mechanical answer
to the class and would check this format once it exists.

*Home: a new user-scoped skill, not the org-dev skill.* org-dev's trigger is
changing this repo's own code and verifying it took effect; writing a prompt at
end-of-session is neither, so the rule would silently never load -- the same
failure CLAUDE.md names for path-scoped rules. And the idiom is org-free,
Emacs-free and repo-free, which makes it the most portable thing here.
[[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] measured that only
=~/.claude/= travels; this is its easiest first instance, a skill with *zero*
repo coupling against the org skill's measured 16 lines. The format can
additionally self-host in the prompt file's own header, which needs no trigger
at all.

*Open:* there is no end-of-session trigger to hang the writing rule on.
Candidate is firing on an explicit request to write or revise the prompt, with
the self-hosted header as the backstop.

Overlap to resolve, noted 2026-09-03: the slice NEXT (:ID: abce1850 — at most
one slice carries it, meaning "this is the slice to pick up next") occupies the
same portfolio-nomination niche as this prompt format, and neither artifact
mentions the other. Whichever mechanism lands first decides whether they are one
mechanism or two, and records the decision in the other's terms — the twin
asymmetry at the portfolio tier is otherwise invisible from inside either.
Related and deliberately deferred: one-NEXT-per-slice has no lint or report yet;
observe the days-old rule before building machinery for it.

*One input to the format, from the user 2026-09-19:* a slice member's part of the next-session plan is written *from its =:PLAN:= drawer*, which is where the plan now lives (the plan-file link is retired for new work; see =ff1bec00=). So the prompt is a composition over member drawers, in slice order, rather than an authored document — which makes "every item states whether it carries an unresolved decision" a property the drawer should already hold, and the composition step the place a checker can assert it.

*Second input, 2026-09-19: the trigger.* The home decided above — a user-scoped skill — is on-demand, the right side of the always-loaded budget ([[id:bf7e0b8e-f83a-4d4a-9950-3ab23a225156][bf7e0b8e]]); but a skill loads only when its description matches the prompt, and end-of-session prompts are various. A skill that never loads is [[id:befaed0a-18ed-445e-b997-8b9c2965ad45][befaed0a]]'s silent failure in another coat, the same shape [[id:436e0991-c8ac-40fb-860d-a6c4c381bee5][436e0991]] found for path-scoped rules. Two consequences. The description is written for the trigger and tested against real end-of-session prompts rather than assumed — CLAUDE.md's engineering rule already exempts trigger-matching from deterministic testing, so a documented manual pass. And the decision marker is a literal token a script can assert on the composed prompt, because that assertion is the one part of the format that cannot fail to load.


* TODO Open today's node
SCHEDULED: <2026-08-24 Mon 07:00 ++1d>
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Meta
:ID:       2aff1e6d-71cf-4b3f-8a3f-b3fc63d07c99
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 13:20]
:END:

The ritual: a standing daily prompt to actually do review and planning.

*It reminds; it does not create.* Creating today's node is
[[id:9575e65b-b589-465a-830c-286541780f96][9575e65b]]'s job and happens when
work happens. A repeater that created the node instead would manufacture one per
calendar day whether or not anything occurred, and re-couple the node's id to a
schedule -- both of the things the on-demand resolver exists to avoid.

*A category is the one place in this repo a repeater is safe*
([[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]): a repeater never
reaches DONE, so it can never sit under a completable ancestor, and a category
carries no keyword and completes nothing.


* TODO Comma-escape headline lines when documenting org structure inside a body :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Skill
:ID:       8a23d6ec-ca45-454d-b65f-e46ea9204816
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 10:03]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 10:03] \\
  document the escape, decide on enforcement
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 10:35]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:37] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-21 Fri 10:35]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:37] evaluate inline src_org for snippets
:END:

Proposed by the user 2026-08-21, while asking for a materialized example of a
proposed subtree: if showing org structure inside a heading body works, make it
a general habit rather than a one-off, since it would also help linting.

*It works, and the mechanism is not the one the question assumed.* The block
type is irrelevant. Measured over four variants, counting what =org-map-entries=
returns from a buffer holding one real heading, an example block, and a second
real heading:

| block            | escaped | org-map-entries sees                        |
|------------------+---------+---------------------------------------------|
| =#+BEGIN_SRC org=  | no      | Real, *Fake A*, *Fake B*, Second            |
| =#+BEGIN_SRC org=  | =,*=      | Real, Second                                |
| =#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE=  | no      | Real, *Fake A*, *Fake B*, Second            |
| =#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE=  | =,*=      | Real, Second                                |

=SRC= and =EXAMPLE= behave identically in both columns. *The comma escape is the
whole mechanism*, and execution has nothing to do with it -- disabling
evaluation would not have helped, because the risk is the outline, not the code.

*Unescaped, example org is not example org -- it is real headings.* A raw =*
Fake A= inside a block is walked into by =org-map-entries= and matched by a bare
=^\*+ = regex alike, so it would corrupt =org_outline=,
=claude-code-ide-org--container-heading-p= (a body full of examples would make
any heading a container), the statistics cookies derived from that, and
=bin/lint-org= -- each silently, and each in a way that looks like a content bug
rather than a formatting one.

*So the rule is "comma-escape headline lines", not "use example blocks".*
Stating it the second way is what would make it fail: someone reaches for a
block, gets the syntax highlighting they wanted, and inherits the defect the
block never protected against.

*Related precedent already in the suite.*
=claude-code-ide-org-test--decoy-heading= pins that a *drawer*-shaped line
inside =#+begin_example= is not a drawer, and =claude-code-ide-org--find-drawer=
skips it. Headings are the case that was never covered, and they behave the
opposite way -- the parser is not consulted, so the block does not protect them.

*Where this should land if adopted:* =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=, which
is path-scoped to =**/*.org= and already owns the file-shape conventions. A
first specimen is live in
[[id:25ec37b0-460f-404d-be83-8a4b4d355cdc][25ec37b0]]'s body.

*Open:* whether =bin/lint-org= should *enforce* it -- flagging an unescaped
headline line inside a block -- or whether documenting it is enough. Enforcement
is attractive because the failure is silent, but it needs care: a block quoting
a *diff* or a shell transcript may legitimately contain a line starting with
=*=.

*Inline =src_LANG{BODY}= was raised by the user 2026-08-21 as a possible
companion for short snippets, and as another aid to linting. Measured, it is
neither -- and on the linting question it cuts slightly the wrong way.*

*It cannot help, because the hazard it would guard against cannot occur inline.*
The defect this heading exists for is a =*= at *column 0 on its own line*. An
inline snippet sits inside a paragraph by definition, so its =*= is never at
column 0. Confirmed alongside the alternatives -- =src_org{,* x}=, ==*
asterisk== and =~* asterisk~= each left =org-map-entries= reporting exactly the
two real headings. There is nothing here for an inline form to protect, and
===verbatim=== is equally safe and already this repo's convention.

*Where the hazard does live, inline src fails.* An attempted multi-line inline
block leaked:

#+BEGIN_EXAMPLE
src_org{,* one
,* two}
#+END_EXAMPLE

Unescaped, the second line's =*= is at column 0 and =org-map-entries= returned a
heading titled =two}=. Inline forms cannot span lines, so they cannot express
the multi-line structure that made this heading necessary.

*It adds an execution surface that ===verbatim=== does not have.*
=org-babel-map-inline-src-blocks= finds =src_emacs-lisp{(+ 1 2)}= as one
executable block, so anything that walks a buffer for babel --
=org-babel-execute-buffer=, export with default =:exports= -- treats it as code
rather than as text. For files that tools read and rewrite constantly, that is a
new hazard bought for no linting benefit. Disabling evaluation per block is
possible and is exactly the kind of per-use precaution that gets forgotten once.

*So: reserve it for rendering, if these files are ever exported, and do not
adopt it as a habit.* ===verbatim=== stays the convention for inline code, and
the comma escape stays the rule for block-level structure. Two mechanisms, each
for the case it actually addresses.

*One honest limit on the above.* Whether org parses these as =inline-src-block=
/ =verbatim= objects was *not* verified -- three attempts at an
=org-element-map= probe returned nil even for ===verbatim===, which org
certainly does parse, so the probe was broken rather than the syntax. The
outline and babel results above stand on their own mechanisms and do not depend
on it.

*Inverted 2026-09-19 against the drift analysis (§4.1) and the slice =f6d160c2=.* This heading's own measurement made the case: unescaped example headings corrupt =org_outline=, =--container-heading-p=, the cookies and =lint-org=, each *silently* — so the writer who needed the rule never learns they broke it, and a rule whose violation is invisible to its violator is the one prose cannot carry. The deliverable is therefore the enforcement, and the prose is the residue. Two mechanisms, strongest first: =org_amend= and =org_capture= escape a =^\*+ = line inside a =#+BEGIN_= block on write, which makes the omission impossible on the tool path; and =bin/lint-org= errors on an unescaped one, which catches the hand-edit path at commit. The conventions then need one sentence naming the mechanism, in =org-conventions.md= beside the existing passage on documenting org structure — and given [[id:436e0991-c8ac-40fb-860d-a6c4c381bee5][436e0991]] that sentence is documentation, not the safeguard. Candidate for the slice's enforcement audit, [[id:3cd7b7d3-25eb-4f30-ae90-9174a6876674][3cd7b7d3]], as the first clause it would convert.

*The enforcement half is done, 2026-09-21* (under 3cd7b7d3): =org_amend= and =org_capture='s note comma-escape raw =^\*+ = lines inside any =#+begin_=…=#+end_= block on the way in, idempotently, so the convention no longer has to be recalled on the tool path. Still true and still prose: a hand edit, and text that defers to the queue, are not covered.


* TODO Decide how far the review-apply cycle can be automated, given the interactive constraint :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       361e2708-8124-4bd1-9c44-f97ca97aca4a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 08:08]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 08:09] \\
  decide the automation boundary
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-21: the review-apply cycle "seems pretty
straightforward lately", so how much of it still needs a human at all?

*It is three layers, and only one of them is actually walled.* Keeping them
apart is the whole of this heading, because "automate the review" reads as one
question and is not:

1. *Deciding what a span means* -- computing a heading for an unassigned
   interval. Pure computation over the queue and whatever external evidence is
   admitted; it touches no org buffer and no live Emacs state. Automatable in
   principle, and the thing standing in its way is evidence, not architecture --
   see [[id:5a6d2c75-9a42-4dd4-b4d6-670aeebc5b46][5a6d2c75]].
2. *Confirming* -- the human keystroke per item. Not walled, just currently
   per-item.
3. *Writing* -- applying through native
   =org-todo=/=org-clock-in=/=org-clock-out=. This is the wall, and CLAUDE.md
   names it as load-bearing: org's native state-change logging only completes
   correctly inside a genuinely interactive command, and a non-interactive
   =emacsclient -e= hits the exact hang the queue exists to route around. The
   concrete mechanism is the =#+TODO:= cookies -- =WAITING(w@/!)= and
   =CANCELLED(c@)= carry =@= and *prompt for a note*, so those two keywords and
   only those two block a non-interactive apply.

*So "completely" is available for layer 1 and not for layer 3.* The honest
target is that layer 2 collapses: a pass in which every item is unambiguous
should be appliable with one confirmation for the whole pass rather than one per
item, still inside =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review= so layer 3 keeps its
interactive context. Nothing about that weakens the constraint -- the command is
still human-triggered and still interactive.

*What has to be defined before any of it is built:* what "unambiguous" means
mechanically. A rule that admits a bulk confirmation has to be one a human can
trust without reading the items, which is a higher bar than the per-item readout
meets today.

*The objection that binds, and its exact scope.*
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] records that a model
reasoning over the queue file alone has *strictly less* information than the
threshold algorithm -- the same timestamps and heading IDs, minus any domain
knowledge -- and that a fluent proposal is harder to reject than a transparently
mechanical one. That holds against any automation whose only input is the queue.
It does not settle the case for an automation that reads the session
transcripts, which carry what the queue does not.


* TODO An unassigned span's readout omits the evidence that would make its heading obvious :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       5a6d2c75-9a42-4dd4-b4d6-670aeebc5b46
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 08:08]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 08:09] \\
  add transcript evidence to span readout
:END:

Stated by the user 2026-08-21, as the one hitch left in an otherwise
straightforward review pass: the readout "lacks adequate context of what was
happening immediately before it to make the assignment obvious, but I believe
it's there in the data."

*Not a re-file of [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]].* That
one shipped 2026-08-14 and put commits and heading creations inside an
unassigned span's window on screen. It helped and did not close the gap; this is
the next increment against the same complaint.

*Where the data is, and the distinction that decides the approach.* The queue
carries timestamps, =session_id=, =agent_id=, =agent_type= and =source= -- and
0c8644ff's objection that this is *strictly less* than the threshold algorithm
already has is correct and not disputed here. The session *transcripts* are a
different corpus: [[id:420816ec-677e-45a5-b898-fb5db70d214b][420816ec]]'s
research half read 69 of them. A transcript records what the session was asked
and which files it touched in a given window, which is exactly the missing
context, and extracting it is mechanical rather than a judgement call.

*Candidate evidence, cheapest first:* files touched in the window, taken from
the transcript's tool calls; the prompt text that opened the enclosing turn;
and, for the case where the competing activity was not this project at all,
[[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]]'s power assertions.

*Render evidence, do not recommend a heading.* "This session edited =config.el=
around 10:20" is a fact the human weighs; "this span was probably c6fc6f46" is a
proposal, and 0c8644ff's anchoring argument applies to the second and not the
first. Holding that line is what keeps this heading independent of the
automation decision in [[id:361e2708-8124-4bd1-9c44-f97ca97aca4a][361e2708]]
rather than a prerequisite for it.

*Test corpus is already sitting in the queue:* three unassigned spans as of
2026-08-21 -- =16:28--16:54= and =17:18--17:22= on 08-20, =21:08--21:11= on
08-20. Whether the added evidence makes each one's heading obvious is answerable
against those three before anything is generalised.

*Two cues the user asked for by name, 2026-08-22, after allocating a batch and
only afterwards realising what it contained.* Both are about what the buffer
*already knows* and does not show, which is this heading's subject rather than a
new one.

*"Am I actually catching meta-work? I didn't even notice because there's no
visual cue in the review dialog."* A span assigned to the =Review and planning=
day node and a span assigned to an ordinary task look identical, so the one
measurement the datetree exists to produce is invisible at the moment it is
being made. The day node is mechanically distinguishable -- it is the
=:DATE_TREE:= heading's descendant -- so this is a rendering decision, not an
inference.

*Coverage, per span rather than per item.* Once
[[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] makes explicit =clock_in=
pairs authoritative, most runs in a span will already have an owner. The buffer
should say which: "12 of 15 runs attributed, 3 need you" turns an hour-long
yes/no into three small local questions, and shows the human where their
judgement is actually required. Rendered from the same data, and it is the
difference between allocating and *confirming*.

*Why this pairing matters more than either alone.* The evidence this heading was
filed about answers "which heading?" The coverage line answers the prior
question -- "is this even mine to decide?" -- which on the measured day was the
one that went wrong: an hour that the queue had already attributed was presented
as a single undifferentiated choice.

*Refined by the user 2026-09-04, and the refinement changes the approach rather
than adding to it:* "when presenting an unassigned span for review, look back in
the transcripts and summarize what was happening."

**The word is /summarize/, and the paragraph above says the opposite** —
"extracting it is mechanical rather than a judgement call", with candidate
evidence listed as files touched and prompt text. Those are *signals*, and the
ask is for *prose*. A reviewer looking at a span does not want four file paths
and a prompt fragment; they want a sentence that names the work. So this heading
now spans a seam it previously sat on one side of, and which side it lands on is
the open question:

- *Mechanical*, as originally scoped: surface the evidence, let the human
  summarize. Cheap, deterministic, no new transport, and it degrades to the
  =5ff5a4b8= behaviour that already helped without closing the gap.
- *Judged*, which is what "summarize" literally asks for: a subagent reads the
  transcript window and writes the sentence. That is =295cde3a='s mechanism —
  the join is exact and measured, 28 of 28 =resume= events matching a transcript
  timestamp within two seconds — and =0c8644ff= already settled the transport
  (=claude -p= asynchronously, since there is often no live session at review
  time).

*A worked instance, produced by hand the same day.* The span
=[2026-09-03 Thu 14:10]--[14:34]= came up in conversation and was reconstructed
from the queue file plus the session transcript: four turns, 0:05 of actual
running inside 0:24 of wall clock, spent relaying a code review, diagnosing why
local =main= was 165 commits stale, checking that the posted comments fell
inside the right diff, and confirming =main= was still behind. That
reconstruction took one =jq= over the queue and a read of the turns, and it made
the assignment obvious immediately — "Review and planning", continuous with the
interval already filed there at 14:34. **That is precisely this heading's
output, done manually**, which is the strongest evidence yet that it is worth
building; note it needed both corpora, not just the transcript.

*One caution the instance also produced.* Part of the supporting evidence had
already been destroyed by the time it was wanted: =.git/FETCH_HEAD= carried a
timestamp inside the span, and a later =git fetch= in the same session overwrote
it. Filesystem mtimes are not a corpus. Whatever this builds must read the queue
and the transcripts, which are append-only, and not the working tree, which is
not.


* TODO Index a heading's git history from a drawer, so the body never has to narrate it :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Skill
:ID:       41cf5830-fc2e-4dc6-b243-a0c2718a53a3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 11:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 16:44] \\
  captured keywordless; state never set
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-20 alongside the retention policy at
[[id:3063c3e5-0dd0-4354-8985-271b0313c5d1][3063c3e5]], and separable from it:
once a body may be revised wholesale, the evolution it used to carry lives only
in git, and nothing points a reader at it.

Proposal: a timestamped commit log in a drawer -- possibly =:LOGBOOK:=, possibly
its own -- listing the commits that touched this heading. Not the history
itself, an index into it. The framing that makes it worth building is the
user's: "how did we get here?" is a *rare* question, so the answer belongs one
hop away rather than inline, which is precisely the trade the current bodies get
backwards.

*The query, settled 2026-08-20 and measured.* The first proposal here was =git
log -S<id>=, and it is wrong. Against =02aaae22=: of the 24 commits =-S=
reports, *only 4 actually touched the heading*, and it misses 23 of the 27 that
did. Both failures follow from what =-S= does -- it matches the ID string
anywhere in the *file*, so a commit is reported merely for adding an
=[[id:02aaae22...]]= link inside some other heading's body, while an edit to
this heading's own prose never touches the =:ID:= line and so is invisible.

The user's formulation is the correct one: commits touching anything from the
heading line to the character before the next heading. That is =git log -L
<start>,<end>:TODO.org=, which follows the range backwards through history as
the file changes. Locate the range at HEAD by =:ID:=, then hand the line numbers
to =-L=.

Anchoring =-L= on a regex instead was tried and is worse: =/:ID: <uuid>/= starts
the range *below* the headline, losing retitles, tag edits and cookie updates --
20 commits against the line range's 27, with no false positives. A title regex
is worse still, since titles and statistics cookies both change.

*Range compression works but does not transform the problem.* Of 302 commits to
TODO.org, 27 touched =02aaae22=; consecutive runs are 4, 3, 2, 2, 2, 2 and
twelve singletons, so =a..b= notation gives 18 entries instead of 27 -- a third
off.

*That number now argues against the drawer.* Eighteen entries for one heading,
in a file whose largest containers will accumulate more, is not an index a human
scans -- it is a second body, which is the thing this heading exists to prevent.
Derived on demand still looks stronger, but on *volume* grounds rather than the
staleness grounds first recorded here.

Remaining open: which drawer, if stored; and what generates it. A commit
touching a heading is detectable at commit time, and =.githooks/pre-commit= is
the only thing that runs then -- at the cost of making a lint hook a writer.

/Body restored 2026-08-20 by =org_amend=; the original capture silently
discarded it -- see/ [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]].
/This heading being empty is how the loss was noticed at all: the user asked
where the concept was parked, and there was nothing there./


* TODO Moving a heading between files by hand leaves org-id pointing at the old one :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       8ddd7fa8-e847-42e9-b82c-63bbe4c11b7b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 19:24]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 18:58]--[2026-08-19 Wed 19:03] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-19 Wed 18:58]--[2026-08-19 Wed 19:03] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 19:07]--[2026-08-19 Wed 19:09] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-19 Wed 19:07]--[2026-08-19 Wed 19:09] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 19:18]--[2026-08-19 Wed 19:31] =>  0:13
- [2026-08-19 Wed 19:18]--[2026-08-19 Wed 19:31] assigned at review
:END:

Observed 2026-08-19: an apply pass refused
[[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]]'s =none -> TODO=
because =--at-id= could not find the heading. The heading existed; the
index did not know where.

*Cause.* It was captured with no target, so
[[id:97696fc2-f07b-4586-b280-de6a7d26fb87][97696fc2]] put it in
=~/org/notes.org=, and it was then moved into =TODO.org= by cutting and
re-inserting the subtree text. That never touches =org-id-locations=, so
=org-id-find= kept resolving to a file the heading had left.

*Wider than its cause.* Any hand move between files does this — a
=git= operation, an =Edit= tool call, a refile done as text. Only
=org-refile= maintains the index, and this project edits org text
directly all the time.

*Candidate fixes, none chosen.* Call =org-id-update-id-locations= after
any tool that relocates a subtree; or have =--at-id= fall back to a scan
of the tracked files when the index misses, which is self-healing and
costs a scan only on the failing path. The second also covers moves made
outside Emacs entirely, which the first cannot.

*Left as a question:* whether the =Error:= that reaches the review
buffer should distinguish "no such :ID: anywhere" from "not where the
index says", since the two want different responses and currently read
identically.


* TODO Applied review changes can be destroyed by a git checkout that looks like it reverts only Claude's edit :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       79c224ab-188a-4ed4-a606-c28ee2cfe72d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-19 Wed 08:06]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:02]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:08] =>  0:06
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:02]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:08] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-19 Wed 08:07] \\
  reconciliation check is the durable fix
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:20]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:22] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:20]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:22] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:28]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:29] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:28]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:29] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-19 Wed 08:42]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:46] =>  0:04
- [2026-08-19 Wed 08:42]--[2026-08-19 Wed 08:46] assigned at review
:END:

*Incident, 2026-08-19.* Five queued transitions were applied by the
human, marked consumed in the watermark, and then destroyed —
=09c134c4= -> DONE, =507754ba= -> DONE, =7d739afd= -> TODO,
=9202b39d= -> TODO, and =507754ba= -> DOING. The queue said they were
applied; the file never carried them; =git log -S= confirms the keywords
reached no commit at all. Discovered only because someone asked whether
there were open loops.

*Cause.* Claude ran =git checkout -- TODO.org= to undo a botched edit of
its own, having first run =git status --porcelain=, seen =M TODO.org=,
and assumed the modification was entirely its own. It was not: the same
working-tree change also held the human's applied review results from
after the previous commit. One command reverted both.

*Why nothing caught it.* The watermark lives in =~/.claude/org-updates/=
and the record lives in git, so discarding the second leaves the first
claiming work that no longer exists. Nothing reconciles them, and the
review buffer reads the watermark — so the lost transitions did not
reappear as pending. They were invisible in both directions: gone from
the file, and gone from the queue's idea of what is outstanding.

This is the same class as the note already in CLAUDE.md's memory about
never running a pathspec-less =git commit=, and it generalises: *the
working tree is shared*, so any command that discards uncommitted state
— =checkout=, =restore=, =stash=, =reset --hard= — must be preceded by
reading the diff, not by assuming authorship of it.

*Recovered* by re-queueing the four transitions that mattered. The
=DOING= step of =507754ba= was not re-queued: it is now DONE, and
replaying an intermediate state would write a State line for a
transition that never happened at that time.

*Not recovered:* the =- State ...= log lines the original apply wrote,
which carried the real timestamps. The re-queued events carry today's.

*Candidate fixes, none chosen.* A reconciliation check that reports
watermark entries whose effect is absent from the file would have caught
this the moment it happened, and is the only option that catches it
whoever the culprit is. Cheaper and narrower: a hook or a documented
rule that refuses to discard =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= working-tree changes
without showing the diff first.


* TODO Type a span's annotation at review time, not by hand-editing it after :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       8028e875-3032-4ade-949e-414d9861b928
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 12:27]
:END:

*Observed 2026-08-14, in the file rather than in the abstract.* The user
hand-edited an applied annotation on
[[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]] after the fact,
rewriting =suggested span, accepted at review= to =task definition=. The
commit record confirms the correction was right — =d34c809= landed at
08:58, before the 09:09--09:42 span opened, so that interval really was
task definition and not the implementation it sat next to.

*The mechanism, read rather than assumed*
(=claude-code-ide-org--review-annotation-label=, =config.el= line 3298 as
of today). It returns one of three things:

- the enclosing =clock_in='s note, when the span inherited one — which is
  where a genuinely informative line like =show commits and :CREATED: in
  span windows= comes from;
- the empty string, for a bracketed span whose =clock_in= carried no note;
- =assigned at review= / =suggested span, accepted at review= for an
  *unbracketed* span, reconstructed from loose guideposts.

*The gap is the third case, and it is the case that matters.* An
unbracketed span is unbracketed precisely because no session ever said
what it was working on — so it is the one span with no note to inherit,
and the label it gets instead says only *that* it was reconstructed. Yet
at review the human has just decided what it was; that is what accepting
it means. The information exists, in someone's head, at exactly the
moment the line is written, and is discarded.

*A second finding, and it argues against the naive fix.* The hand-edit
*destroyed the provenance*. That boilerplate is not filler — the function's
docstring makes a deliberate case for it: provenance is "the one thing a
reader cannot recover from the timestamps," namely that the interval was
reconstructed and confirmed rather than clocked as it happened, which
matters for a record whose trustworthiness is a stated project goal.
Overwriting it in place trades one irrecoverable fact for another. They
are not competing, and a typed annotation should *append* to the
provenance rather than replace it — =task definition, accepted at review=
keeps both.

*Open, and worth settling before building:* whether the prompt belongs at
mark time (while the span's evidence lines are still on screen, which is
the argument for it) or at apply; whether it fires for every span or only
unbracketed ones; and whether an empty answer is allowed to fall through
to today's behaviour, which it probably must, since a review pass that
cannot be completed silently is worse than a vague label.

*Not blocking anything, and deliberately small.* It changes what one
function returns and adds one prompt. Related to
[[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]] by adjacency — that
heading put the evidence on screen, and this one would capture the
conclusion the human draws from it — but neither requires the other.

:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue

:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 18:34]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 18:34] \\
  captured from conversation, awaiting your read
CLOCK: [2026-08-13 Thu 19:24]--[2026-08-13 Thu 19:25] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-13 Thu 19:24]--[2026-08-13 Thu 19:25] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 08:21]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:22] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-14 Fri 08:21]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:22] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 08:26]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:28] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-14 Fri 08:26]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:28] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 08:37]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:44] =>  0:07
- [2026-08-14 Fri 08:37]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:44] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 08:49]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:59] =>  0:10
- [2026-08-14 Fri 08:49]--[2026-08-14 Fri 08:59] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 09:02]--[2026-08-14 Fri 09:04] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-14 Fri 09:02]--[2026-08-14 Fri 09:04] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:
:PLAN:

*Noticed 2026-08-13.* =WAIT= had quietly acquired a second, narrower
meaning here — the resting state at the end of a Claude work increment,
"the ball is in the human's court" — which is not what the =#+TODO:=
table says it means. The proposal was to read a =DOING -> WAIT=
transition as a *bracket*: a hard right edge after which guideposts
provably do not belong to that heading, killing the span guess's known
latching defect. It needed no new event kind — =todo= events already
carry state and timestamp whether or not anyone has applied them.

Two caveats came with it. The keyword is ambiguous: a =WAIT= meaning
"waiting on a vendor to email back" carries none of this, and the real
discriminator is probably the event's =source=/=session_id=. And the
bracket is only as reliable as its consistency — a session that ends
leaving a heading =DOING= emits nothing at all.

Composes with [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]]:
commits and =:CREATED:= stamps inside a window are the evidence,
brackets are the bounds on which windows are worth looking at. A
cheaper, mechanical partial answer to
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]], which proposes
asking Claude to narrate the same gaps; and it bounds the damage from
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]] and
[[id:09c134c4-31eb-4f6c-aa10-f836d1d28f16][09c134c4]], since a bracket
constrains what a stray guidepost can be attributed to even when the
guidepost itself is wrong.

*Provenance:* the pattern came from the user's own correction, not from
design — two instances, one person, one afternoon. An observed habit
worth testing, not a measured regularity.

:END:
*Twice overtaken by the code, and only one thing is left.*

*First, 2026-08-14.* The claim above that "nothing ever unsets it" was
wrong, and was already wrong when this heading was written.
=claude-code-ide-org--review-suggest-heading= already cleared the active
heading on every keyword except =DOING= and =PLANNING=, and had done
since =3c6b3f2= on 2026-08-13 — *hours before* this heading was
captured, with the latching-guess comment sitting directly above it.
Found by grep while inventorying keyword touch points; it was never
verified when the heading was written, which is the actual lesson.

*Second, and it closes one of the two survivors that correction left.*
The "denylist should be an allowlist" half shipped in =6a81822=
("Bracket the span guess on any keyword that is not work in progress").
The six named keywords are gone;
=claude-code-ide-org--work-in-progress-keywords= is =("DOING")= and
every other keyword brackets by construction — so adding =REVIEW=
needed no edit here, and neither did retiring =PLANNING=. Confirmed by
reading =config.el= on 2026-09-01.

*What is left is one thing, and it is data rather than logic.* The
bracket fires only when a =todo= event exists to fire it. A session that
ends leaving a heading =DOING= emits nothing, so the guess still latches
across exactly that case. Whether that is answered by a lint
([[id:3bd3402b-3ab6-4ace-b20b-3ff84caa3fb2][3bd3402b]]), a =Stop=-hook
transition, or a bracket inferred at review time is undecided.

The =WAIT= framing in the title is vestigial — the keyword was never the
load-bearing part — and the title should be rewritten if this is picked
up.


* TODO Re-measure the guidepost gap threshold against a month of queue data :code:research:
SCHEDULED: <2026-09-13 Sun +1m>
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 12:20]
:END:

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
on 2026-08-13.* It was filed there because the threshold it re-measures
belongs to that epic's queue — but it carries a =+1m= repeater and so
never reaches =DONE=, which meant the epic could not either, and
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] is blocked on that
epic. A recurring measurement has no terminal state and therefore no
completable parent.
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
  scheduled re-measure, one month out
CLOCK: [2026-09-04 Fri 09:59]--[2026-09-04 Fri 10:00] =>  0:01
- [2026-09-04 Fri 09:59]--[2026-09-04 Fri 10:00] record what active marking would reverse
:END:

Scheduled rather than left to memory: the finding below is real but
thin, and the failure mode is adopting a four-day number as permanent
because nobody re-ran it.

*The survey, 2026-08-13.* 422 queue events, 2026-08-10 to 08-13, across
six sessions. Measured the *queue* rather than =:LOGBOOK:= deliberately:
=:LOGBOOK:= intervals are reconstructions produced by the very threshold
under test, so measuring them would partly measure the parameter.

Decomposed each session's stream into the two gaps that mean different
things:

- =resume= -> =pause= — how long the agent worked. n=111, median *81s*,
  p90 *211s*, one outlier over an hour (the concurrent subagent run).
- =pause= -> =resume= — how long the human took to reply. n=150, median
  *181s*.

*The result, and it is sharper than expected: the second is bimodal with
an empty band between the modes.* Sorted gaps run =… 949, 971, 1061=
and then jump straight to =2070, 2103, 2135 …=. *No gap anywhere in the
corpus falls between 1061s and 2070s.* A threshold sweep sees the same
hole from the other side: span count falls steeply, then sits flat at 29
across 1200-1800s, then resumes falling. Every value inside the band
produces an identical reconstruction.

Acted on immediately: =claude-code-ide-org-guidepost-gap-threshold= 900
-> *1200s*, the round number inside the band, at the start of the
plateau with margin either side. 900 sat just below the band and split
five spans nothing justifies splitting.

*A stated assumption the data contradicted, which is the more useful
half.* The survey was proposed on the premise that silence from ~18:00
to ~08:00 is almost certainly AFK. True, but not where the long gaps
are: of 19 gaps over 2000s, *11 start inside working hours* — 07:00,
10:00, 12:00, 14:00, 15:00, 16:00, 17:00 — including the two longest
(151 and 158 minutes, starting 12:59 and 15:34). Daytime absence is
indistinguishable from nighttime absence. Gap *length* separates the
modes cleanly; clock hour does not. Recorded against
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]], whose
working-hours guess rests on exactly the premise this refutes.

*What to re-run on the scheduled date*, and the reason the number is not
yet settled: n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually
intense stretch of work on this very project. The band may be an artifact
of that. Re-derive the histogram and the sweep; if the band has moved or
closed, retune again and say so here rather than defending 1200.

*This is a moving target, not a one-off calibration* — the user's own
framing, 2026-08-13, and it changes what the re-measure is for.
Proficiency, setup and work objectives all evolve, and the habits
generating these gaps evolve with them. A faster operator, a different
mix of long-running subagent work, or a stretch of shallow triage rather
than deep implementation would each shift the modes and could close the
band entirely. So the finding above describes *August 2026*, not this
project. The scheduled task is not "confirm 1200 was right"; it is
"re-derive, and expect a different answer."

*Made a =+1m= repeater at the user's instruction, 2026-08-13*, which is
the honest encoding of "moving target": a one-shot date would have
implied the number gets settled once. It does not. Each occurrence
re-derives the histogram and the sweep against whatever the preceding
month's habits actually were, and either confirms the current value or
moves it.

Practical note for whoever handles an occurrence: marking it =DONE=
rolls the =SCHEDULED= stamp forward a month and returns the heading to
=TODO= — org's own repeater behavior — so the record of *that* month's
finding has to be written into this body before closing it, or it is
gone. Append; do not overwrite the previous month's numbers.

Charts from the original survey are an artifact, not committed here —
regenerate rather than trusting a stale rendering.

*Retarget before the September run — this is now pointed at the wrong
knob.* Since 2026-08-18 (=226ed53b= step 2(b)) the gap threshold governs
only *grouping and display*: it decides how many items a review buffer
shows and how wide each reads, and moves no recorded minute. Its
docstring says so. Re-measuring it on a month of data would answer a
question whose answer changes nothing.

What now decides recorded duration is the pair of floors:

- =claude-code-ide-org-span-idle-floor= (120s) — how much idle is
  absorbed into a run.
- =claude-code-ide-org-span-minimum-interval= — the floor on what gets
  written, named 2026-08-20 by
  [[id:e0c55d26-706c-42b6-9405-54e9d5ed4750][e0c55d26]]. It is 0 today,
  meaning the two rendering conditions alone; its docstring carries the
  sweep this review needs. *Do not cite a stored number.* 120s measured
  at 6.5% of recorded time on 2026-08-18 and at 3.03% two days later on
  a grown corpus — the estimate halved without the code changing.

Both rest on eight days of data. This heading's own body already names
that failure: /"adopting a four-day number as permanent because nobody
re-ran it."/ Same trap, one knob over.

Keep the =+1m= repeater and the parentless placement — the reasoning
recorded above still holds, and applies to the floors identically.

*Interim observation 2026-09-01, twelve days before the scheduled occurrence and
deliberately not one.* Prompted by the user asking whether the 1200s figure had
drifted. It has, and the headline is the one this body said to look for.

*The empty band has closed.* The August survey's central finding was that no gap
anywhere in the corpus fell between *1061s and 2070s* — a hole that made every
value inside it produce an identical reconstruction, which is what justified
picking 1200. Against the corpus as it stands there are *43 gaps inside that
band*.

*The corpus is now large enough that this is not sampling noise.*

| | Aug 2026 survey | 2026-09-01 |
|--------------------------+-----------------+------------|
| distinct days | 4 | *22* (2026-08-07 .. 09-01) |
| queue events | 422 | *2735* |
| =pause= -> =resume= gaps | 150 | *867* |
| =resume= -> =pause= runs | 111 | *886* |
| gap median | 181s | *232s* |

So the band was an artifact of four days, exactly as this body allowed for —
/"n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually intense stretch of work
on this very project. The band may be an artifact of that."/ It was. The
prediction and the refutation are both recorded here, which is the value of
having written the caveat down.

*What this does and does not change.* 1200s is no longer a round number inside a
hole; it is an arbitrary point on a continuum. But since =226ed53b= the gap
threshold governs only *grouping and display*, so an arbitrary value there costs
legibility rather than accuracy — which is why the retarget note above still
stands and the floors remain the live question.

*Explicitly not the scheduled run.* This is a histogram check on one statistic.
It is not the sweep: no span-count-versus-threshold curve, no re-derivation for
=span-idle-floor=, no percentage-of-recorded-time figure for the floors. The
occurrence on =<2026-09-13 Sun>= should still do all of that, and should now
expect to *move* 1200 rather than confirm it.

*A third question the September run should absorb, from the user's framing
2026-09-01.* The floors are tuned to keep the human's idle *out* of the agent's
clock, which is right for per-heading attribution and backwards for the ratio of
human time to agent time that the user is actually curious about
([[id:20cecd53-2338-4237-ab06-f8129e10a2c8][20cecd53]]). Measured on one session
that day: of ~90 minutes of plausible at-keyboard gap time, 3 minutes were
credited to the agent and 87 recorded nowhere. The same histogram answers both
questions; only the threshold's *purpose* differs. Re-deriving without asking
what the gaps say about the human would waste the pass.

*A second question for the September occurrence, added by the user 2026-09-04.*
Alongside re-deriving the threshold: **assess whether a clear class of spans
emerges that should be marked _active_ — angle-bracketed — at review time**,
meaning the interval was the human's attention rather than the agent's.

That is the automatic half of =01849bef=, which today gives the review buffer no
key for the flip and leaves it as four bracket characters edited by hand. The
distinction is not cosmetic: =b8e6007a= settled that the queue records *agent*
activity and the agenda must not absorb it, so an active timestamp is an
assertion about *whose* time an interval was, and it is the only kind the agenda
should show.

**Do not expect the class to be found by a rule.** =409a69ff= now records the
user's own account of inconsistent =AFK= discipline — popping in mid-turn to
queue a prompt, then out again without re-declaring — which makes a marker land
in the middle of an absence as often as at its edge. So the question this
occurrence answers is empirical and narrower than "what is the heuristic":
*given a month of spans, is there a subset whose evidence is unambiguous enough
to mark without asking?* A negative answer is a real result and should be
recorded as one, since it retires the search rather than leaving it open
indefinitely.

The corpus for it is already larger than the threshold work needs: the queue,
the transcripts, and the =AFK= interjections that =e75d660f= says are ground
truth and are currently discarded.

*Checked 2026-09-04, prompted by the user asking what a no-run span actually
writes — and the answer changes what "mark it active" costs.*

**A span with no runs is not a no-op.** =--review-apply-clock= writes no CLOCK
line for it, and does write a =:LOGBOOK:= annotation, gated on =(unless
observed)=. Its docstring gives the reason: "the guideposts are real evidence
that something happened, and the absent CLOCK line is the same refusal to claim
a duration nobody observed." So the evidence is kept and the duration is
deliberately not claimed. (An earlier statement in conversation that such a span
"records nothing" was wrong.)

**Which means the active-marking question is a policy reversal, not a formatting
change**, and it must answer the measurement that produced the current policy
rather than pass it by. =--review-format-annotation= says "Always *inactive*
timestamps, so nothing written from the queue reaches =org-agenda=", and it says
so because an active branch **existed and was removed**: it branched on
=:agent=, testing "is this a subagent" while claiming to test "is this a human",
and on 2026-08-14 all 68 events of one session carried =agent_id= nil — so every
span it produced was published to the agenda as though the user had been at the
keyboard. Its conclusion is the sentence this proposal contradicts: *"there is
no case in which a queue-derived span is the user's own attention, and no branch
to make."*

*The mechanism is not the obstacle.* =c084553c= established that an active
timestamp inside =:LOGBOOK:= does reach the agenda, and the docstring confirms
that remains the mechanism — "reserved for intervals a human logs themselves".
So the edit is small; the argument is the expensive part.

*The distinction that keeps =01849bef= clear of this.* A **keystroke** is a
human logging their own interval, which the current design already sanctions —
=4f8500e6= notes a human clocking in Emacs writes a bare CLOCK line with no
annotation at all. Only the **automatic** half asks the queue to produce
something the design says only a human may produce. So the manual half can ship
without reopening any of this; this occurrence decides the other half.

*What the September pass therefore has to establish*, and it is a higher bar
than "is there a pattern": evidence that distinguishes the human's attention
**without inferring it from a field that means something else**, which is
exactly how the removed branch failed. =AFK= markers, an apply timestamp inside
the gap (=996fd2bd=) and the transcript are the candidates; =409a69ff= already
records why the first of those is fuzzier than it looks.

*Correction to the note above, same day.* It said the active branch in
=--review-format-annotation= "existed and was removed", and quoted that
function's own conclusion — "there is no case in which a queue-derived span is
the user's own attention, and no branch to make".

**That quote is from a stale docstring.** The function's =let*= four lines below
it reads =(fmt (if (plist-get item :active) "<%Y-%m-%d %a %H:%M>" "[...]"))=.
The branch was *re-added* on 2026-08-24 — keyed on a human's typed bracket style
through =e=, not on =:agent=, which is the distinction the removal argument
actually turned on.

So the removal argument stands and is narrower than the docstring now implies:
what was removed is *inferring* human-ness from a queue field. What exists is a
human *asserting* it. This occurrence still has to answer the first question —
whether evidence can distinguish attention without inferring it from a field
meaning something else — but it is no longer proposing to reinstate something
the project decided against. Half of it is already built.

*Recorded as a lesson rather than only a correction:* the stale sentence misled
a reading of that function **twice in one day**, in opposite directions, and
both times the code four lines away said otherwise. Filed separately.


* TODO Concurrent sessions share one git index, and explicit staging protects nothing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 16:20]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 16:22] \\
  user is blocked on this: no second session until a mechanism exists
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-17 Mon 12:11] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:95087d8f-e16d-45d3-bdea-9b179dc672b4][Lint for headings that are valid org but structurally unintended]] becoming NEXT.
:END:
:PLAN:
Refuse the sweep at commit time, in =.githooks/pre-commit-guards=.
=GIT_INDEX_FILE= identifies the command form (measured 2026-08-12):

| Command                 | =GIT_INDEX_FILE=             | Sweeps a staged file? |
|-------------------------+------------------------------+-----------------------|
| =git commit=            | =.git/index=                 | yes                   |
| =git commit --amend=    | =.git/index=                 | yes                   |
| =git commit -a=         | =.git/index.lock=            | yes                   |
| =git commit -- <paths>= | =.git/next-index-<pid>.lock= | no                    |

Refuse when the form is a whole-index one (basename =index= or =index.lock=)
and the real index is non-empty, and only when =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= is set.
=git commit -- <paths>= always passes, =git commit -a= against an empty index
passes, and a human at a plain terminal is never affected. The message names
the staged paths and prints the pathspec form to use instead. The hook reads
both views: =git diff --cached= for what the commit will contain, and the same
with =GIT_INDEX_FILE= set to the real index for what that holds.

=--amend= reports =.git/index= like the sweep, so it escapes with
=ALLOW_SWEEP=1= (the user, 2026-09-21), shaped like =ALLOW_MERGED_BRANCH= and
=ALLOW_LOCAL_MERGE=; each use counts a =miss= through =count-miss=. There is no
ownership tracking: the command form alone discriminates.

Accepted limits: =--no-verify= bypasses it, making the mistake loud rather than
impossible; the merge, rebase and cherry-pick paths are untested; and it is
opt-in per clone through =core.hooksPath=. Test it the =bin/*-test= way: a
scratch repo, each of the four forms, asserting exit status and message.
:END:

Every session on a checkout shares one =.git/index=, so one session's
pathspec-less =git commit= sweeps up whatever another session has
staged, under a message describing unrelated work. Content survives;
attribution does not. The user will not run a second concurrent session
until a mechanism prevents it, so this gates concurrent work generally.

*Established:*
- It happened on 2026-08-12, in a deliberate resilience test: one
  session's =git commit= swept the other's 69 staged =TODO.org= lines
  into =a8dc693=; =36410e2= records that, since =a8dc693= was already
  pushed.
- The shared state is the index, not the files, so every file-level
  convention misses it, and staging early makes it worse by loading
  work into the index sooner.
- Worktrees do not help: =TODO.org= must stay one canonical copy in the
  main checkout (2e09adb7), so a worktree session still commits it
  through the shared index.
- =git commit -- <paths>= commits only those paths and leaves the rest
  staged (scratch repo, 2026-08-12), but it protects only when the
  *committing* session uses it, and it refuses an untracked file.
- As of 2026-09-01 nothing enforced it: neither the pathspec
  convention nor any =GIT_INDEX_FILE= check existed in the repo.
- 3cb3f955 is the single-session sibling: one session sweeping its own
  unrelated hunks within a file. Worth answering together.

*Ruled out:*
- Tracking which session staged which path. The command form alone
  tells the sweep apart from a pathspec commit, so no ownership state
  is needed.

*Observed 2026-09-22, and the sweep went the other way this time.* One
session composed a 27-member slice (`c7619140`) across a capture, a
property set, 27 `org_slice_add_member` calls and a `:PLAN:` amend,
holding it in the working tree while it linted. A concurrent session
committed `TODO.org` in that window and the whole slice landed inside
commit `f7e4cd2`, "7ee3b71a: the brainstormed design, and a miss the
user caught" — a message that describes none of it. The composing
session found out by running `git diff --stat` and getting nothing
back.

Two things this adds. Committing *by explicit path* is what both
sessions did, correctly, and it protected nothing: the path is the same
path, and the index is shared. And the damage is not a lost change but
an unfindable one — `git log --oneline -- TODO.org` shows four commits
in six minutes, none of whose messages mentions a slice, so the only
way back to it is `git log -S` on the id. The content survived intact
(`[0/27]`, derived `:BLOCKER:`, order as composed), which is why this
is evidence rather than an incident.


* TODO Read power assertions as a review-time attribution signal :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 15:52]
:END:

CLAUDE.md records =pmset -g log= as "not yet attempted" and frames the
per-app power assertions as noise obscuring clean sleep/wake
transitions. For *this* question the framing is backwards: a laptop that
never sleeps emits zero transitions and a rich assertion record, and the
assertion holder is the answer.

Tried 2026-08-11 against a known-answer window. The log named the
competing activity exactly:

: 14:01:56  Created   NoDisplaySleepAssertion  (zoom.us, PID 58601)
: 14:42:14  Released  NoDisplaySleepAssertion  00:40:17

The user's own estimate was "roughly 14:00 to roughly 14:45" — the log
was right to the second, and the 40m17s sits entirely inside the 54m11s
permission-block of
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]].

*Not a validated method, on one observation.* Zoom happened to hold a
cleanly-named assertion; =coreaudiod= held 158 of the 361 entries in the
same window and is a far noisier proxy. An assertion also is not
inattention — people work through calls. The most it can honestly do is
prompt the human reviewer, never decide.

*Retention bounds it:* the log went back ~7 days (earliest 2026-08-04).
That puts a deadline on "the human applies at a moment of their own
choosing."

Revises the disposition recorded in
[[id:9d2fcdad-9bf7-47b6-8018-223b13ec4577][9d2fcdad]]'s neighbourhood,
which proposed closing the =pmset= lead as won't-do — correct for the
stale-interval guess heuristic, wrong for attribution.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on
2026-08-14,* as a capability rather than a requirement. Note it is
explicitly *not* the =pmset= lead that
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] closed as
won't-do: that one guessed when a stale clock stopped, this one assigns
an existing span to a heading at review time. Same data source,
different question, and only the first was refuted.

*A ground-truth absence window, reported by the user 2026-08-25 and worth
keeping because this heading has none.* They started a =/next-session= run and
left immediately, confirming at 17:59 that they had been away since roughly
16:56 and still were. So the interval
=[2026-08-25 Tue 16:56]--[2026-08-25 Tue 17:59]+= is a *known* absence, stated
rather than inferred -- which is exactly the kind of label any proposed signal
has to be scored against, and exactly what the corpus lacks.

*Note what the session's own guideposts said about that hour, because it is the
whole problem.* The agent ran continuously and productively throughout it -- two
headings worked, a commit landed, four probe scripts ran -- so
=Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= bracketing produced one long unbroken span of genuine
*agent* work. Nothing in the queue distinguishes that hour from an hour with a
human watching every turn. Both look identical from the queue, and both are
correctly credited as agent time; what is missing is orthogonal to the clock
rather than an error in it.

*Which sharpens what a power-assertion signal would be for.* Not correcting a
clock -- the clock was right. It would answer a different question the review
pass asks constantly: whether a span had a human *available to be interrupted*,
and therefore whether the work in it has had any human judgement applied to it
at all. Everything this session queued is unreviewed in that stronger sense, and
no existing field records the difference.

*Caveat on using this datum.* It is one window, reported to the minute by
memory, with a soft start ("immediately after kicking off" -- the session's
first event was 16:56) and no end yet. Good enough to sanity-check a signal's
shape; not a benchmark.

*Tested 2026-08-25 against a known event, and the result corrects this heading's
own proposal.* The user reported that the Mac slept mid-session because the
clamshell was closed, and could not say for how long -- so there was ground
truth for the *fact* and none for the *bounds*, which is exactly the case this
heading is for.

*The signal is real, but it is not the one proposed here.* This heading asks for
*power assertions*. Those are precisely the noise =7771fc63= dismissed: =pmset
-g log= for the session window is dominated by =PID 5048(Claude) Summary
NoIdleSleepAssertion "Electron"= every fifteen minutes, =AddressBookSourceSync=,
=storagekitd=, =coreaudiod= -- hundreds of lines, no transition. What answered
the question was the =Notification= domain in the same log, two lines:

: 2026-08-25 17:06:53  Notification  Display is turned off
: 2026-08-25 17:50:00  Notification  Display is turned on

*It agrees with the queue to the second.* =clock_in= on
[[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was queued at 17:06:54,
one second after the display went off; the =resume= guidepost is at 17:50:14,
fourteen seconds after it came on. Neither number was available to the other
mechanism.

*Cross-validated against a third, independent source* rather than trusted on the
coincidence: mtimes of the probe scripts this session wrote to its scratch
directory show execution at 17:07:07, 17:07:35, 17:08:10, 17:08:55, 17:09:39,
17:09:45 -- and then nothing until 18:02. So work continued for about three
minutes after the lid closed and stopped somewhere in 17:09:45--17:50:00.

*The crucial limitation, and it is what stops this being a drop-in fix.*
=Display is turned off= means *the lid closed*, not *the machine stopped*. The
same session has a counterexample two hours later: display off 18:05:47, on
18:10:34, with continuous work throughout
([[id:f421c5c3-f81f-4ebf-99c9-42c53cc00558][f421c5c3]]'s probes wrote at
18:06:08, 18:10:09 and 18:11:33). Something held an assertion that time and the
machine kept running.

*So the two edges are not equally useful, which is the finding worth keeping:*

- *The wake edge is a reliable *resume* signal.* Display-on means someone opened
  the lid, and work can restart from there. Cheap and unambiguous.
- *The sleep edge is *not* a reliable stop signal.* It bounds when work *may*
  have stopped and must be combined with evidence of absence -- no queue events,
  no file writes -- to say that it did.

*What it would have bought today.*
[[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was credited 0:03 for
roughly seven minutes of real work. The wake edge anchors the post-wake segment
exactly; the pre-sleep segment is bounded below by its own =clock_in= and above
by the last artefact, giving 17:06:54--17:09:45. Neither bound is a guess. The
sleep *onset* stays unbounded within a forty-minute window, and should stay that
way in any record.

*Note the shape of the correction.* This is the second time the useful signal
turned out to be a different line in a log that had already been dismissed
wholesale. Worth re-reading a rejected source for the *specific* record wanted,
rather than re-litigating the rejection.


* TODO Make task lifecycle answerable from one timeline, starting at creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 10:32]
:END:

Prompted 2026-08-11 by a concrete reporting question — "how long did it
take from capture time to the PLANNING phase, for each of these tasks?"
— which is the first properly articulated demand on this project's
second stated goal, trustworthy tracking of where attention actually
went. It cannot be answered today, and the reason is not the one it
looks like.

*Measured 2026-08-11:*

: TODO.org  headings carrying :CREATED:   56
: TODO.org  "- State ..." lines            7
: DONE.org  "- State ..." lines            0

All seven come from the two hooks that write their note explicitly
(=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=, =--promote-planning-to-doing=). Not
one came from =org_set_todo=, which binds =org-inhibit-logging= to =t=
unconditionally for every state. So the transition history barely
exists, and in DONE.org — where post-hoc analysis would actually look —
it does not exist at all. That zero is *not* the archiver stripping
drawers: =org-archive-subtree= moves the whole subtree intact. Nothing
was ever written.

The example question is the worst case of this. =PLANNING= is transient
— auto-promoted to =DOING= the instant a plan is approved — and the
same census found *zero* headings sitting in it. Current state can
therefore never answer "when did this reach PLANNING"; only a
transition log can.

*So the blocker is the missing log, not =:CREATED:='s location,* and
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] already fixes it:
apply runs inside a genuinely interactive command, which is what lets
org's native logging fire instead of being suppressed. This heading is
the remaining half — making the timeline start at the right place — and
is close to worthless before the cutover, since there would be a
creation line and then silence.

*Proposal: duplicate =:CREATED:= into =:LOGBOOK:= as a creation entry.
Do not move it.* As a property it is cheaply queryable — org-ql filters
and sorts on properties directly — and drawer text is not. Moving it
trades an easy query for a parse. Duplicating buys one timeline and one
parser: analysis becomes "read the entries in order" rather than "read
a property, read a drawer, reconcile two timestamp conventions." It also
gives [[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]]'s single
chronological drawer somewhere to begin.

*org already has the shape, so nothing needs inventing.* With no
previous state org renders the field as an empty string — =org.el= line
11092, =(or org-log-note-previous-state "")= — producing a native
creation entry with no previous state.

*Corrected 2026-08-11, twice, both by direct test rather than reading.*

1. *The literal above was wrong.* The empty string is padded, not
   quoted, so the real rendering carries no ="= pair at all:

   : - State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-11 Tue 15:46]

   A parser keyed on =from ""= matches nothing. The claim that "existing
   parsers, and org-ql, handle it unmodified" was never verified and
   should be re-checked against that actual string before anything is
   built on it.

2. *=TODO= alone carried no =!= marker*, so the one transition this
   feature most needs — into =TODO=, the default keyword a capture
   receives — logged nothing, silently and without error. Interactivity
   is necessary for native logging but not sufficient; logging also has
   to be *requested*. Fixed by
   [[id:90a5a0cf-a81d-45dc-a453-0bfe61e3ef93][90a5a0cf]]. Had this
   shipped as written, it would have produced no creation entries for
   precisely the common case and looked like a bug in apply.

*The alternative, which may well be better and is not yet weighed:*
synthesise the creation entry *at read time*, in whatever reporting
function consumes this, from the =:CREATED:= property that already
exists. Nothing in any file changes, there is no duplication to diverge,
and no migration. The cost is that every consumer must know to do it,
where writing it once puts it in the record for anything that reads the
drawer — including a human. Decide this before writing anything.

*If the write-it-down option wins, two consequences:*
- *Backfill.* 56 headings have =:CREATED:= and no creation entry. A
  one-time pass can synthesise them from the property — mechanical and
  safe, with precedent in the retrospective consolidation pass recorded
  in DONE.org. Without it the analysis has a cutoff date.
- *It depends on [[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]].*
  =org_capture= does not write =:CREATED:= at all, so headings born from
  the tool have no creation time for a backfill to read. Fix that first
  or those headings have no origin in either place.

*Mechanism, if written at creation time:* it need not be special. Under
the queue model creation can simply *be* the =capture= event proposed in
[[id:7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f][7fa68d5c]], with apply
writing its =:LOGBOOK:= entry exactly as it writes a state transition.
The two headings are one feature approached from different sides —
capture durability there, timeline origin here.

*One semantic caveat for whoever writes the parser:* "created" and
"acquired its first TODO keyword" are not the same event in general.
They coincide throughout this project because every task heading is born
with a keyword, but the equivalence is a local accident, not a rule, and
a heading that gains a keyword later would make them diverge.


* TODO Fall back to the queue when org_capture cannot reach Emacs :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 07:31]
:BLOCKER:  3ad389be-2797-46c3-a51d-9f603053955a
:END:

=org_capture= requires a live Emacs. When the server is unreachable the
call fails outright and the task exists only in the conversation, which
ends. Not hypothetical: on 2026-08-10 the Emacs server was down for the
first ten minutes or so of a session (=emacsclient= reporting "can't
find socket"), and any capture attempted then would simply have been
lost.

This is the one creation path that cannot use the property the event
queue exists for — durable, cheap, no Emacs required. Everything else a
session records survives an Emacs outage; the act of writing down a new
task does not.

*Proposal:* attempt the live capture; on failure, append a =capture=
event to the queue, and let apply create the heading.

*Three things this raises, none of them settled:*
- *It is the queue's first outbox use.* Every existing kind is a
  proposal about a heading that already exists; a =capture= event would
  bring one into being. That is a genuinely different contract, and the
  review buffer would need to render it as such.
- *The :ID: must be minted by the session, not at apply time.* An ID
  assigned on application is useless to the session that queued it — it
  could not link to the heading, block on it, or clock against it, which
  is exactly what =claude-code-ide-org-capture= returns its ID for
  today. Minting without Emacs is fine (=org-id-new= is a UUID), but it
  means an ID can be referenced before the heading it names exists.
- *Reconciliation.* If the live capture succeeded and only its reply was
  lost, applying the queued event would duplicate the heading. A
  session-minted ID gives apply a cheap idempotency key: if that :ID:
  already resolves, the event is already realised.

*The larger caveat, which bears on this and on
[[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]] both.* This tool creates a
bare =* TODO <title>= at top level in a single target file. It cannot place a
heading under a named parent, nor carry tags, a body, or any property beyond the
=:ID:= it generates. Consequently almost every heading in this project is
written by direct Elisp instead — four were created that way on 2026-08-10
alone, none through the tool. So instrumenting or repairing =org_capture=
improves a path that is rarely taken, and the prior question is whether the tool
should become capable enough to be worth using. Decide that first; it may
reorder or absorb both headings.

*Considered and set aside:* queueing a =capture= event on *every*
capture, as an audit record carrying =session_id=/=agent_id= — which is
the only place session attribution exists, and would answer "which
session created this heading?" structurally rather than from prose in
the body. Rejected for now on the caveat above: it would record almost
nothing real. Revisit if the tool becomes the normal creation path.


*Also the timeline origin:* recording creation as a =:LOGBOOK:= entry
([[id:d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5][d9132c57]]) can ride this same event
rather than needing a mechanism of its own — apply would write the creation
entry exactly as it writes a state transition.

*Builds on 3ad389be's =PostToolUseFailure= hook* (2026-09-23): the
fallback becomes a branch of that hook, whose payload carries the whole
=tool_input=. It fires on a refused connection only, because a timed-out
call keeps running and a queued copy of a capture that later lands would
create the heading twice.

:END:

- [2026-08-21 Fri 10:10]--[2026-08-21 Fri 10:13] assess the daily-recurrence proposal
